But in the last of these skirmishes Osberne was hurt sorely, and though he was brought off by his fellows, and lost not Boardcleaver, as well-nigh betid, he must needs keep his bed somewhat more than a month ere he was well healed.
But on a day in September, when he was much amended and was growing strong again, came to him Stephen, whom he had not seen for some days, and seeing that there was no man in the chamber save they two, spake to him and said: “Captain, I would have a word with thee if I might.”
Said Osberne: “Speech is free to thee, Stephen.” And the Eater said: “I have been out a-gates of late, for I deemed that if I might find adventures it would be for thy health.” Said Osberne, laughing: “Yet maybe not for thine, Stephen. I were loth to come to Wethermel without thee.” Said Stephen: “At this rate it may be long ere we come to Wethermel.” “I would we might hasten the homecoming,” said Osberne, knitting his brows, “but I wot not how that may be since the Baron is yet so strong.” “Ah, but I have a deeming how it may be done,” said Stephen: “but there is peril in it.” Osberne stood up and said: “What hast thou been about, runagate?”

===Chinese===

此后男爵不再攻城，却在城外掘壕环围，坐困起来；他乡间的粮秣源源接济，部众一无所缺。只是那道围壕、连同他命人筑在壕上的土木望楼，因绵延太长，守备难得处处周密；东集人便不给它们片刻安生，屡屡猛力突袭，杀伤了男爵许多人马，教他大受亏损。城中人人意气高昂，彼此相告：只要事态不比这更坏，他们尽可欢欢喜喜支撑下去，直等到冬天来拆散这场围困。然而在末了一回突袭里，奥斯伯恩伤得极重；他虽被同伴们救了回来，断板剑也险些儿失落——终究不曾失落——却只得卧床将养了一月有余，方才痊愈。
九月里的一天，他已大有起色、渐渐恢复气力，斯蒂芬前来看他——两人已有几日不见；斯蒂芬见屋里除了他们俩再无旁人，便开口道：“统领，我有句话想跟你说，不知可否。”
奥斯伯恩道：“你只管说便是，斯蒂芬。”饕客道：“近来我常出城去，因为我想，若能寻着些奇遇，于你的病体必大有益处。”奥斯伯恩笑道：“只怕于你自己的身子无益呢，斯蒂芬。要我回韦瑟梅尔时身边少了你，我可不甘心。”斯蒂芬道：“照这么下去，我们回到韦瑟梅尔的日子只怕还远着呢。”“但愿能催一催这归期才好，”奥斯伯恩蹙起眉头道，“只是男爵势头尚强，我不知道这如何办得成。”“啊，我却有个主意，知道这事怎生办得到，”斯蒂芬道，“只是其中带着凶险。”奥斯伯恩站起身来，说道：“你这浪荡子，又在外面折腾了些什么？”

===Original===

“Master,” said he, “I will tell thee. Five nights ago I did on raiment of the fashion of them beyond Deepdale, and I had with me a fiddle, and was in the manner of a minstrel, and thou wottest that I am not so evil a gut-scraper, and that I have many tales and old rhymes to hand, though I am no scald as thou art. Well, I got out a-gates a night-tide by the postern on the nook of the southeast tower, the warden whereof is a friend of mine own, and then by night and cloud I contrived it to skirt the dyke and get me about till I came northwest of our north gate, and then somehow I got up over the dyke, which is low there and was not guarded as then, and in a nook I lay still till morning came. And there I let myself be found by one of the warders, and when he kicked me and challenged me, I told him what I would as to myself, and he trowed it, and he brought me to his fellows, who, a five of them, were cooking their breakfast, and they gave me victual and bade me play and sing for their disport, and I did so, and pleased them. Thereafter one of them took me along with him toward the west side of the dyke, and I played and sang; and so, to make a long story short, I worked round the dyke that day till I was come to the south side of the leaguer, and there I lay that night in good entertainment; but on the morrow I went on my way, and before evening I had come back again to the northwest, just where I had started from. There I fell in with the man-at-arms who had kicked me up the morning before, and he fell to speech with me, and showed me many things, and amongst the others the great bastide wherein, said he, the Baron of Deepdale was lodged, and that it was little guarded, which mattered nothing by day, but by night he deemed it something rash of the Baron to suffer so few men of his anigh him.

===Chinese===

“主人，”他说道，“我便告诉你。五夜之前，我穿上一身深谷那边人的装束，随身带一把提琴，扮作个卖唱的乐师；你也知道，我这拉琴的本事不算太坏，肚里攒着的故事和古歌谣也着实不少，虽然我不像你这般做得吟游诗人。于是我趁一个夜头，从东南角楼拐角处那道暗门溜出城去——守那门的是我自己交下的朋友——随后借着夜色阴云，贴着围壕边儿绕行，兜兜转转，来到我们北门的西北面；再往后不知怎的，我翻上了围壕——那一段壕身低矮，当时又无人把守——便寻个角落一动不动，直躺到天明。在那里，我有意教一个哨兵撞见了；他连踢带喝地盘问我，我便把关于自己的一套说辞讲给他听，他信了，把我带到他的同伴们那里；那五个人正在做早饭，他们给我吃食，叫我弹唱一曲替他们取乐，我便弹唱了，很讨他们欢喜。随后其中一人带着我朝围壕西面去，我一路弹唱；长话短说，那一天我绕着围壕兜了一整圈，直到来到围城大营的南面，那一夜便在那里受了很好的款待；次日我继续赶路，天黑之前又兜回西北面，恰是我起脚的地方。在那里，我遇上了前一天清早把我踢起来的那个兵，他同我攀谈起来，指点我看了许多东西；其中便有那座大堡楼——据他说，深谷男爵就驻在里头，而且守卫寥寥：这白天倒无关紧要，可到了夜里，男爵容许身边只有这么几个人，他觉得未免有些莽撞了。

===Original===

“Now while we spake together thus there was a stir about us, and we and others rose up from the grass where we were lying, and lo it was the Baron who was come amongst us, so we all did him reverence. He was a dark man, rather little than big, but wiry and hard-bitten; keen and eager of face, yet was there something lordly about his bearing. As luck would have it he came straight to where we stood together, and stayed to look upon me as something unwonted to him, for I was wholly unarmed, save for a little knife in my girdle; and I was clad in a black gown and a cotehardy of green sprigged with tinsel, and had my fiddle and bow at my back. We louted low before him, and he spake to my friend: ‘Is this big fellow a minstrel?’ ‘Yea, lord,’ said the other. Said the Baron: ‘Looking at his inches, ’tis a pity of him that he hath not jack and sallet and a spear over his shoulder. How sayest thou, carle; what if I were to set thee in the forefront of the press amongst the very knighthood?’ ‘Noble lord,’ quoth I, ‘I fear me that if I came within push of spear thou wouldst presently see me running, so long are my legs. I am a big man, so please you great lord, but I have the heart of a hare in me.’ He looked upon me somewhat grimly, then he said: ‘Meseems thou hast a fox’s tongue in thee, carle, and I promise thee I have half a mind to it to hand the over to the provost-marshal’s folk, to see what they could make of whipping thee. Thou man-at-arms, hast thou heard him lay his bow over the strings?’ ‘Yea, lord,’ said the man; ‘he playeth not ill for an uplander.’ ‘Let him try it now before us, and do it well withal if he would save the skin of his back.’ Speedily I had my fiddle in my hand, and fell to, and if I played not my best, I played at least something better than my worst. And when I had done, the Baron said: ‘Friend, how many such tunes canst thou play? and canst thou sing aught?’ ‘It would not be so easy to tell up the tunes I can play, lord,’ said I; ‘and sing I can withal, after a fashion.’ Said the Baron to the man-at-arms: ‘Bring thou this man to my lodging tonight some two hours before midnight, and he shall play and sing to us, and if we be not sleep-eager he shall tell us some old tale also; and I will reward him. And thou, I shall not make thee a man-at-arms this time, though trust me, I misdoubt thy hare-heart. There is no such look in thine eyes.’ And he turned away and left us. So we wore the night merrily enough till the time appointed, what with minstrelsy and some deal of good wine.

===Chinese===

“我们正这般说着，四下里起了一阵骚动，我们和旁人纷纷从躺卧的草地上站起身来；一看，原来是男爵驾临到我们中间，于是众人无不向他致敬。他是个肤色黝黑的人，个子偏小，算不得高大，却精悍结实、饱经磨砺；面容锐利热切，只是举手投足之间自有一股贵气。也是合该有事，他径直走到我们一处站着的地方，停步打量起我来，只当我是个希罕人物：因为我通身不带兵器，腰间只有一把小刀；身上穿一件黑袍，外罩一件缀着闪线的绿紧身袍，背上背着提琴和琴弓。我们在他面前深深躬身行礼，他向我的朋友开口道：‘这个大个子是个乐师么？’‘是，老爷，’那一位答道。男爵道：‘瞧他这副身量，竟不穿甲衣、不戴铁盔、肩上不扛一杆长枪，真是可惜了这块材料。你说呢，汉子？倘若我把你安插到战阵最前沿，教你同正经的骑士们站在一处，你意下如何？’‘尊贵的老爷，’我答道，‘我怕的是，一等挨着枪尖儿，大人您转眼便要瞧见我撒腿飞奔了——我这两条腿实在太长。我是个大个子，敬禀老爷，可我这腔子里装的是一颗兔子心。’他略带狠色地瞅了我一眼，随后说道：‘我看你这汉子肚里长着一条狐狸舌头；我告诉你，我简直有半个心思，要把你交到军法官手下，瞧瞧一顿鞭子能从你身上抽出些什么名堂。当兵的，你可曾听他拉弓上弦？’‘听过，老爷，’那兵答道；‘照一个山野之人而论，他拉得不坏。’‘那就叫他这就当着我们的面试上一试，好好儿地拉；他若要保全背上的皮肉，就拿出真本事来。’我赶忙把琴抄在手里，拉将起来；虽说算不得我最佳的功夫，至少也比我最蹩脚的强着一筹。一曲奏罢，男爵说道：‘朋友，这样的曲子你能来多少？你可还会唱些什么？’‘我能拉的曲子，老爷，怕不好一一数清，’我说；‘唱歌嘛，我也还凑合能来几段。’男爵对那兵道：‘今夜午夜之前约莫两个钟头，你把这人带到我下处来，教他给我们弹唱一番；倘若我们还不困倦，再教他讲个古时的故事；我自有赏赐。至于你嘛——这一回我且不收你当兵；不过不瞒你说，你那颗兔子心我却信不过，你眼睛里没有那副神色。’说罢他转身去了。于是我们弹琴的弹琴、喝酒的喝酒，靠着乐声和几分好酒，把那一夜过得好不快活，直挨到约定的时辰。

===Original===

“To the Baron’s lodgings I went, which was not right great, but hung goodly with arras of Troy. And I had the luck to please the lord; for I both played and sang somewhat near my best. And he bade give me a handful of silver pennies, though I must needs share them with my soldier friend, unto whom the lord forgat to give aught, and bade me come the next night at the same time, which I did, after I had spent the day looking into everything about that side of the leaguer. But when I came forth with my friend from the lord’s lodging that second night (and I the richer therefor), I did him to wit that the next morning early I should take my soles out of the leaguer and make for my own country, whatever might happen, so that no so many questions might be asked if I were missed on the morrow, as belike I was. Well the end of this long story is, that a little before midnight I crept away and over the dyke and came to the postern and my friend, who let me into the town, and here I am safe and sound. Now, Captain, canst thou tell me why I took so much trouble in my disport, with no little peril withal?”

===Chinese===

“男爵的下处我去了；那屋子算不得十分宏大，却挂满了漂亮的特洛伊挂毯，满室生辉。我运气不坏，很讨那位爵爷的欢喜，因为我的弹唱都使出了近乎生平最好的功夫。他吩咐赏我一把银便士；虽说那位爵爷忘了赏我的兵友、他什么也没落着，这笔钱我少不得要分他一份。男爵又叫我第二天夜里同一时辰再去，我去了；那一整个白天，我便用来把围营那一带的情形看了个遍。可是第二天夜里，我同朋友从爵爷下处出来的时候（这一趟我又发了些小财），我便让他知道：明天一早，不论出什么岔子，我也要迈开脚溜出这围营，赶回自己的家乡去——如此一来，明日我若被人察觉不见了，也不至于被盘问出这许多话来；而我十有八九是会被察觉的。好了，这段长故事的结局是：午夜之前不多一会儿，我悄悄爬将出来，越过围壕，摸到暗门和我那朋友跟前，他放我进了城，如今我便好端端地站在这里了。那么，统领，你可说得出么——我为什么这般不辞劳苦地寻我的乐趣，还搭上不小的凶险？”

===Original===

Now for some time Osberne had been walking to and fro as he hearkened to the tale, and now he turned about sharply to Stephen and said: “Yea, I know; thou wilt mean it in a day or two that we should go, we two, by night and cloud to the Great Bastide and carry off the Baron of Deepdale, that we may give him guesting in the good town.”
Stephen smote his palms together and said: “Wise art thou, child of Wethermel; but not so wise as I be. We shall go, we two, but not alone, but have with us four stout fellows, and of wisdom enough, not Dalesmen, for too simple are they and lack guile. To say sooth I have chosen them already, and told them how we fare, and they are all agog for it.”
“Well,” said Osberne, “and when shall it be? Of a sooth thou lettest no grass grow under thy feet. But hast thou told anyone else?” Said Stephen: “Tomorrow night is the time appointed, and I have bidden my friend the warder of the postern to hold ready a score of men well-armed against the hour we are to be looked for to knock at the door with our guest, if so be that we should need them, but I have not told him what we are about. Well now, what sayest thou? Have I done anything to amend thine health?” “Thou hast made me whole and well, friend,” said Osberne; “and now I think we shall soon look upon Wethermel, and I shall never be sick or sorry again.”
The Eater smiled, and they fell to talking of other matters as folk came into the chamber to them; and all that came in wondered to see the captain looking so much mended in health.

===Chinese===

原来奥斯伯恩听着这段故事的时候，早已来回踱了好一阵子；这时他霍地转过身来，对着斯蒂芬说道：“是了，我知道了；你的意思是，这一两天之内，你我两人趁着黑夜阴云，摸到大堡楼去，把深谷男爵活擒了来，好教我们在好城里款待他这位客人。”
斯蒂芬拍了一下巴掌，说道：“你真聪明，韦瑟梅尔的孩子；可到底还不及我聪明。我们去是要去的，我们俩去，却又不单是我们俩：还要带上四个剽悍的汉子，也都是心机足够用的——不是谷民，谷民们太实心眼，缺些狡狯。说实在的，人我早已挑选停当，此行的情况也告诉了他们，他们个个摩拳擦掌，巴不得立时便去。”
“好啊，”奥斯伯恩道，“那么定在哪一天？说真的，你脚底下真是一刻也不肯让草长出来。可你还告诉过旁人么？”斯蒂芬道：“明晚便是约定的时辰；我已嘱咐我那守暗门的朋友，教他备下二十名衣甲齐全的汉子，候在我们该领着客人去敲门的那个时辰上听用——万一我们果真用得着他们的话；只是我们要干的是什么，我并不曾告诉他。好了，如今你说罢？我这一番作为，可算治好了你的病？”“你把我治得又齐全又爽利了，朋友，”奥斯伯恩道，“如今我想，我们不久便要望见韦瑟梅尔了，而且我从此再不会生病，再不会愁苦了。”
饕客莞尔一笑；随着人们陆续进屋来，他们便谈起别的事情；凡是进来的人，见统领气色已痊愈到这般地步，个个都啧啧称奇。

## XXVI / 二十六

===Original===

They Bring the Baron Into Eastcheaping

So on the morrow just before midnight came Osberne and Stephen and the four others to the postern abovesaid. Osberne and the four were clad, over their armour, in frocks and hoods of upcountry fashion; but Stephen was in his minstrel’s raiment, save that he bore no fiddle, and had a heavy short-sword girt to him under his cotehardy. The night was moonless, but there was little cloud, so that there was a glimmer of starlight. As they opened the door, came forth from the ingle a tall man, unarmed as it seemed, and clad as a gangrel carle, and Stephen without more ado stretched out his long arm and caught him by the breast of his coat. The man stirred not nor strove, but said softly: “Dost thou not know me, Stephen the Eater? I come to see the child of Wethermel; he shall know me by the token of the Imposition of Hands. And I am come to help him and all you.” That heard Osberne and spake softly to the others: “This is a friend and a stout-heart; he shall be of all avail to us.”

“Speak not,” said Stephen, “but hold we on, and go crouching till we be under the lee of the dyke.” Even so did they, and Stephen led the way, but Osberne came next and Steelhead with him; they spake not together, but Osberne felt the stronger for having him beside him, and his heart was full of joy.

So they clomb the dyke, and as they topped it they saw a weaponed man on his feet betwixt them and the sky. Stephen stood up straightway and fell a-whistling a merry tune, but softly enough, while he made a sign to the others to fetch a compass and go creeping past this man. So they did, while Stephen and the warder walked toward one another; but so soon as they met, the warder knew his friend, and hailed him and said: “Well, minstrel, thou art back again pretty soon; what is toward, man?” Said Stephen: “Sooth to say, I went not all the way home; for it came into my mind that maybe the Baron might call for me again; and when it rains florins I am fain to have my hat under the spout.” Said the warder: “Thou art come in time, for the Baron is somewhat ailing, and whiles he sleeps not well a-nights; it was but last night when it was so, and he sends for me and asks me of thee, and biddeth me fetch thee; and St. Peter! the uproar when I told him that thou wert gone; and it was hardly that I escaped a whipcord supper. Howsoever, his wrath ran off him in a little, and then he bade me look out for thee, and if I find thee I am to bring thee to him at any hour of day or night wherein the armour is off him: wherefore, see thou, in happy hour art thou come. So abide me till I go and fetch a fellow to keep my watch, and then will I go on with thee to my lord.”

===Chinese===

他们把男爵带进东集

于是次日，就在午夜之前，奥斯伯恩、斯蒂芬和另外四人来到了前文所说的那道暗门。奥斯伯恩和那四人都在甲胄外面罩了内地式样的布袍兜帽；斯蒂芬却穿一身乐师衣裳，只是没带提琴，紧身袍底下佩着一柄沉重的短剑。那夜没有月亮，云也不多，天上便透着一缕星光的微芒。他们开门时，炉边走出一条高大的汉子，看去手无寸铁，穿得像个流浪汉子；斯蒂芬二话不说，伸出长臂，一把揪住他的衣襟。那人不动也不挣扎，只柔声说道：“你认不得我了么，饕客斯蒂芬？我是来看韦瑟梅尔的孩子的；他见了按手之礼的记号，自会认得我。我是来助他、也助你们大家的。”奥斯伯恩听见了，悄声对众人道：“这是一位朋友、一条好汉；他于我们必有大用。”

“莫作声，”斯蒂芬道，“只管往前，弓着身子走，到围壕掩避之下再说。”他们果然照办；斯蒂芬领路在前，奥斯伯恩随后，钢首与他同行；两人彼此不交一言，但奥斯伯恩觉得有他在身旁，胆气便壮了几分，心头满是欢喜。

于是他们攀上围壕，将到壕顶时，只见一个带甲的汉子直挺挺立在他们与天空之间。斯蒂芬当即直起身来，吹起一支欢快的调子，却吹得极轻，一面打手势叫众人绕个弯子，蹑手蹑脚从那汉子身旁爬过去。众人依计而行，斯蒂芬便迎着那哨兵走去；两人一碰面，哨兵就认出了他的朋友，招呼道：“好啊，乐师，你回来得好快；有什么动静么，老兄？”斯蒂芬道：“实不相瞒，我压根没走回家去；因为我转念想着，男爵爷说不定还要传我；天上下弗罗林的时候，我巴不得把帽子伸到檐槽底下接着呢。”哨兵道：“那你来得正是时候，男爵爷身上略略欠安，夜里时或睡不安稳；昨晚正是这样，他传了我去，问起你来，吩咐我把你找去；圣彼得！我一说你走了，他发起的那阵脾气哟；我好容易才逃过一顿鞭绳晚餐。不过他的火气一会儿就消了，随后便吩咐我留心找你，一旦找着，不论白天黑夜，只消他卸了甲，就得带你去见他：所以说啊，你来得正巧。且等我找个伙伴替我守这一班，我便同你一道去见爵爷。”


===Original===

“Wait a while,” said Stephen; “to say sooth I have hereby an old carle, my uncle, and his son, a young swain, and both they are good at song, and the older man a very poke stuffed full of old tales: how were it if I brought them along?”

“It were good,” said the warder, “for it shall, see thou, make a change of disport for our lord, and that will please him the more. So go now, bring up hither thy kinsmen, and I will see to my watch and we will meet here straightway.”

So then Stephen went to his folk, who were creeping nigher and nigher the Great Bastide, and were as now in broken ground somewhat bushed, a good lurking-place to wit. There he finds them, and bids the four abide their coming back with their prey, which now he nowise doubted of, and takes Steelhead and Osberne along with him, and brings them to the warder; who laughed when he saw Steelhead, for he went for that time all bent and bowed, and, as he deemed by what he could see under the dim sky, ragged and wretched. Said he: “Minstrel, thou wert scarce in luck to happen on this rag of a kinsman of thine. Hast thou no better, man?” Said Stephen, grinning in the dark: “Abide till ye have proved him. Trust me, he hath something better than sour curds in his belly.” “Well,” said the warder, “let-a-be! As for the young man, he seems like enough. Now then, fellow, for a pull at the florin-tree.”

So they went, the four of them, toward the Great Bastide, and none hindered them, deeming that they were of the service of the Baron. Even at the door of the Baron’s lodging the warder (there was but one and a chamberlain) nodded friendly to the soldier and let them pass unquestioned. They entered the chamber, wherein now was no man, as the Baron would have it whenas he listed to sleep. The soldier went forward on tiptoe, but Stephen trod heavily, and Steelhead laughed aloud, and went straight up to the great man’s bedhead, and fared to pass his hand over his face from his forehead to his chin, just touching him, but the sleeping man waked not. As for Osberne, he stood betwixt the door and the soldier, and drew his sword forth from under his carter’s frock, but it was not Boardcleaver, for he had left him at home. The soldier looked from one to another, and stared astonished at their demeanour. Straightway then he had both Stephen and Osberne on him at once: nor had he any senses nor might to strive with them, who stripped his coat off over his head, gagged him, and tied him hand and foot. By then they had done this, Steelhead had taken up the naked Baron and set some of the warder’s raiment on him, and done on him the said warder’s coat and sallet over all; and there stood the man of worship, waked up now, as it seemed, but looked before him as if he saw naught, even as a man who walks in sleep. Stephen the meantime unstrung his fiddle and began to play a slow sweet tune thereon, and let his big but melodious voice go with it, and thus they brought the lordship of Deepdale to the door, and still he seemed of no avail, save to walk on as Steelhead would have him.

===Chinese===

“且慢，”斯蒂芬道，“实对你说，我手边带着一个老家伙，是我叔父，还有他儿子，一个年轻后生，两人都擅歌唱，那老的更是一只塞满老故事的口袋：我把他们一并带来如何？”

“那敢情好，”哨兵道，“你要知道，这正好给爵爷换换消遣的花样，他老人家必定越发高兴。那么去罢，把你家亲眷带上来，我且去照看我的班务，我们即刻在此碰头。”

于是斯蒂芬回到自己人那里——他们正朝大堡楼越爬越近，此刻已到一片坑洼多灌丛的地面，正是个藏身的好去处。他在那里寻着众人，吩咐那四人原地等候他们带着猎物回来——这会儿他已全不疑心猎物会到手——便带着钢首和奥斯伯恩折回来，领到那哨兵跟前。哨兵一见钢首便笑了，因为他这时一路佝偻着身子，而且在昏暗天光下看去，一身褴褛，形容狼狈。他说：“乐师，你撞着这么一块破布片似的亲眷，运气可真不怎么样。你就没有体面些的么，老兄？”斯蒂芬在黑地里咧嘴一笑：“且等你领教过他再说。信我一句，他肚子里装的可比酸凝乳强些。”“也罢，”哨兵道，“由他去！至于那年轻的，瞧着倒还像样。好了，伙计，该去那棵弗罗林树上摇一把了。”

于是他们四人朝大堡楼走去，无人拦阻，都当他们是男爵手下当差的。到了男爵下处的门口，那守门人——门口统共一名守卫，外加一名内侍——朝他那位当兵的朋友亲热地点了点头，未加盘问便放他们过去。他们进了卧房，房中此刻空无一人，这是男爵想要安睡时的规矩。那兵士踮着脚上前，斯蒂芬却脚步沉沉，钢首更是朗声大笑，径直走到那位大人物的床头，伸手从他的额头到下巴抚过他的脸，只轻轻沾着，那睡着的人并不醒来。奥斯伯恩则立在门口与兵士之间，从赶车人的粗袍底下抽出剑来——却不是断板剑，他已把它留在家里了。那兵士看看这个、望望那个，瞧他们这般行径，惊得直发怔。那兵士还来不及多想，斯蒂芬和奥斯伯恩已双双扑到他身上：他哪里还有心思气力挣扎，两人把他上衣从他头上剥下，堵了嘴，捆了手脚。待他们收拾停当，钢首已抱起赤条的男爵，给他套上几件哨兵的衣裳，末了又把那哨兵的外衣和铁盔给他一并罩上。那贵人便直挺挺立在那里，似乎已经醒转，却直望着前面，仿佛什么也看不见，恰似一个梦游的人。斯蒂芬这会儿取下提琴，奏起一支舒缓甜美的曲子，放开他那洪亮而悦耳的嗓门随和着；他们就这样把深谷的爵爷送到了门口，他仍旧全然无用，只顺着钢首的心意一步步走下去。


===Original===

So out they fared, and none hindered them any more than when they went in; and they came to the bushed ground where lay the four townsmen and stirred them, and so went on all seven with their new fellow the Baron, who still walked on like a man in his sleep.

They made a compass about the warder who had taken the place of Stephen’s friend, so that he might not challenge them, and came fair and softly to the dyke, and thereafter to the postern. There Stephen knocked after the manner appointed, and the door opened and showed the passage all full of armed men. But Stephen cried out: “All’s well, friend Dickon, and there shall be no sally out tonight, only take us in, and bring me and Captain Osberne to Sir Medard, for we have somewhat to show him.”

So they gat them into the town, they and their new guest; but ere the door was shut, Steelhead took Osberne by the skirt and drew him a little aside and said: “Lad of Wethermel, in all ways thou hast shown thy valiance, and I am glad of thee. Now I have come from the hillsides and the crannies of the rocks to look upon thee, and I must get me back at once; for within a builded town I may not be. But I can see that it will not be long till we meet in the mountains. So I tell thee, when thou deemest thy need and thy grief to be as great as it may be, hie thee to the little dale where first we met, and call on me by the token of the bow I gave thee then, and presently thou shalt have tidings: now farewell.”

“Yea, but hold,” said Osberne, “wilt thou not enter, even if it be to go forth at once by another gate with much company? Else wilt thou be tangled amongst all these foemen.”

“Trouble not thyself about me,” said Steelhead; “it shall not be hard for me to go where I will in despite of any foeman.”

===Chinese===

于是他们出门而去，一路无人阻拦，同进来时一般无二；他们走到那片多灌丛的地面，四个城里人正伏在那里，便唤醒了他们；于是他们七个便带着新同伴男爵一道上路，那男爵仍旧像个梦游人似的走着。

他们绕了个大弯，避开那个顶了斯蒂芬朋友班次的哨兵，免得他查问，然后稳稳悄悄地来到围壕，随后到了暗门。斯蒂芬照约定的方式叩门，门开了，过道里站满了带甲的兵士。斯蒂芬高声道：“平安无事，迪康朋友，今夜不会出城厮杀，只消放我们进去，带我和奥斯伯恩统领去见梅达德爵士，我们有东西要给他看。”

于是他们进了城，连同他们这位新客；但在门还未关上时，钢首拉住奥斯伯恩的衣角，把他扯到一旁，说道：“韦瑟梅尔的孩子，你在方方面面都显过了你的勇烈，我为你欢喜。如今我从山坡上、从岩缝石隙里出来看了你一趟，这就得立刻回去；因为一座筑成的城池里面，是我待不得的。但我看得出，不久我们便要在群山之中相会。我告诉你，当你觉得你的困厄与悲伤到了无以复加的地步时，你就赶到我们初逢的那条小谷去，凭我当年赠你的那张弓为记呼唤我，你顷刻便会有音信：就此别过了。”

“唉，且慢，”奥斯伯恩道，“你竟不进来么？哪怕立刻就带着大队人马从另一座城门出去也好啊？不然你就要陷在这些仇敌中间了。”

“不必为我操心，”钢首道，“任凭什么仇敌，也拦不住我随心所欲地来去。”

## XXVII / 二十七

===Original===

They Parley from the Walls

Therewith he was gone and Osberne entered the town after his fellows, and the Baron of Deepdale was brought to Sir Medard in the great tower. There they would have served him with all honour, but he was not yet come out of that trance; so they laid him to rest in Sir Medard’s own bed, and had warders both within the chamber and without; and Osberne sat talking with Sir Medard in the said chamber till dawn was, when the Baron awoke really and fairly, and called for drink. And Sir Medard brought it unto him with his own hand, and the Baron stared at him and said: “Art thou of the service tonight? I know thee not.” Quoth Sir Medard: “And yet we have been near enough together ere this, Lord Baron; thou shouldst know me, meseems.” The Baron looked hard on him and then round about the chamber, and cried out: “Holy Mary! ’tis Medard the carle-leader. Where am I, and where is the evil beast of a minstrel? Hath he beguiled me?” Said Medard: “Lord, at this present thou art in a chamber of my poor house in Eastcheaping. Doubtless tomorrow, after we have had some talk together, thou and I and the Porte, thou mayst go back home to Deepdale, or abide here to see how we can feast, we carle-warriors, and to be holden in all honour.”

Now came forth Stephen the Eater and said: “Lord, lo here the evil beast of a minstrel who hath verily beguiled thee; but, Baron, it is to thy gain and not thy loss. For tomorrow shall the war be ended, and thou shalt be free to go back again to the fair women of Deepdale whom thou lovest so much, and shalt save thy men-at-arms, and thy weapons and tents and timber, and victuals and drink a great heap; and all this I deem, and more maybe, wouldst thou have lost hadst thou gone on sitting perversely before Eastcheaping all for nought. So I will not say pardon me, but make friends with me rather for being good to me.” And therewith he reached out his great hand to the Baron; but Osberne drew him back by the girdle, and chid him for mocking a captive, while the Baron turned his face to the wall and covered up his head with the bedclothes.

But ye may judge if there were riding and running in the leaguer next morning when they could find the Baron nowhere; and one said this and the other that; and he cried Kill and slay, and he cried Flee ere we all come to like end; and great was the doubt and the turmoil. Amidst of which comes Sir Medard on to the battlement of the northwest tower, and beside him a squire bearing a white banner, and a herald with a trumpet, which herald presently blew a loud blast, but such an one as sounded not of war but of parley. So when the captains and leaders heard the said blast and saw the white banner of peace, they deemed that new tidings were toward, and a half score of them crossed over their dyke bearing a white banner with them, and came close under the tower whereon stood Sir Medard; and the chiefest of them, an old hoar man and very wise, hight Sir Degore, stood before the others all unhelmed and said: “Is it Sir Medard that standeth up there?”

“Yea verily,” said the Knight; “and what art thou? Art thou a leader of the host that sitteth about us?” Said the other: “I am Sir Degore, of whom thou wilt have heard; under my lord the Baron of Deepdale I am the leader of this host, and I have come to ask what thou wouldst of us.” Said Sir Medard: “I would see the Baron of Deepdale.”

===Chinese===

城头会谈

说罢他便去了；奥斯伯恩随着同伴们进了城，深谷男爵则被带到大塔里梅达德爵士面前。众人本要按全副礼数侍奉他，他却尚未从那昏迷中醒转；于是众人便让他躺上梅达德爵士自己的床，房里房外都派了看守；奥斯伯恩就在那间屋里陪着梅达德爵士说话，直到天亮时分，男爵才算真真切切地醒了过来，开口讨酒水。梅达德爵士亲手端给他，男爵瞪眼打量着他，说道：“今晚是你当值么？我不认得你。”梅达德爵士道：“可是，男爵爵爷，咱们先前彼此离得够近的了；照我想，你该认得我才是。”男爵死死盯住他，又环顾屋内四壁，失声叫道：“圣母玛利亚！这不是汉子头子梅达德么。我这是在哪儿？那恶兽乐师又上哪儿去了？他可是诓了我？”梅达德道：“爵爷，眼下你是在东集我寒舍的一间屋子里。明日你我同市府好生谈过一番之后，你无疑便可以回深谷家里去，或者留在此地，瞧瞧我们这些汉子战士怎样摆宴欢庆，并受全般的礼敬。”

这时饕客斯蒂芬走上前来，说道：“爵爷，瞧啊，那恶兽乐师正在这儿，他当真诓了你；只是，男爵，这于你有利，而非有损。因为明日战事便告终结，你便可以脱身回去，回到你那般疼爱的深谷美人儿们身边，又保全了你的兵士、你的兵器帐篷木料，还有好大一堆的粮秣酒水；这些，依我看，兴许还不止这些——倘若你执意顽抗、白白在东集城外干耗下去，可就都要折损了。所以我不说‘请你饶我’，倒要说：我待你这么好，你该同我交个朋友才是。”说着他向男爵伸出那只大手；奥斯伯恩却一把扯住他的腰带拉了回来，责备他不该嘲弄被俘之人；男爵则把脸转向墙壁，拿被褥把头蒙了起来。

可想而知，第二天早晨营里四下寻不着男爵时，那里头是何等的驰逐奔忙；这人这么说，那人那么说；有人喊“杀呀，斩呀”，有人喊“快逃罢，免得咱们都落得一样的下场”；一时疑惧纷纭，乱作一团。正乱着，梅达德爵士登上西北塔的城堞，身旁一名扈从执着白旗，一名传令官带着号角；那传令官当即吹响一声高亢的长号，却不是杀伐之音，而是邀谈之调。众统领头目听得这号声，又望见那面和平的白旗，料想必有新的消息，便有十名统领越过壕沟，也带着一面白旗，走到梅达德爵士所立的塔楼底下；为首一人是个须发如霜、十分练达的老者，名唤狄戈尔爵士，他不戴头盔立在众人之前，说道：“上头站着的可是梅达德爵士？”

“正是，”骑士答道；“你又是何人？是围在我们四周这支大军的统领么？”那边答道：“我是狄戈尔爵士，你想必也听人说起过；我在我家主公深谷男爵麾下统领这支大军，特来请问你要我们怎样。”梅达德爵士道：“我要见深谷男爵。”


===Original===

“He is sick this morning,” said Sir Degore, “and may not rise; but if thou wouldst render the town and the castle unto him, it is all one, thou mayst make me serve thy turn; I know his mind full well.”

Sir Medard laughed: “Nay,” said he, “we will wait for that till we may see the Baron himself. But tell me, Sir Knight, what is all this stir and hubbub in thine host this morning?”

Said Sir Degore, without tarrying the word one moment: “There is a great aid and refreshment come to us out of the East country, both of victual and men, and our folk be welcoming the men and sharing the victual.”

“There is nothing in this, then, that we have heard, that ye cannot find your Duke, and are seeking him up and down?”

“Nay, nothing,” said the greybeard, wagging his head. But the folk that were with him looked on each other and thought within themselves how wise the old man was. And Sir Medard spake when he might for his laughter: “Sir, thy lord did well to make thee captain under him, for thou art a wise and ready liar. But so it is that thou speakest with one who knoweth the tale better than thou. Ho ye, bring forward my lord.”

===Chinese===

“他今晨身上不好，”狄戈尔爵士说，“起不得身；不过你若肯把城池堡垒献给他，那全是一样，尽可以拿我来当他的替身；他的心思我了然得很。”

梅达德爵士笑了：“不，”他说，“这事且等着，等我们见得着男爵本人再说。只是请告诉我，骑士先生，今晨你军中这般喧腾骚扰，却是为何？”

狄戈尔爵士答话，半刻也不迟疑：“有一大队接济自东方国度来到我们这里，人马粮草都有，我们的弟兄正欢迎来人、分派粮草呢。”

“那么，我们听说的那桩事——你们寻不着你们的公爵，正四处上下找他——也是没有的事么？”

“没有的事，全没有的事，”白胡子老儿摇着头说。可他身后的人们面面相觑，心里暗暗赞叹这老人何等练达。梅达德爵士好容易忍住笑，才开口道：“先生，你家主公派你做他麾下的统领，真是派对了人，因为你是个出口成章、应付裕如的说谎老手。只是如今同你说话的这个人，对这段故事知道得比你还清楚。喂，来人，把我家爵爷带上来。”


===Original===

Straightway came two squires, who led a lean dark man between them, unarmed and clad in a long furred black gown. He took off his hat, and thereupon Sir Degore and all they below knew him for their lord. He spake at once and said: “Sir Degore and ye others, my lords and captains, can ye hear me?”

“Yea, lord,” said Sir Degore.

Then said the Baron: “This then is my word and commandment, that ye give leave to all our folk-in-arms to depart each one to his own house, and to bear away with him his weapons and armour and three horses if he be of the knighthood, and one if he be of the sergeantry; but the others, archers and villeins, may take one horse between three to bear their baggage and ease them on the journey. But the flour and wheat and wine, and all the neat and sheep, ye shall leave behind; for the folk of this countryside and the good town have occasion for them. But as to mine own matters which are of mine own person, as arms and raiment and jewels and the like, ye shall bring them unto me here in the good town, where I am minded to abide two or three days that I may hold counsel about weighty matters with the Porte and the Burgreve. Moreover, I would have thee, Sir Degore, and a five of my counsellors and a half score of my servants, come hither to me to abide with me for my aid and service while I tarry in Eastcheaping. Now this is my will and pleasure, and I shall be no wiser later on; wherefore do thou, Sir Degore, go straightway and tell my will to the captains and sergeants and the knights, so that the hosts may presently break up.”

Ye may deem how Sir Degore and the other Deepdalers were abashed when they knew that their lord was a captive in the hands of the foemen; yet they seemed to think that the terms of the good town were not so hard as might have been looked for, since they had gotten this so great advantage.

Now Sir Degore spake and said: “Sir Medard, wilt thou suffer me to come to thee, so that I may speak with my lord privily?” “To what end,” said Sir Medard, “since thou hast heard thy lord’s commandment? wilt thou not obey him?” “Yea,” said Degore, “if I have heard his last word; nevertheless were I fain to come up and speak with him.” “Come up then,” said Sir Medard; “yet I must warn thee that it may be easier for thee to come in to Eastcheaping today than to go out therefrom. Moreover, bethink ye if ye dally how it would be were we to open our gates and fall upon you with all ours, and ye disarrayed and leaderless.”

===Chinese===

立时上来两名扈从，中间押着一名清瘦黧黑的汉子，未带兵器，身上穿一袭裘皮的黑色长袍。他摘下帽子，狄戈尔爵士和下面众人当即认出正是自家主公。他随即开口说道：“狄戈尔爵士，还有你们诸位、列位大人与统领们，听得见我么？”

“听得见，主公，”狄戈尔爵士道。

于是男爵说道：“这便是我的谕令：着即准我们全军武装之人各自散归本家，随身带走各自的兵器甲胄；骑士身份的每人带走三匹马，军士身份的每人一匹；其余的弓手与庄户，每三人合领一匹马，好驮运行李、纾解路途劳顿。至于面粉、麦子、酒，连同所有的牛羊，都须留下；因为此间乡民和这座良城都用得着它们。至于纯属我个人之身的私物，如兵器衣物珠宝之类，你们须给我送到这良城里来；我打算在此盘桓两三天，好与市府和城守会商几件要务。此外，我要你狄戈尔爵士，连同五名谋臣、十名仆从，上我这里来，在我滞留东集期间随侍左右，供我驱使。这便是我的意旨与心愿，日后决无更改；所以，狄戈尔爵士，你即刻去把我的意旨传告众统领、军士和骑士们，好教各路人马即刻解散。”

可以想见，狄戈尔爵士和其他深谷人得知自家主公已落在敌人手中时，是何等的愕然失色；不过他们似乎也觉得，既已让对方占了这么大的便宜，这座良城开出的条款倒不像原先料想的那般苛刻。

于是狄戈尔爵士开口道：“梅达德爵士，你可容我上你那儿去，让我与我家主公私下说几句话？”“何必呢，”梅达德爵士道，“你既已听得你家主公的谕令，还不肯遵从么？”“遵从自然遵从，”狄戈尔说，“只要这确是他最后的话；然而我还是很想上去同他谈一谈。”“那就上来罢，”梅达德爵士说；“只是我须提醒你：今日进东集怕是要比日后再出去容易些。再者，你们若拖延迁延，也想想看：我们若大开城门，倾尽城中人马扑将出来，而你们阵脚散乱、无人统领，那会是何等光景。”


===Original===

Therewith he gave word to open the postern to Sir Degore, who entered and was brought to the top of the tower, and there he went up to the Baron and bent the knee to him and might not refrain his tears; but the Baron laughed, yet somewhat hardly. So they two went aside into an ingle of the tower toward the town, while sir Medard and his stood aloof a while. Then turned back Sir Degore to them of Eastcheaping, and said: “Sir Medard, I pray thee leave to depart to my host, that I may do after the bidding of my lord.”

“Yea, go,” said Sir Medard; “yet I would have thee remember that I pray for a long life for the Baron of Deepdale, since he hath become so good a friend to our town, and that thou wilt be in the wrong if thou do aught to shorten it.”

So Degore went his ways, and he and those counsellors and leaders went back sadly to the leaguer, and fell to work to undo all they had done the six months past. And it was no long time ere the stout men-at-arms of Deepdale began to flow away from before Eastcheaping, and the men of the town held good watch all the while; and ere it was evening, divers bands of them went out-of-gates in good order to see that none of the Deepdalers abode in array in the leaguer, and found nothing there which they had cause to dread. And they took much spoil of that which the Baron’s host must needs leave behind. Meanwhile, Sir Medard and his made what cheer they might to the Baron; and Sir Medard showed Osberne unto him, and told him all the tale of the wolves and the slaying of Hardcastle, and did him to wit that much of the valiancy which they of Eastcheaping had shown in the war came of this lad of Wethermel. And the Baron marvelled, and looked upon Osberne and said: “Well, lad, if ever thou art hard bestead, come thou to Deepdale, and we shall find somewhat for thee to do; and I bid thee thrive hale and well!”

Howbeit Sir Medard told not to the Baron that Osberne had been one of them that bore him off the last night. Yet somehow he came to know it in time to come; I wot not through whom or how.

===Chinese===

说罢他吩咐开边门放狄戈尔爵士进来；狄戈尔进了城，被带上塔顶，走到男爵面前屈膝下跪，忍不住落下泪来；男爵倒笑了，只是笑得颇有些勉强。于是他们二人踱到塔上朝城那面一处炉角的僻静所在，梅达德爵士和他的人则退开站了一会儿。随后狄戈尔爵士转身回到东集众人跟前，说道：“梅达德爵士，请你准我回营中去，好让我依我家主公的吩咐行事。”

“去罢，”梅达德爵士说；“只是望你记住：我祈愿深谷男爵福寿绵长，因为他已成了我们城的大大的好友；你若做什么折损他寿数的事，那便是你的不是了。”

于是狄戈尔自去了；他和那些谋臣头领黯然回到营中，动手把过去六个月里经营起来的一应工事尽数拆撤。没过多久，深谷那些剽悍的甲士便开始从东集城外陆陆续续地流退而去；城中人自始至终严加守望；天未黑，城中几队人马便出得城去，队列严整，查看再没有深谷人留在营中列阵的，却见那里已无一物足以令他们忌惮。男爵大军不得不留下的东西，他们掳得了许多。这其间，梅达德爵士和他的人尽力承欢，让男爵开怀；梅达德又把奥斯伯恩引见给他，把群狼的事和诛杀哈德卡斯尔的始末一一讲给他听，并使他知道，东集人在这次战事中表现的许多英勇，都是出自韦瑟梅尔的这个少年。男爵惊叹不已，看着奥斯伯恩说道：“好孩子，往后你若遇了窘难，只管到深谷来，我们总有事情给你做；我祝你康健兴旺！”

不过梅达德爵士并没有告诉男爵，昨夜把他劫走的人里头就有奥斯伯恩。然而日后不知怎的，他终究得知了此事；经由何人、如何得知，我便不得而知了。

## XXVIII / 二十八

===Original===

The Baron of Deepdale Makes Peace

So now the war was over, for the next day the Baron of Deepdale signed the deed of peace which gave up to the Porte of Eastcheaping all that for which they had withstood him; and withal some deal of ransom he had to pay for his own body, how much my tale-teller knoweth not, but deemeth that they would scarce put the snepe upon him as to bid but a squire’s or knight-bachelor’s ransom for a free baron, a lord of wide lands, who had under him towns, tolls, and markets.

So the ransom being paid, or some deal of it, and pledges left for the remnant, the Baron went his ways in no very evil mood, and it was soon seen that they of Eastcheaping would no longer need the men they had waged over and above those who were due to them for service, wherefore leave was given to such waged men to depart, and the Dalesmen among others. But gifts were given them largely, over and above their war-pay, and to Osberne and Stephen the Eater in especial. Unto whom, amongst other things, the Butchers’ guild of the good town did on the eve of his departure bring a great and fair ox, white of colour; and they had gilded the horns of the beast, and done him about with garlands: but on a scroll between the horns was fairly writ the words, The Eater’s Ox. Which gift Stephen received as it was given, very lovingly, and many a cup they drank together over him; but Stephen said ere his friends departed: “Yet look ye, lads of Eastcheaping, though this ox be mine, yet shall he not be the ox of the Eater; for slay him will I never, but let live on and on for love of our friends of Eastcheaping so long as I may buy, beg, or steal a cow’s grass for him.”

===Chinese===

深谷男爵议和

如此，战事便告终结：次日，深谷男爵就在和约上签了押，把他们当初为抗拒他而力争的种种权益，尽数让与了东集市府；此外，他还须为自身缴纳一笔赎金，数目几何，我这说书人不得而知，只是心中忖度：对一位自由男爵、一位坐拥广土、麾下辖有城镇、税捐与市集的领主，他们总不至轻慢他到这般地步，只索一名扈从或见习骑士身价的赎金罢。

赎金既已缴付——至少缴了一部分，余下的也立下了信物作保——男爵便自去了，心绪倒也不算太坏。不久便见分晓：东集人再也用不着那些额外雇募、超出应役定额的兵士了，于是便准许这等雇兵各自散去，谷民也在其中。但众人在军饷之外又厚予赏赐，与奥斯伯恩和饕客斯蒂芬两人的尤为丰厚。礼物之中有一件：临行前夜，好城的屠夫行会给两人牵来一头又大又俊的公牛，毛色雪白；他们给牛角镀了金，牛身上缠了花环：两角之间还悬着一幅卷轴，上书一行端丽的字：“饕客之牛”。这份礼物，斯蒂芬怀着满腔的情意照单全收；众人又对着这牛彼此对饮了许多杯；只是临别之前，斯蒂芬说道：“不过东集的弟兄们，你们且听着：这牛虽说是我的，它却永不做‘饕客之牛’；宰它的事，我断断不干，要让它长长久久活下去，好记着我们东集朋友的情谊——只消我还买得到、讨得到、偷得到一口母牛的草料喂它。”

===Original===

As for Osberne, though he bought in the booths a pretty many of such things as were goodly and little, of goldsmiths’ work and the like, to flit to his friend across the Sundering Flood, yet no gift would he take, save a very fair armour of the spoils of Deepdale: and this was no gift, said Sir Medard, but what he had earned himself by hard toil enough.

All loved him, but Sir Medard in especial, who had fain dubbed him knight; but Osberne would not, and said that such had been no wont of his fathers before him; and he looked never to go very far from the Dale and for no long while. “And even if I may not live there,” quoth he, “I look to die there;” and he reddened therewith till the eyes looked light in the face of him. But Medard said: “Wheresoever thou livest or diest thou wilt live and die a great-heart. But this I bid thee, whenso thou hast need of a friend who may show thee the road into the world of deeds, when thou hast aught to hide or aught to seek, come thou unto me, and be sure that I shall not fail thee.”

Osberne thanked him from his whole heart, and they kissed and departed with all love; and as the Dalesmen rode down the street toward the western gate, it was full of folk shouting out praises and blessings; and the windows were full of women who cast down flowers on them as they went along, saying that but for these stout-hearts they might have had neither town nor honour nor children, and that nought was good enough for such friends as these. Thus rode the Dalesmen out of Eastcheaping.

But of the ten score and six that had ridden out of the Dale, two score and two were lacking, who had either been slain in battle or so sorely hurt that they were no longer fight-worthy; but sixteen had dropped in by ones and twos and threes to fill the places of these, so that they rode back but little fewer than they came.


===Chinese===

至于奥斯伯恩，他虽在集市铺面里买了好些精巧细小的物件——金匠手艺之类——预备带过分离洪流去送给对岸的朋友，礼物却一概不肯领受，只收下了深谷战利品中一副极精美的甲胄。而这一件，梅达德爵士说，算不得什么礼物，原是他自己吃够了苦头、亲手挣来的。

人人都爱他，梅达德爵士爱得尤其深切，巴不得就封他做骑士；奥斯伯恩却推辞不就，说自家的列祖列宗从无此惯例；又说他也从不指望远离谷地，纵然离去，也去不长久。“便算我不能住在那儿，”他说，“我也指望死在那儿；”话到此处，他脸上登时飞红，红得连那双眼睛在脸庞上都显得放出光来。梅达德却道：“你无论在哪儿生、在哪儿死，总归生也是一条好汉，死也是一条好汉。只是我嘱咐你一句：几时你需要一位能替你指点功业世界门路的朋友，几时你有事要遮掩、有物要寻访，只管到我这儿来；你尽管放心，我决不误你。”

奥斯伯恩打心底里向他道谢，两人亲吻作别，情意殷殷。谷民们沿街往西门行去时，满街都是高声欢呼、赞颂祝福的人群；窗口也挤满了妇人，向他们一路抛撒鲜花，口中说道：若没有这些刚勇的汉子，她们只怕城也没有、名节也没有、儿女也没有；这样恩重的朋友，拿什么都报答不够。谷民们就这样出了东集城。

只是当初二百零六人骑行出谷，如今短了四十二名：他们或已殒于战阵，或伤重至此，再不堪临阵了；不过后来又有十六人三个两个、陆陆续续赶来，补上了这些空缺，是以归去时的人数，比起来时相差无几。

## XXIX / 二十九

===Original===

Osberne and His Men Return to Wethermel

Now on a fair evening a little ere sunset of the beginning of October, came those Dalesmen amongst the black rocks and rough places that crowned the bent which looked down west over the Dale. And now, though they had been talking merrily and loud for the last three hours, their hearts were so full within them that scarce a word might they say one to another. And when at last they had won through that rocky tangle and had opened Wethermel, and nought lay before them but the grassy slopes and the widespread valley cleft by the line of the Sundering Flood; now, when they saw in the clear air the grey houses of Wethermel lying together, and the smoke of the evening cooking fires going up to the heavens, and the sheep wending on, thick and huddling before the driving of three tall men, and the kine moving toward the byre and the women amongst them, then this befell: that whereas they had been all of one mind that when they came to the crown of the bent, they would spur on and race merrily toward Wethermel, yet now when it lay before them, and there was so little a way betwixt them and its hearth, they all of them with one consent drew rein and sat still on their horses, as if they had suddenly come face to face with the host of the foemen. Yea, some there were, and they rather of the oldest than the youngest, who might not refrain them, but fell a-weeping and sobbing, whether it were for joy or sorrow, or a blending of both, may scarce be said.

Osberne wept not: sooth to say, the turmoil of hope and fear within his heart ate up somewhat the softness that might else have mastered him at this new sight of his fathers’ house. He rode forth before the others, and lifted up his voice and loudly and clearly cried a blessing on the Dale and the dwellers therein, and then rode soberly down the bent, and the others followed him still silently. But when they were drawn anigh, and every soul, man, woman and child, ran forth from the garth to meet and welcome them, then at last their joy brake forth, and they gat off their horses and gave themselves up to the caresses of the women and the embracing of the carles, and loud was the speech and the laughter amongst them.

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩偕众返回韦瑟梅尔

且说十月一个晴好的傍晚，日头将落未落之际，那队谷民来到了黑岩嶙峋、崎岖坎坷的高处——那正是草坡顶上的冠冕，朝西俯瞰着谷地。这时，虽说方才三个时辰里他们还谈得欢畅、说得响亮，此刻心头却满得盛不下，彼此之间竟说不出一句话来。待到他们终于穿过那片乱石丛，韦瑟梅尔豁然在望，眼前再无别物，唯有青青草坡，与那被分离洪流一线剖开的宽阔谷地；此刻，清明的空气里，他们望见韦瑟梅尔的灰屋子攒聚一处，晚炊的炊烟直上云天，羊群密密匝匝挤作一团，在三个高条汉子的驱赶下前行，牛正朝牛栏挪动，妇人们夹在牛群中间——于是便有了这么一桩事：他们原都同心合意，说到得坡顶，便要催马竞驰，欢欢喜喜赛着奔向韦瑟梅尔；可如今庄子就在眼前，离它的炉火只余这么一小段路了，他们却个个不约而同勒住缰绳，安坐马上不动，仿佛骤然间同敌军的大阵打了个照面。真的，更有那么几个人——年长的居多，倒不是最年轻的那拨——再也把持不住，竟自啼哭呜咽起来；那是为喜还是为悲，抑或悲喜交并，可就说不大清了。

奥斯伯恩没有落泪：说句实在话，他心中希望与忧惧交相翻腾，把那点柔情吞蚀掉了不少——若非如此，乍然重见父祖之宅，这柔情原是要把他制伏的。他策马赶在众人前头，扬起声音，高亢而清朗地为谷地祝祷，为谷中的居民祝福，随后端稳地缓缓驰下草坡，其余的人依旧默默跟在后面。可是待到行得近了，男女老幼、每一个魂灵都从院垣里奔出来迎接他们、欢迎他们，这时他们的欢喜终于迸发出来：众人纷纷下马，任凭妇人们的爱抚、汉子们的拥抱，人丛中言语喧阗，笑语连连。

===Original===

Osberne was first met by Nicholas his grandsire, who kissed and embraced him, and then gave him up to his grandam and the foster-mother, and one or other of these twain would scarce let go of him a long while.

But now was riding and running after victual for so big a company of men; for nought would serve the folk of Wethermel but that the whole fellowship must abide there that night. But all was got ready in a while, and meanwhile the stay-at-homes might not have enough of praising and caressing the folk returned, and everything they said or did was a wonder.

At last the feast was arrayed, and the hall was thronged as much as might be, and folk fell to meat, and now they were all exceeding merry; and when they had done eating, the boards were drawn to make more room, and they fell to the drink, and after the first cup to Christ, and the second to Allhallows, the third was drunk to the home-comers from the war. Yet were not the stay-at-homes to be put off with so little, and they called a cup for Osberne the Captain of the warriors; and when it had been drunk, then all folk looked toward the captain to see what he would do; but he rose up and stood in his place, his cheek flushed and his eyes sparkling: and the word came into his mouth and he sang:

===Chinese===

头一个迎着奥斯伯恩的，是他祖父尼古拉斯庄主；老人又吻又抱，随后把他交给他祖母和乳母；而这两位老妇人，这一位也好、那一位也好，都久久不肯放他走。

这时便又是一番骑马奔跑，为这么一大伙人张罗吃食；因为韦瑟梅尔的庄客说什么也不依，定要全队人马当夜都宿在庄上。好在不消多久，一切便都齐备；这当口，留守在庄的人夸赞疼爱还乡的亲人，怎么也疼不够、夸不够，他们说的每一句话、做的每一件事，都成了奇事一桩。

终于，宴席摆齐了，厅堂里挤得不能再满，众人放开大嚼，这时人人快活到了十分；吃罢，餐板撤下腾出地方，众人又开怀畅饮起来：头一杯敬基督，第二杯敬诸圣堂，第三杯便敬那些从战地归家的还乡人。可留守的人岂肯这么轻易便算了，他们又为好汉们的统领奥斯伯恩叫上一杯；这杯饮罢，众人都望着统领，看他要做什么；他却站起身来，立在原处，双颊烧得通红，两眼灼灼放光：词句涌上口边，他便唱道：

===Original===

> The War-god’s gale
> Drave down the Dale
> And thrust us out
> To the battle-shout;
> We wended far
> To the wall of war
> And trod the way
> Where the edges lay,
> The rain of the string rattled rough on the field
> Where the haysel was hoarded with sword-edge and shield.

> Long lived the sun
> When the play was begun,
> And little but white
> Was the moon all night;
> But the days drew in
> And work was to win,
> And on the snow
> Lay men alow,
> And at Yule fared we feasting in war-warded wall
> And the helm and the byrny were bright in the hall.

> Then changed the year
> And spring was dear,
> But no maid went
> On mead or bent,
> For there grew on ground
> New battle-round,
> New war-wall ran
> Round houses of man,
> There tower to tower oft dark and dim grew
> At noontide of summer with rain of the yew.

> Neath point and edge
> In the battle hedge
> We dwelt till wore
> Late summer o’er;
> In the autumn night
> We steered aright
> The wisdom bark
> Through the steel-thronged dark,
> The warrior we wafted from out of the fray,
> And he woke midst the worthy and hearkened their say.

> Now peace is won
> And all strife done,
> And in our hands
> The fame of lands
> Aback we bear
> To the dale the dear,
> And the Fathers lie
> Made glad thereby.
> Now blossometh bliss in the howes of the old
> At our tale growing green from their tale that is told.

Loud was the glee and the shouting at his song, and all men said that every whit thereof was sooth, and that this was the best day that had ever dawned on Wethermel; and great joy and bliss was on the hall till they must needs go to their rest. So changed was Wethermel, the niggard once, and that, it might be deemed, was but one youngling’s doing.

===Chinese===

> 战神掀动的狂风
> 刮下了谷中，
> 将我们卷出，
> 掷进战吼的呼呼；
> 我们跋涉迢迢，
> 直抵战争的高壕，
> 踏行过的道上，
> 尽是锋刃横躺，
> 弓弦的骤雨在沙场上噼啪作响，
> 那里的干草，凭剑锋盾缘才得护藏。

> 开场那阵，太阳走得很长，
> 月亮彻夜皓皓，白得近乎精光；
> 可白昼渐渐收短，
> 战功还待人建，
> 白雪之上，
> 儿郎偃仰，
> 岁末冬至，我们在战守的城垣里欢宴，
> 厅堂中头盔与锁子铠灼灼闪闪。

> 后来岁序更张，
> 春光何其芬芳，
> 却再无一个少女
> 走上草甸、走上草坡，
> 只因大地上
> 生出了新的战场，
> 新的战墙
> 围了世人的住房，
> 仲夏亭午，塔楼对塔楼常常转成晦暝，
> 为的是紫杉之雨纷纷倾。

> 在锋镝之下，
> 在战阵之篱下，
> 我们栖身，直待
> 晚夏消磨过来；
> 在秋天的夜里，
> 我们将那智慧之舟
> 把舵把得端正，
> 驶过钢铁丛集的幽冥，
> 我们把那武士送出厮杀的纷乱，
> 他醒来置身群贤之中，听他们言谈。

> 如今和平已赢获，
> 一切争斗都收煞，
> 万邦的荣名
> 都在我们手中擎，
> 我们携着它归返
> 那亲亲的谷间，
> 列祖列宗躺着，
> 心头为此欣然，
> 如今福泽在先人的古冢里开出花朵——
> 我们的故事从他们讲毕的故事里，长出青青的苗禾。

他歌罢，欢声与喝彩震耳欲聋，人人争说歌中字字句句皆是实情，又说日头从未在韦瑟梅尔照临过比这更好的一天；满厅的大喜大乐，直延到众人不得不去安歇方休。韦瑟梅尔，这个当初寒碜悭吝的庄子，就这样改换了容颜——而这，想来不过是区区一个小后生的作为罢了。

## XXX / 三十

===Original===

Osberne Goes to the Trysting-Place

But on the morrow ere the day was old, the guests departed in all contentment each to his own folk, and Osberne and the Wethermel men led them out with blessings.

When they were all gone and the unwonted stir was over, it seemed to Osberne as if he were awaking from a dream, and his heart was in a turmoil of hope and fear, so that he knew not what to do till he was once more at the Bight of the Cloven Knoll. He tarried for nought save to take up the gifts of Eastcheaping, and he had no weapon with him save his bow and arrows wherewith to flit the said gifts across the water, but he was gaily clad in a coat of green, flowered with gold, which he had bought him at Eastcheaping; and a fair and lovely youth he looked, as he strode along at his swiftest toward the trysting-place, his face flushed, his brows a little knit with mingled trouble and joy, his lips parted with his eager breathing. Whiles as he went he said to himself, How many chances and changes there were, and how might he expect to find Elfhild there again? And next, when he had enough afflicted himself with thinking of her sick, or dead, or wedded, his strong heart of a youth threw it off again, and he thought, How could evil such as that befall him, the stalwarth and joyous?

So he fared till he came within sight of the ness, and saw no figure there on the top of it: yet he straightway fell to running, as though he knew she had been waiting for him a long while; but as he ran he kept his eyes down on the ground, so that he might not see her place empty of her. But when he came to his place he lifted up his eyes, and there to his great joy saw her coming up the ness; and when she saw him she uttered a great cry and spread out her arms and reached out to him. But as for him, he might make neither word nor sound a great while, but stood looking on her. Then he said: “Is it well with thee?”

“O yea, yea,” she said, “and over-well as now.”

“Art thou wedded?” said he.

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩赴相会之处

次日天色尚早，宾客们便个个称心满意，各归本族去了；奥斯伯恩同韦瑟梅尔的汉子们送他们出来，一路祝祷不已。

待众人都去了、那阵不惯有的喧扰也平息下来，奥斯伯恩只觉自己恍如从一场梦中醒来，心里指望与忧惧交相翻腾，教他不知如何是好，直恨不得立刻又回到裂丘湾去。他只为取了东集的礼物才略略耽搁；随身别无兵器，只有弓和箭，好把那些礼物飘送过水面；身上却穿得鲜丽，是一件绿色外衣，金线团花，是他在东集给自家买的。他以最快的脚步向相会之处赶去，望去真是个俊美可爱的少年：脸庞红扑扑的，眉尖因忧喜交集微微蹙着，双唇因喘息急切而微微张开。一路走着，他时而自忖：世间有多少机缘变数啊，他又怎能指望埃尔弗希尔德还在那里？转而他又拿她病了、死了、嫁了这些念头把自家折磨了个够，他那一颗少年的坚强的心却又把这一切甩开，心想：他这样一个雄壮欢快的人，哪能遭那样的凶事？

他这样一路行去，直到望得见海岬，却见岬顶上不见人影；但他立刻拔腿奔跑起来，仿佛明知她已等了他好半天似的；一面跑，一面却只顾低眼盯着地面，生怕瞧见她惯立的地方空着无人。待到了自家惯立之处，他才抬起眼来，喜出望外，只见她正从海岬走上坡来；她一望见他，便高声欢呼，张开双臂，向他这边探身过来。他却半晌作声不得，一句话也说不出，只管立着呆望她。然后他说道：“你可安好？”

“嗳，好，好，”她说，“而且这会儿好得都过了分。”

“你可嫁了人？”他问。


===Original===

“Yea,” she said, “unto thee.”

“O would that we were, would that we were!” said Osberne.

“O!” she said, “be not sad this morning, or wish for aught so that it grieve thee. Bethink thee how dear this moment is, now at last when our eyes behold each other.”

“Hast thou come here often to look for me?” said he. She said: “It was the fourteenth of May was a year that we parted; now is this the eighth day of October. That makes five hundred and eleven days: not oftener than that have I come here to look for thee.”

So piteous-kind she looked as she spake, that his bosom heaved and his face changed, and he wept. She said: “I wish I had not said that to make thee weep for me, my dear.” He spake as his face cleared: “Nay, my dear, it was not all for thee, but for me also; and it was not all for grief, but for love.” She said: “With this word thou givest me leave to weep;” and she wept in good sooth.

===Chinese===

“嫁了，”她说，“嫁的是你。”

“唉，但愿咱们真的成了亲，但愿咱们真的成了亲！”奥斯伯恩道。

“嗳！”她说，“今儿早晨可别伤心，也别许什么愿，倒教自家愁苦。你想一想，这一刻何等宝贵：到如今，咱们的眼睛总算又彼此相望了。”

“你常到这里来盼我么？”他问。她道：“咱们分离，是在一年前的五月十四日；今日已是十月初八。算来共计五百一十一天：我来此盼你，再勤也不过如此了。”

她说这话时神情那般哀婉温存，教他胸脯起伏，面色都变了，于是他落下泪来。她说：“亲爱的，我但愿不曾说那句话，惹得你为我落泪。”他面色转霁，说道：“不，亲爱的，这不全是为你，也是为我自己；也不全是悲伤，更是恩爱。”她道：“你这一句话，便是准我哭了；”于是她当真哭了起来。


===Original===

Then in a while she said: “And now thou wilt sit down, wilt thou not? and tell me all thy tale, and of thy great deeds, some wind whereof hath been blown to us across the Sundering Flood. And sweet it will be to hear thy voice going on and on, and telling me dear things of thyself.”

“Even so will I do,” said Osberne, “if thou wilt; yet I were fain to hear of thee and how thou hast fared; and thy words would I hear above all things.” The voice of him quavered as he spake, and he seemed to find it hard to bring any words out: but his eyes were devouring her as if he could never have enough of looking on her. Forsooth there was cause, so fair she was, and he now come far into his eighteenth year. She was that day clad all in black, without any adornment, and her hair was knit up as a crown about her beauteous head, which sat upon her shoulders as the swan upon the billow: her hair had darkened since the days of her childhood, and was now brown mingled with gold, as though the sun were within it; somewhat low it came down upon her forehead, which was broad and white; her eyes were blue-grey and lustrous, her cheeks a little hollow, but the jaw was truly wrought, and fine and clear, and her chin firm and lovely carven; her lips not very full, but red and lovely, her nose straight and fine. The colour of her clear and sweet, but not blent with much red: rather it was as if the gold of her hair had passed over her face and left some little deal behind there. In all her face was a look half piteous, as though she craved the love of folk; but yet both mirth and swift thought brake through it at whiles, and sober wisdom shaded it into something like sternness. Low-bosomed she was yet, and thin-flanked, and had learned no tricks and graces of movement such as women of towns and great houses use for the beguiling of men. But the dear simpleness of her body in these days when the joy of childhood had left her, and a high heart of good longing was ever before her, was an allurement of love and far beyond any fooling such as that.

Now she said: “How thou lookest on me, dear Osberne, and thy face is somewhat sober; is there aught that thou likest not in me? I will do as thou biddest, and tell all the little there is to tell about me, ere thou tellest me all the mickle thou hast to tell about thee.”

He said, and still spake as if the words were somewhat hard to find: “I look upon thee, Elfhild, because I love thee, and because thou hast outgrown thy dearness of a year and a half agone and become a woman, and I see thee so fair and lovely, that I fear for thee and me, that I desire more than is my due, and that never shall we mend our sundering; and that even what I have may be taken from me.” She smiled, yet somewhat faintly, and spake: “I call that ill said; yet shalt thou not make me weep thereby, such joy as I have of the love in thy words. But come, sit thou down, and I shall tell thee my tidings.”

So they sat down as nigh unto the edge as they might; and Osberne spake no more for that while, but looked and listened, and Elfhild said: “Day by day I have come hither, sometimes sadder and sorrier than at others, whiles with more hope, and whiles with less, whiles also with none at all. Of that thou wottest already or may bethink thee. Of tidings to call tidings the first is that my kinswoman, my mother’s sister, has changed her life: she died six months ago, and we brought her to earth by the church of Allhallows the West, hard by the place of the Cloven Mote. Needs must I say that, though she was the last one of my kindred, the loss of her was no very grievous sorrow to me, forever she had heeded me little and loved me less, though she used me not cruelly when I was little; and her burial was a stately one as for a poor house in the West Dale. Now furthermore, as for the carline who is the only one left to look after me, by my deeming she doth love me, and moreover she hath belike more of a might than were to be looked for of so old and frail-seeming a woman, and that besides here mickle wisdom. Whereof hearken this, which is the second tidings of note I have to tell thee. It is now some two months ago, when summer was waning into autumn, that on an evening just after sunset we were sitting after our wont in our house, which, though it be neither grand nor great, is bigger than we need for us twain. Comes a knock on the door, and the carline goes thereto, and is followed back into the chamber by a tall man, clad neither as one of our countryside nor as a warrior, but in a long black gown with furred edges. He had no weapons save a short sword and a whittle in his girdle; he was not ill-looked, black-bearded and ruddy-faced, and seemed strong-built, a man of about five and forty winters. He hailed us courteously, and asked if he might abide with us till morning, and we naysaid him not, if he might do with such cheer as we might make him. He smiled and said any cheer was better worth to him than the desert as at that time: and he said withal that he had a way-beast without who was as weary as was he; and, says he, there is a pair of saddlebags on him, which many would not deem overmuch of a burden, if they had not very far to carry it.

===Chinese===

过了一会她说道：“如今你且坐下，好么？把你全部的经历讲给我听，讲讲你的那些大功业——有些风声，已经越过分离洪流吹到我们这边来了。听你的声音这样滔滔不断地讲下去，讲你自身种种可爱的事，那将是何等甜美。”

“我自然依你，”奥斯伯恩说，“只要你愿意；只是我也渴想听听你的情形、你这些日子怎样过来；你的言语，比什么我都想听。”他说这话时声音发颤，仿佛什么字句都难以出口：一双眼睛却把她吞噬也似地望着，仿佛怎么也看她不够。这也确有缘故：她实在美，而他也已深深走入了他的第十八个年头。那一天她通身穿着黑衣，不带一点妆饰；头发在那秀美的头上绾作一顶冠冕，那头颅安放在她双肩上，宛如天鹅浮在波涛之上。她的头发自孩提时代以来颜色深了些，如今是褐色里掺着金色，仿佛太阳就藏在发中；额前的头发微微低覆，那额头又宽阔又白皙；她的眼睛是蓝灰色的，莹莹有光；两颊略见凹陷，可下颚却生得端正，细腻而明晰，下巴坚实，好似可爱的雕镂；双唇不算丰厚，却红艳可爱；鼻子又直又秀。她的面色清丽甜美，却不掺多少红晕：倒好像她头发里的金色掠过面庞，在那里留下了一星半点。她整张脸上有一种半是哀婉的神情，仿佛渴求着人们的爱怜；然而欢笑和敏捷的思绪又不时从底下透出来，沉静的智慧又给它罩上一层近乎严峻的阴影。她的胸脯犹未丰隆，腰身犹自纤瘦，也不曾学会城里和大宅门的妇人们用来蛊惑男子的那些体态与风致。然而，在童年之乐既已离她而去、一颗怀着美好渴慕的高昂之心又始终牵引着她的这些日子里，她身子这份可爱的质朴，本身便是爱的魅惑，远非那种弄姿的把戏可比。

这时她说道：“你这样瞧着我，亲爱的奥斯伯恩，你面色有些沉郁；莫非我身上有什么你不中意的处所么？我依你的吩咐，先把我那一点可讲的事都讲了，然后你再把你那一大堆要讲的事讲给我听。”

他开口了，说话仍像字句难以寻觅似的：“我望着你，埃尔弗希尔德，因为我爱你；也因为一年半之前你那份可爱，如今已长过了头，出落成一个妇人了。我见你这样美丽可爱，便为你我二人害怕：怕我奢求了非分的东西，怕咱们这场分离永无弥合之日，又怕连我眼下所有的这一点，也要被夺了去。”她微微一笑，却笑得有些淡，说道：“这话我说它讲得不好；然而你休想凭它教我哭——你话里那份恩爱，已给了我这样的欢喜。来罢，坐下，我便把我的消息讲给你听。”

于是两人挨着水边、尽量坐到最近处；这一会儿奥斯伯恩不再言语，只是望着她、听她讲。埃尔弗希尔德道：“我日复一日到这里来，有时比旁的日子更愁苦些，有时指望多些，有时指望少些，有时竟全无指望。这些你已知道，或者自家想得起来。说到当得起消息二字的，头一件是：我的亲眷、我母亲的姊妹，已经辞了今生：她六个月前死了，我们把她安葬在西岸诸圣堂旁，就在裂丘大会场的近旁。我不能不说，她虽是我亲人中最后的一个，她的死却算不得教我如何悲痛；因为她向来不大把我放在心上，爱更是少，虽然我小时候她待我并不残酷；照西岸谷地一户穷人家来说，她的葬礼也算得一场体面的葬礼。再说那位老婆子，她是如今留下来照管我的唯一的人：照我看来，她倒是真心爱我；况且她的能耐，多半还超出人们对这样一个年老而看似衰弱的妇人的料想，这还不算她那满腹的智慧。这里你且听一桩，便是我要告诉你的第二件要紧消息。那是约莫两个月前，夏天正衰入秋天的时候：一日傍晚，日落才过，我们照常坐在家里——我们那房子虽说不上宏伟宽大，给我们两人住却绰绰有余。忽然门上来了敲声，老婆子便去开门，随在她身后走进屋里来的，是一个高大的汉子：他衣着既不像我们乡里人，也不像武人，却是一件缘边镶皮的黑色长袍。他腰带上除了短剑和一柄小刀，别无兵器；他相貌不恶，黑须红脸，看来筋骨结实，年纪约莫四十五个冬天。他彬彬有礼地向我们招呼，问能否在我们这里栖身到明晨；我们也不曾回绝他，只要他不嫌我们所能张罗的这般款待。他微笑说，随便什么款待，在那时那地对他都比荒野强得多；他又说，他外面还有一头赶路的牲口，同他一样困乏；还说，那牲口身上挂着一对褡裢，若不是要驮着它走极远的路，许多人本不觉得那是多大的负担。


===Original===

“So I went out a-doors with him to see after his nag and saddlebags; and I led the horse into the same stall where was winter quarters for our two horses; but this was a very big stark beast, grey of colour, such as we have not in this land; and I gave him hay and barley, but the saddlebags he brought back with him into the chamber. And he kept ever by my side on the way there and back, and looked at me oft in the failing light, though I was but in my sorry old raiment with bare feet, in such guise as thou hast not seen me for years, my dear. Howsoever, I heeded it not at the time, and we both came back into the chamber, where Dame Anna had now lighted the candles. Shortly to say, we put what meat and drink we might before our guest, and he seemed well content therewith; and he was merry with us, and showed himself a man of many words deftly strung together, and spared not to tell us many things about tidings of far and noble countries, and the ways of men both great and small therein. And he said that he was a chapman journeying after gain, and looked to buy wares in the Dale, and therewith he asked us if we had aught to sell him, but Anna laughed and said: ‘Fair sir, were ye to buy all this and all that is in it, from groundsell to roofridge, and all our kine and sheep and horses to boot, little would the tide of gold ebb in thy bags yonder.’ ‘I wot not,’ said he; ‘who may say what treasure ye have been hoarding here this long while?’ He looked on me as he spake, and I reddened and looked down, for in my heart I was thinking of the pipe and the gemmed necklace which the Dwarfs had given me. And yet more than all, of thy gifts, Osberne, which have been so dear to me: for soothly to say, of these matters I had never told Dame Anna, though she knoweth that I go oft to look upon thee here and that I love thee. However, that talk ran off, and presently the chapman got to asking Anna about the matters of the Dale, and the ways of its folk, and amongst other things as to how wealthy they were, and she answered him simply as she could. He asked her also if they loved their bairns and children well, and also if they had any custom thereabout of casting any of their women-children forth, if it happened to be their fortune to have many daughters and little meat, and that especially when the years were bad. But thereat she cried out Haro! and said that such a deed was unheard of, and that when times were bad and there was lack, then hand helped foot and foot hand.

“ ‘Well,’ says he, smiling, ‘that failed Hamdir’s Sons once, and may do others again.’ Then he asked withal if it were not true that things had run short in the Dale this last season; and she answered, as was true of this west side of the Dale, where was no man called to war, that so it was. And again that talk dropped. But the carline, methought, looked keenly at him. After a while Anna asked the guest if he had will to go to bed, and he answered, No, he would wake the meat well into his belly. Then she bade me fare to bed, which I did, nought loth, for when all was said, I scarce liked the looks of the man. As for my bed, it was a shut-bed, and opened not out of the chamber wherein we were, but out of an inner one, rather long than wide. There I lay down and went to sleep before long, but deemed I heard no little talk going on betwixt Anna and the guest ere I forgat it all. And moreover Anna came to me and waved her hands over me before I went off sound.

“But when I woke again it seemed to me that I had slept long, but I slipped out of bed and laid hold of my smock to do it on, and even therewith I shrank aback, for there before me, naked in his shirt and holding the door of my shut-bed with one hand and his whittle in the other, was the stranger. But therewithal came Dame Anna and said: ‘Heed him not, for as yet he is asleep though his eyes be open. Do on thy raiment speedily, my Elfhild, and come forth with me, and let him wake up by himself.’ Even so I did, not rightly understanding her words. But when we were gotten into the garth and the mead Anna told me all, to wit, how that this wretch, after I had gone to sleep, had bidden her a price for me to bear me off safely and wholly with him. And that may easily be done, says he, as I see of thee that thou art wise in wizardry and canst throw the maiden into a sleep which she will not awake from till due time is; for, says he, I want two things, to have her in mine arms to do as I will with, and thereafter to bear her home with me, will she nill she. ‘Now,’ said Anna, ‘I would not wholly gainsay him at once, for I would have my fox safe in my trap; so I hemmed and hawed, and said that he might belike rue his bargain unless he were full sure what it were worth; and to be short, I so egged him on and drew him back, and drew him back and egged him on, that at last he took off his outer raiment, gat his bare whittle in one hand, and laid the other on the door. Now, my dear, I have long known thy door that I may so do that it will do my will in many matters; so when I saw the chapman’s hand on the edge thereof, I spake a few words to it and went to bed myself, whereas I wotted that runagate could not move hand from door-board, or foot from floorboard, till the time which I had appointed to him; and thee also I had sent to sleep till the very time when thou didst awaken e’en now.’ ‘But what shall we do now?’ said I. Said Anna: ‘We will abide here in the shaw: there is meat on the board for the guest, and his raiment will not be hard to find, and he knows where are his horse and his gear and his saddlebags. I doubt me he will not be eager to say farewell either to thee or to me; for he is not man enough to take his sword in his fist against even an old carline and a young maiden.’ So in the shaw we gat us; as I have told thee, it is at the back of our houses but a furlong off. And there we lay till a little past noon, when we heard a horse going not far off. So we crept to the very edge of the wood and looked forth privily, and presently we saw our chapman riding off west with his saddlebags and all, and his face was worn and doleful; at that Anna grinned spitefully, nor for my part might I altogether refrain my laughter. But thou dost not laugh, Osberne?” He sprang up and cried out fiercely: “I would I had been there to cleave his skull! Many a better man I have slain for less cause.”

Then they were silent a while, and she sat looking on him fondly, till she spake at last: “Sweetheart, art thou angry with me for telling this tale?”

===Chinese===

“于是我同他出到门外，去照看他的驽马和褡裢；我把马牵进我们那两匹马过冬的栏里——这却是一匹十分高大倔壮的牲口，灰色的，我们这地方没有这样的马。我给它添了干草和大麦，褡裢他却自家提回屋里去了。来回的路上，他始终紧挨在我身边走，在渐暗的天光里频频看我，虽然我不过穿着一身破旧的衣裳，赤着脚——这副模样，亲爱的，你已有好几年不曾见过了。不过我当时没有理会，我们两人一同回到屋里，那时安娜大娘已点起了蜡烛。长话短说，我们把办得出的酒食摆在客人面前，他似乎十分受用；他同我们谈笑，显得是个满肚子话、又串得灵巧的人，又毫不吝惜地给我们讲了许多远邦贵国的新闻，讲那里贵贱大小人物的风习。他说自家是个逐利赶路的货郎，指望在谷地采买货物，接着便问我们可有东西卖他；安娜却笑了，说：‘好先生，你就是把这一切连房带物都买下，从地基买到屋脊，再把我们的牛羊马匹一股脑儿都添上，你那边褡裢里的金潮也泻落不了多少。’‘那可说不准，’他说；‘谁说得准你们这许久以来，在这里囤下了什么财宝呢？’他说这话时眼睛望着我，我涨红了脸，低下头去，因为我心里正想着矮人们给我的那管笛子和那条宝石项链。而比这些更要紧的，是想你送的礼物，奥斯伯恩，它们于我多么珍贵：因为说实话，这些事我从不曾告诉过安娜大娘，虽然她知道我常来这里望你，知道我爱你。不过那一番话滑了过去，随后货郎问起安娜种种谷地的事情、谷民的风习，其间也问到他们的家境殷实到什么地步，安娜便照自家所知、怎样老实怎样答他。他又问她，他们可疼爱自家的娃娃儿女；又问他们可有什么风俗，倘若命里女儿生得多、肉食又短少，便把女娃子撵出门去——年成不好的时节尤其如此。可她一听这话便高声叫屈，说这样的事真是闻所未闻；又说时世艰难、缺衣少食的时候，手帮着脚，脚帮着手。

“‘也罢，’他含笑道，‘这话在哈姆迪尔诸子身上就不曾应验过，往后保不齐还要在旁人身上失灵。’接着他又问，这最近一季，谷地里的物事可真的短少了；她答说确是如此——就西岸谷地而论倒是实情，这里没有人被征调去打仗。那话头又滑了过去。可是那老婆子，我瞧着，却把他打量得好生锐利。过了一会，安娜问客人可想上床安歇了；他答说不忙，还要醒着喝上几盅，把肚子里的肉食好生送下去。于是她吩咐我先去睡，我巴不得呢，因为说到底，我不大喜欢那人的相貌。说到我的床，那是一张闭床，床门不开在我们所在的外间，却开在一间里间——那屋子长而不宽。我在那里躺下，不多时便睡着了，只朦胧觉得，在忘却一切之前，还听见安娜同客人絮絮说了好一阵子话。而且在我睡沉之前，安娜还走到我床前，在我头顶上挥了几下手。

“可等我再醒来，只觉已睡了许久；我溜下床来，抓起衬衣要穿，恰在此时，我猛地一缩：只见那生人立在我面前，身上只一件衬衫，一手扶着我闭床的床门，一手握着他的小刀。可恰在此时，安娜大娘来了，说道：‘别理会他，他这会儿还睡着呢，虽然眼睛睁着。快快穿上衣裳，我的埃尔弗希尔德，随我出来，让他自个儿醒去。’我便依了她，心里并不真明白她的话。待我们出到院子草场上，安娜才把原委都告诉了我：原来那恶徒，在我睡去之后，向她出了个价钱，要买我，好把我妥妥当当、完完整整地带了走。他说，这事容易办到：因为我瞧得出你精通巫术，能把这少女掷入一场睡梦，不到定好的时辰她醒不过来；因为，他说，我要的是两样：先把她搂在我怀里，任我摆布，随后再把她带回我家里去，随她愿意不愿意。‘当时，’安娜说，‘我不肯立刻一口回绝他，因为我要教我那只狐狸稳稳落进陷阱里；我便含糊支吾，说他若不弄清楚这买卖究竟值多少，只怕过后要懊悔；长话短说，我这样撺掇他一阵又拽住他一阵，拽住他一阵又撺掇他一阵，末了他终于脱下外衣，一手攥住那柄光杆小刀，一手按上了床门。我的乖，我早同你这扇门相熟了，自有法子教它在好些事情上依我的意；我一见货郎的手搭上那门边，便对它念了几句话，自家上床去了，因为我知道，那歹徒的手休想离开门板，脚休想离开地板，直到我给他指定的时辰；而我也把你送入了沉睡，直睡到你方才醒来的那一刻。’‘那如今我们怎么办呢？’我说。安娜道：‘我们便守在这小树林里：板上有肉留给客人，他的衣裳也不难寻见，他的马匹行头褡裢在哪里，他自家知道。我料他既不热心同你道别，也不热心同我道别；因为教他攥住剑柄，去对付哪怕一个老老婆子同一个年轻姑娘，他还没有这份胆量。’于是我们便进了那小树林；我告诉过你，它就在我们房后，相去不过一箭之地。我们在那里直卧到过午不久，听见不远处有马行的声音。我们便爬到林子最边上，悄悄往外张望，不多时就见我们那位货郎带着褡裢和一切行头，策马往西去了，脸上一副憔悴愁苦；安娜见了，刻毒地咧嘴一笑，我自己也没能全忍住笑。可你怎么不笑呢，奥斯伯恩？”他跳起身来，狠狠地喊道：“但愿我当时在场，一剑劈开他的脑壳！为了比这轻的因由，我杀过的汉子还更了得呢。”

随后两人静默了一会，她坐在那里深情地望着他，末了才开口道：“心肝儿，你可是恼了我，讲了这段故事？”


===Original===

“Nay, nay,” he said; “how might I live save thou told me everything that befell thee? Yet I must tell thee that I well-nigh wish I had not heard this one; for there thou dwellest, with none other to ward thee than a carline stricken in years; and though I wot well from all thou hast said of her, and this last tale in special, that she has mickle might in her, yet she cannot be always with thee, nor belike ever thinking of thee. God forbid, sweetheart, that I should speak to thee in the tongue of the courts and the great houses and lords’ palaces, whenas for a fashion of talking they say of their lemans, and they not always nor often exceeding fair, that they be jewels beyond all price, whom an host of men were not enough to ward. But this I will say,” and he blushed very red at the word, “that thou art so lovely and so dear that thy man, thy love, and the stout and good friends who love him, were not over many for thy guarding even in this lonely place. And with all that I can be of no more use thereto than if I were a wooden man.”

She stood up also, and he saw that the tears ran over her cheeks, and he stretched out his arms to her; but she said: “Grieve not too much, my friend; and know, as thou saidst e’en now of thyself, that these tears are not wholly for sorrow of thy grief, but O! so much and so much for joy of thy kindness. And one thing I must tell thee, that if I am alone in my house, I am at least alone with a friend, and one who loves me. And this shall come of it, that now every day I shall come down to the tryst, for the carline will hinder me in no way. But I know that oft thou wilt come to meet me: yet belike often thou wilt not, because I wot how thou hast work to do and things wherein folk call for thee to serve them. So any day if thou come not it shall be well, and if thou come it shall be better.”

Now at last he seemed to be learning the full sweetness of her. But she held up her band and said: “Now I bid thee tarry no longer, but fall to and tell me the tale of thy deeds; for soon shall the short autumn day be waning, and the moment of parting shall steal upon us ere we be ware.” Even so he did now; but at first, to say sooth, he made but a poor minstrel, so much his mind was turned unto what she had been telling him; but after a while his scaldship quickened him, and he told her much in a manner like life, so that she might see the tidings going on before her. And he held her enwrapped in his tale till the dark and the dusk began to rise up over the earth, and then for that time they parted, and there was to be more of the war of Eastcheaping on the day after tomorrow.

So went Osberne home to Wethermel, and at first it seemed to him as if this first meeting after so long a while had scarce been so good as he had looked for; for both his longing to be close to his love, and the fear which had arisen in him as to the stealing of her, were somewhat of a weight on his heart. But after a little, when he had first been among folk and then alone, all that doubt and trouble melted away in the remembrance of her, as she had been really standing before his eyes, and there was now little pain and much sweetness in the longing wherewith he longed for her.

So on the said day appointed he went to meet her, smiling and happy and fresh as a rose; and she was of like mien, and when they faced each other she smote her palms together as in the old childish time, and cried out: “Ah! now the warrior is all ready, and the minstrel is stuffed full of his tale, and happy shall be the hour.” And even so it was.

===Chinese===

“不，不，”他说；“你若不把遭际的桩桩件件都告诉我，我怎能活得下去？只是我须对你说，这一段我几乎宁愿不曾听见；因为你住在那里，护着你的，除了一个上了年纪的老婆子，再无旁人；虽说从你讲她的一切、尤其是方才这段故事里，我深知她身负大神通，可她不能时时刻刻守着你，只怕也未必时时刻刻把你放在心上。上天不容，心肝儿，我拿宫廷和大宅门、爵爷府邸里的言语来同你说话——那不过是他们的一种谈吐风气：说起自家的情妇来——哪怕她们并非总是、也难得美到十分——也要说成无价的珍宝，说成千军万马也护卫不过来的宝物。可是这句话我定要说，”说到这里，他脸涨得通红，“你是这般可爱、这般珍贵，你的男人、你的爱人，连同爱他的那些刚勇良善的朋友，纵然都来护卫你，在这荒僻之地也不为多。可我呢，纵有一身本领，在这上头却抵不得一个木头人。”

她也站起身来，他看见泪水顺着她的双颊流下来，便向她张开双臂；她却说道：“莫要太过忧伤，我的朋友；也请你相信，正如你方才说自家那样，这些泪不全是为你的悲伤而淌，却是，啊！千遍万遍地为你这片恩情欢喜。还有一件事我须告诉你：我在家中虽是孤身一人，好歹也是同一个朋友、同一个爱我的人守在一处。由此还生出一样好处：往后我要日日下到相会之处来，那老婆子必不拦我。我也知道你会常常来会我：可你也常常来不得，因为我知道你有你的事业，有众人唤你去效劳的事。所以哪一日你来了自然更好，你不来也不打紧。”

到了这时，他才算真正领尝了她全部的甜美。可她举起手来，说道：“如今我命你休再耽搁，这就开讲你功业的故事；因为这秋日短促，转眼便要向晚，分手的时辰会在咱们不备之际悄悄袭来。”他便当真讲了；只是起初，说实话，他这乐师做得实在蹩脚，他满心还萦绕着她方才所讲的事；过了一会，他那吟游诗人的才情提掣了他，他讲得栩栩如生，教那些事迹仿佛在她眼前一一演出。他把她牢牢裹在他的故事里，直到黑暗与暮色开始从大地上腾起，两人才暂且分手；后天，还有更多东集的战事可讲呢。

奥斯伯恩就这样回了韦瑟梅尔；起初他只觉得，这场久别之后的初会，似乎竟不及他先前指望的那般美好；因为渴念亲近爱人的心，同那因她险遭劫夺而生的忧惧，都在他心头压上了些分量。可过了不多时，他先在人丛里待了一阵，又独自待了一阵，那一切疑惧烦忧便都在对她的忆念中消融了——她仿佛真真切切立在眼前；于是那思念里，痛苦少了，甘甜多了。

到了约定的那一日，他去会她，满面笑容，欢欢喜喜，鲜妍得像一朵玫瑰；她也是同样的神采。两人对面相逢，她像儿时那样合掌一击，高声叫道：“啊！这会儿武士一切停当，乐师也满肚子装着他的故事，这一个时辰必定快活了。”事实也正如此。

## XXXI / 三十一

===Original===

They Meet Through Autumn and Winter

So many a time they met that autumn, and Elfhild would ever be asking him some boon; as the next time after this, it was the gifts which he had brought for her from the Cheaping; for in thinking of her he had clean forgotten them. So then was the merry time in talking of them, and shooting and hurling of them over, and the donning of them, and the talking of them again. Another time she prayed him to come clad in that goodly armour of the spoils of Deepdale, and he could no less than yeasay her, and there he was on the trysting-day, striding by the riverbank in the sun, like an heap of glittering ice hurrying before the river when the thaw is warm and the sun shining bright at Candlemas. And over that also went many pretty plays, as taking the pieces off, and naming them, and doing them on again, and the like.

So wore the days into winter, and yet the two saw each other full often even through the frost and snow and ill weather. And when the spring came, then it was dear to them indeed. And by that time had Osberne’s fears about the stealing of Elfhild much worn off; though it is to be said that exceeding oft his heart was weary and sore with the longing to hold her in his arms. Yet the most of these times he kept his grief in his own heart; so much as Elfhild was moved when it brake forth from him, and she might, so to say, see the torments of him before her very eyes. Indeed on one time, when for a long while she might not comfort him, she told him that this was almost as bad as seeing him laid a-dying before her.

But kind and dear they were to each other, and there was nought in them that was not lovely in those first days of their manhood.

===Chinese===

他们自秋徂冬的相会

那个秋天，他们相会了一回又一回，埃尔弗希尔德总有些恩典要向他讨：譬如紧接下来的那一次，她讨的便是他从东集给她捎来的那些礼物——只因他一心念着她，竟把礼物忘了个干净。于是便有一段快活时光：先把这些礼物谈论一番，再将它们或射或掷地送过河去，穿戴起来，接着又谈论一番。又有一回，她求他穿戴起那副得自深谷战利品的华美甲胄前来；他不能不点头应承她。于是到了相会之期那一天，他便在太阳底下沿着河岸昂然阔步，恰似圣烛节前后天气回暖、日光大亮之时，河面上一大团晶莹的浮冰顺流奔涌而去。在这一副甲胄上也有许多有趣的戏耍：把甲件一件件卸下，说出它们的名目，再一一穿戴上，诸如此类。

如此，日子便渐渐磨进了冬天；然而纵然霜冻雨雪、天气恶劣，两人依然频频相见。待到春天来临，那时节于他们才真是亲爱无比。到那时候，奥斯伯恩唯恐埃尔弗希尔德遭人劫夺的忧惧已消磨大半；虽说也须讲一句，他心里渴望将她拥入怀中，那份情思常常熬得他心头又疲又痛。然而这些时候，他大都将苦楚闷在自己心底；以致一旦迸发出来，埃尔弗希尔德便大为动容，简直可以说，他备受煎熬的情状就摆在她眼前。的确有一回，她好半天也宽慰不了他，便对他说，这几乎同眼看着他奄奄一息躺在自己面前一般难当。

只是两人彼此恩爱亲厚，在那成人之初的日子里，他们身上没有一处不是可爱的。

## XXXII / 三十二

===Original===

Foemen Among the Westdalers

But when the spring was worn into April there fell new tidings: for on a morning early came Stephen the Eater hurrying into the hall at Wethermel and cried aloud: “Bows, bows! Come afield all ye of this hall, and thou chiefly Osberne the Captain!”

Out then tumbled the stout men of Wethermel from shut-bed and hutch, and were presently armed, and Osberne was in his byrny and steel hood straightway, his bow in his hand and his quiver at his back.

They gathered about him and Stephen amidst of the hall, and then Osberne asks what is toward. “Great matter enough,” says Stephen. “Yet how is to help therein? There is unpeace in the Dale, but it has fallen on the Westerlings.”

===Chinese===

西岸谷民中的敌寇

然而春光渐渐磨进了四月，又有新的消息传来：一天清早，饕客斯蒂芬急匆匆奔进韦瑟梅尔的大厅，高声叫道：“拿弓！拿弓！这厅里的人通通下地去，尤其要请你奥斯伯恩统领出马！”

韦瑟梅尔的健儿们当即从箱床铺柜里翻身滚出，顷刻间披挂停当；奥斯伯恩随即穿上锁子甲、扣好钢盔，手中执弓，背上负着箭囊。

众人把他和斯蒂芬围在大厅当中，奥斯伯恩便问外头出了什么事。斯蒂芬道：“事情着实不小。可咱们如何搭得上手呢？谷里起了刀兵，只是这刀兵落在了西岸人头上。”

===Original===

Quoth Osberne, short and sharp: “Ye, Otter, Simon, Longdeer, Alison, take horse and ride straightway down the Dale and round to every stead, and bid men gather to the side of the Flood with bows and sling-spears and shot-weapons of all kinds, and that they stand not in knots and clumps, but drawn out in line, and space enough between each shooter. Bid them to leave not a shaft at home﻿—we may speedily make more﻿—but not to loose once till they have marked their man. Now hasten ye four! But ye others come after me at once, for we will go afoot for the saving of time and the steadying of the shooting.”

So they went toward the water, a dozen men all told, and all had bows and good store of shafts. And as they went, Osberne spake to those about him and said: “Spread out, and make little show of force, and show not your bows to the foemen, so that they may contemn us and venture the nearer to the bank. But shoot not till they defy us, lest we smite a peaceful man.” Now they were presently nigh enough to see the going of men on the further shore, and they were all riders. It was clear to see that they were aliens, men upon big horses clad in outlandish armour with bright steel headpieces; they bore long spears with light shafts, and a many of them had short horseman’s bows and quivers at their backs along with their targes.

Now as the men of Wethermel drew up to the water’s edge, a knot of the said aliens, about a score, came to them shouting and yelling, and there were within sight scattered about the fields some two hundreds in all. When they reined up by the Flood-side one of them, who seemed by the gold on his armour and weapons to be a chief, hove his spear aloft and brandished it, and fell to crying out in what seemed to be words; but since they knew not his Latin they gat no meaning from them, but he spake in a masterful and threatening voice. Then by Osberne’s bidding, Stephen, who stood anigh him, drew a white clout from his scrip, made it fast to his spear and held it aloft, to show that they would have parley. But for all answer the chieftain and his brake out a-laughing; and then the chieftain gat his spear by the midmost, and made as if he would cast at them; but the Flood there was over-wide for spear-shot. Then one of his folk unslung his shortbow and nocked a shaft, and turned to the chief as if asking leave, and the chief nodded him yeasay. Quoth Osberne hastily: “Stephen, cover thee! It will be thou. Then if he looses, we loose, for this is a foeman.”

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩言简声厉地吩咐道：“水獭、西蒙、长鹿、艾莉森，你们四人上马，即刻沿谷而下，挨个庄子跑一遍，吩咐众人带着弓箭、掷矛并各式射远兵器，聚集到洪流岸边；切莫成堆扎团，须得一线排开，射手与射手之间留足空当。吩咐他们一支箭也不要留在家里——箭杆咱们很快补得上——但在各自认准目标之前，一支也不许放。你们四个这就快去！其余的人立时随我来，咱们步行前往，一来省时候，二来放箭时脚下稳当。”

于是他们朝水边进发，总共十二人，人人都带着弓和充足的箭。一路行去，奥斯伯恩对身边的人说道：“散开来，别摆出多大阵仗，也别让敌人瞧见你们的弓，好教他们轻看我们，放胆逼近岸边。但他们不曾挑衅之前切莫放箭，免得误杀了并无敌意之人。”这时他们已走得够近，看得见对岸人马的动静了，来的全是骑者。一望便知是外来的寇仇：人人骑着高头大马，披着异样的甲胄，戴着亮闪闪的钢盔；他们拿着轻杆的长矛，其中许多还带着骑兵用的短弓，背上箭囊旁边又悬着盾牌。

韦瑟梅尔众人刚开到水边，那一伙外寇中便有约莫二十骑的一簇，呼喝叫嚣着朝他们逼来；放眼四野，散在各处田间的，统共约有两百人。他们在洪流岸边勒住马，其中一人甲胄兵器上金光灿然，瞧着像个头目，把长矛高举挥舞，随即扯开嗓子叫喊，喊的似乎也是言语；只是这边听不懂他的话，听不出个所以然来，唯觉得他声色俱厉，满是威吓。于是奥斯伯恩吩咐近旁的斯蒂芬从挎囊里抽出一幅白布，系上矛头高高举起，表示这边想要会谈。那头目和他的一伙却只报以一阵大笑；接着那头目把长矛攥住中段，作势要向这边投掷；无奈此处河面太宽，长矛掷不过来。于是他手下有一人卸下短弓，搭上一箭，回头望着头目，似在请示；头目点头应允。奥斯伯恩急忙道：“斯蒂芬，遮护住自己！这一箭多半冲你而来。他若放箭，咱们便齐放，须知这便是敌人了。”

===Original===

Even therewith the shaft flew, and Stephen turned it with his shield. Then the Wethermelers set up a shout and bent their bows, and Osberne loosed first, and the shaft smote the chieftain in the eye, and he fell dead off his horse: Stephen also put a shaft into the man who had shot at him, and three others of them fell withal at the first loose, besides three that were hurt. And the aliens liked the Wethermel breakfast so ill, that they turned their backs to the river at once and scuttled away into the field out of shot, yet not before they had lost two more men and three horses.

Osberne stayed his men there a little while to see if the foe would bring up others to go on with the game; but the aliens were over-wily for that, as it seemed; for they but gathered together, and turning all their heads down-dale fared on in one body.

As yet the Dalesmen had seen nought of any onset of their neighbours of the West, and sore troubled was Osberne when he fell to thinking that, as the robbers were wending, they must needs chop upon Hartshaw Knolls; so the best he could hope was that Elfhild might flee from her house to some other, or even, it might be, hide her in the wood, which she knew so inwardly.

===Chinese===

话音未落，那支箭已经飞来，斯蒂芬举盾把它磕开。韦瑟梅尔众人顿时发一声喊，纷纷弯弓；奥斯伯恩头一个放箭，一箭正中那头目的眼睛，他当场栽下马来死了：斯蒂芬也一箭结果了方才射他的那名敌兵；头一轮箭放罢，对面又倒了三人，另有三人带伤。外寇们对韦瑟梅尔这顿“早点”实在消受不起，当即背转身去，窜进田里逃出箭程之外；只是逃窜之前，又折了两人三马。

奥斯伯恩让部众在原地稍停片刻，看敌人会不会调人马来接着玩这场把戏；但外寇看来狡猾得紧，不肯再上这个当：他们只是聚拢起来，一齐掉头朝下游去，结成一股赶路了。

这时谷民还全然不见西岸邻人有什么迎击的动静。奥斯伯恩一想到那伙强盗此去的路径，势必撞上哈特肖丘，心里便烦恼万分；他至多只能指望埃尔弗希尔德或者弃家逃往别处庄舍，或者索性躲进林子里去——那片林子她了如指掌。

===Original===

Meanwhile he bade his men go quietly downstream on the river’s edge. Saith he: “If aught is to be done from this side, we shall presently have the folk from the lower steads drifting into us, and we should make a good band were it not for yonder wet dyke which the thieves have gotten them for a defence.”

So they fared on, and now and again some man of the lifters turned somewhat toward them to look on their demeanour, and whiles one would speed an arrow to them, but did no harm; at last, as they began to draw nigh the narrows above the Bight of the Cloven Knoll, a whole sort of the foemen came riverwards, but somewhat more than half held on the straight way down the Dale. Even therewith came to join the Wethermelers a many of the folk from the downward steads, stout fellows all, and well armed with shot-weapons.

And now there was nought for it but on both sides men were drifting toward the Bight of the Cloven Knoll, nor needeth words to tell of the anguish of Osberne’s heart and the fierce wrath of his spirit. When the aliens, who were thronging to the riverbank, saw how narrow the stream was growing, they set up a whoop and drew closer to the Eastdalers, and the more part of them got off their horses and marched along foot by foot with them, and they were now within shot of each other, so that the foemen stayed at whiles and shot them a shaft; and now they hurt divers of the Dalesmen, but Osberne would not suffer them to shoot back as yet.

===Chinese===

这时他吩咐部众沿着河岸悄悄往下游去。他说道：“若说这边能有什么作为，下流各庄的人很快便会陆续来投；若非那边隔着一条湿壕给强盗们做了凭仗，咱们本可结成一支像样的队伍。”

于是他们继续前行；盗伙中不时有人侧过身来，打量这边的举动，间或也有一两支箭朝这边射来，却不曾伤人。末了，当他们渐渐挨近裂丘湾上游的窄流处时，一大股敌寇转向河边而来，但也有一多半仍顺着谷中直路往下走去。恰在此时，下流各庄的许多人前来与韦瑟梅尔众人会合，个个都是好汉，带着齐全的射远兵器。

事到如今，别无他法，两岸的人马都渐渐往裂丘湾涌去；至于奥斯伯恩心中何等煎熬、胸中何等狂怒，也不用多费笔墨了。外寇们涌到河岸，见河道越来越窄，便发一声喊，向东岸谷民逼近过来；他们大半下了马，一步一步与这边齐头并进。这时双方已进入彼此箭程之内，外寇不时停步，朝这边放上一箭；谷民中已有几人受伤，但奥斯伯恩还不许他们回射。

===Original===

So came they within sight of the Dwarfs’ cave, and there were not a few of the Dalesmen who feared the place even in the turmoil of battle; and some deemed it might be unlucky to them; but others said that most like Osberne’s good luck would prevail over the evil will of the Dwarf-kind.

So when Osberne came to the trysting-place, he and his were fully two score men, and they of the stoutest; and he stood before them all on the very place where his feet had so often stayed for the comforting of his heart and the caressing of his love: there he stood, handling a heavy cast-spear.
