Even therewith the aliens poured on to the ness, howling like dogs, and on to Elfhild’s very standing-place. Before all his men came a chieftain of them, clad in armour wrought gaudily and decked all with gold and silver, and with a great red horsetail streaming from his helm. He hove up his hand and poised a great spear, but in that nick of time Osberne cast his weapon suddenly, with a fierce shout, and all about him and behind him he heard the loose of the Dalesmen’s bows. Sooth to say, as he cast, he almost looked to see all that turmoil clear away as a dream, and that he should see Elfhild falling with the spear in her breast. But nought it befell: the gold-decked chieftain took the spear under his arm, and he and his spear fell over clashing and clattering down into the gulfs of the green water, and many of the strong-thieves fell before the shaft-storm of the Dalesmen; but therewith the foemen shot also, and some of the Dalesmen were slain and divers hurt, but that abated their hearts no jot. But Osberne took twelve shafts from out his quiver and nocked them one after the other, and every time he loosed a man’s life went away on the arrow-point; but bitter was his wrath and his grief that he might not slay them all and deliver his love. Many a shaft smote him, but the more part of them fell off scatheless from the rings of Hardcastle’s loom. Now were many of the thieves slain; yet so fierce and eager were they, that the more part would not draw aback, nay, some were so hungry for that cruel slaughter of them that they heeded not the sundering of the Flood, but rushed on as if there were nought between them, and fell over into the boil of waters and were lost in the bottomless depths.

===Chinese===

不久他们便望得见矮人岩洞了；即便在酣战的纷乱之中，谷民中也颇有惧怕那地方的；有人觉得它未必吉利，也有人说，多半还是奥斯伯恩的好运亨通，压得过矮人一族的恶意。

待奥斯伯恩赶到那相会之处时，他和部众已足足有四十人，且都是最矫健的汉子；他立在众人之前，正站在他双脚曾无数次驻留的那块地方——为宽慰心怀、为温存爱意而驻留的地方：他便立在那里，手里掂着一杆沉重的投枪。

恰在此时，外寇们嚎叫着如群犬一般涌上海岬，涌上埃尔弗希尔德素日站立的地方。当先一员头目走在全军之前，身披纹彩斑斓的甲胄，遍缀金银，盔上一束大红马尾迎风招展。他扬起手臂，一杆大矛蓄势欲掷；就在这电光石火的一瞬，奥斯伯恩猛然奋力掷出手中的投枪，随之一声怒吼，前后左右立时响起谷民弓弦齐放的声响。说句实话，掷出那一枪时，他几乎当真以为这一场纷乱会如梦一般消散，他会看见埃尔弗希尔德胸口带着矛头倒下去。然而什么也没有发生：那金装头目被长矛贯入腋下，连人带矛哐啷作响地栽进绿水的深渊里；剧盗们也有许多倒在谷民的箭雨之下。但外寇们也随即放箭，谷民战死了几个，伤了几个，可这丝毫没有挫了他们的心气。奥斯伯恩从箭囊里抽出十二支箭，一支接一支搭上弦，每放一箭，便有一条性命随箭头而去；然而他怒极痛极，恨不能把敌人杀个精光，救出他的爱人。多少支箭射中他，但大半都从哈德卡斯尔那副锁环甲的环圈上滑落，不曾伤他分毫。这时盗众已被射杀许多；然而他们凶悍狂热之极，大半竟不肯后退，更有一些人杀红了眼，急欲对这边的人痛下屠戮，竟把分离洪流之隔全不放在心上，只顾往前冲，仿佛中间空无一物，纷纷跌进翻滚的激流里，葬身于无底的深渊。

===Original===

So fared the battle, and the ranks of the Dalesmen began to thin; but Osberne had no thought of going back a foot’s length, and his men were so valiant that they deemed nought evil save the sundering of the Flood. Osberne was hurt in three places, but not sorely; but Stephen bore a shaft in his side, yet he stood upon his feet and shot no less valiantly than erst.

But now all of a sudden the raging throng before them had some new goings-on in it and began to sidle landward, and therewithal beyond them rose a great shout, and therein the Eastdalers knew the voice of their kinsmen, and they shouted all together in answer as they plied the bow, and the strong-thieves turned about and ran yelling and cursing toward the landward and the southwest, for the Westdalers were upon them with spear and axe and sword.

That was the end of the shot-stour, and the aliens came never again that tide under the shafts of the Eastdalers. But betwixt the kenning of their dead and the tending of their hurt folk, they stood gazing out anxiously over the field, if they might but see how the battle of handy-strokes had gone, and by seeming right hard it had been; but in a while they saw the aliens thrust back and edging away towards their horses, which they had left standing out of bowshot not far from the Bight of the Cloven Knoll. The Westdalers were following on, smiting great strokes, but not so as to be mingled up with them; nor did they seem as if they would will to hinder them if they should get on their horses and ride away; and even they did so presently, and the Dalesmen saw them never again.

===Chinese===

战斗便这样进行下去，谷民的行伍渐渐稀疏了；但奥斯伯恩全无后退一步的念头，他的部众也个个奋勇，觉得天下再没有可恨的事，唯独这分离洪流之隔可恨。奥斯伯恩身上三处带伤，却不沉重；斯蒂芬肋上中了一箭，却仍挺立不倒，放箭之勇不减先前。

可是正在此时，眼前那狂怒的敌群忽然起了新的骚动，开始向陆地一侧悄悄挪移；紧接着敌群后面爆起一片大喊，东岸谷民从那喊声里听出了自己族人的声音，便一面弯弓连射，一面齐声呼喊应和；剧盗们掉头就跑，叫骂着朝陆地和西南方向溃去——西岸谷民已经挺着矛、斧、长剑杀奔他们来了。

箭斗到此便算完了，这一阵里外寇再没有回到东岸谷民的箭下来。但东岸的人在清点阵亡、照料伤者之余，都焦灼地朝野外眺望，巴望能看出白刃之战的胜负；看那光景，厮杀得着实惨烈。不多时，他们便望见外寇被杀得步步后退，渐渐挪向他们的马匹那边——那些马就留置在离裂丘湾不远的箭程之外。西岸谷民随后追赶，大刀阔斧地砍杀，却并不与他们搅作一团；看那意思，纵使外寇上马逃走，他们也未必存心阻拦；外寇果然随即上马去了，谷民从此再没有见过他们。

## XXXIII / 三十三

===Original===

Osberne Seeks Tidings of Elfhild

Now when this stour was all over, and the men of the East Dale were still standing together (not very triumphantly, because of their slain) on the east side of the Cloven Knoll, the Westdalers came toward them treading the field of dead from which the Flood sundered them. As aforesaid, neither the East nor the West had heretofore been much wont to resort to that place because of their dread of the Dwarfs who dwelt in the cave above the whirlpool; but now the passion of battle, and the sorrow for the dead, and the perplexity of the harrying had swept all that out of their minds a while. So the chiefs of the Westdalers stood among the corpses of the aliens on the crown of the ness where Elfhild was wont to stand, and fell to talking with their brethren of the East; and the man who took up the word for them all was Wulfstan of Coldburne, a stead of the lower West Dale. And he fell to praising the good help which the Eastdalers had given them by cleaving so manfully to the shot-stour, which he said had been their deliverance; for delivered they looked to be. “Albeit,” says he, “they whom ye dealt with so manfully, and whom ye have now put to the road, be not the whole host of them, whereas while one moiety turned aside to the shooting, the other went on down the Dale and somewhat away from the Flood; and we left our brethren marching against them, and must turn presently to their helping lest they be outnumbered by the strong-thieves. Yea, and already we fear lest these devils have wasted certain of our steads which would lie on their road, before our folk might fall in with them. And now give us leave! but we pray that ye may live hale and happy for the help ye have given us; and thou in special, Osberne Wulfgrimsson, whom we know, and the tales of thee.”

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩打探埃尔弗希尔德的消息

这一场厮杀方才了结，东谷的人还聚立在裂丘东侧（因了阵亡的弟兄，神色间并无多少得胜之意），西岸谷民便踏着那片尸骸枕藉的战场，朝他们走来——先前正是分离洪流把这战场隔在两岸之间的。如前所述，无论东岸还是西岸的人，素来都因惧怕住在漩涡上方岩洞里的矮人，不大惯到那地方去；可是这时鏖战的血勇、对阵亡者的哀恸、加上这场劫掠留在心头的惶乱，一时把那些忌惮全扫出了脑海。于是西岸谷民的头领们就立在外寇的尸首中间，立在埃尔弗希尔德素常站立的海岬顶上，同东岸的同胞叙谈起来；替众人开口代言的，是科德本的伍尔夫斯坦——科德本是西谷下流的一处田庄。他便盛赞东岸谷民坚守箭斗、勇武不退，帮了他们这样的大忙；他说，正是这一着解救了他们，因为看那光景，他们眼见得就要脱险了。“然而，”他说道，“你们这般勇猛对付、又赶得上路的这伙人，并非他们的全数：一半人折向河岸来赴箭阵，另一半却顺着谷地一路下行，渐渐离了洪流；我们把自己的同胞留在那里迎击他们，此刻就须赶去接应，免得他们被剧盗以众凌寡。何况我们已然担心，这些魔鬼在我们的人追上之前，怕已把落在他们路上的几处田庄糟蹋了。如今且容我们告辞！惟愿你们因了这番援手，从此康健欢喜；尤其是你，奥斯伯恩·伍尔夫格里姆森，我们是识得你的，还有关于你的那些传闻呢。”

===Original===

But as he was on the point of turning away, Osberne said in a loud shrill voice: “Abide, master, and tell me one thing, to wit, the names of the steads which the thieves have wasted.” Said Wulfstan: “I may not, because I know not: hereabout it is thin of dwellings; ’tis a five miles ere ye shall happen on a good homestead, Longryggs to wit: here is nought but a little stead, fallen to be a cot, wherein dwell none save two women, one old and one young. It is not like that the thieves would have stayed for so little a thing. Farewell; if the battle goes handily with us ye shall have tidings thereof tomorrow if ye will come down hither; or a little lower down maybe, lest the Dwarfs begrudge us.”

And therewith he turned and went toward the place where they deemed they should find the battle. As for the Eastdalers, they might tarry no more in looking to their wounded folk; and a many were hurt so grievously, that they had to be borne home in the four corners; of whom was Stephen the Eater, and he lay long sick, but in the long last, and it was a two months, was healed as well as ever he was. A half score were sore hurt like to this; but of them who might carry their grief home on their own legs were at least a score and six; but thirteen were slain outright. And these it was deemed good, after due thought taken, to lay them in earth in the field but a little way from the Bight of the Cloven Knoll; and the place where they are laid, with plenteous earth heaped over them, has ever since been called Shooters’ Knowe.

===Chinese===

不料他正待抽身离去，奥斯伯恩却提高了嗓音，尖声叫道：“且慢，老丈，请告诉我一件事：强盗们糟蹋的都有哪几处田庄，务请把名目见告。”伍尔夫斯坦道：“我说不上来，因为我并不知道：这一带人烟稀少；走出五英里，才碰得着一处像样的庄舍，唤作朗里格斯；此间不过一座小小的庄子，败落得同茅屋相似，里头住着两个女人，一老一少，再无旁人。强盗们未必会为这么一点儿东西停留。再会了；倘若战事顺手，明天你们肯下到这里来，便可听到消息；或者再往下游一点儿的地方也使得，免得矮人们忌恨我们近了他们的地界。”

说罢他转过身，朝着他们料想能寻见战事的地方去了。至于东岸谷民，他们照料伤者，再不能多作耽搁；有许多人伤得极重，只得用四角担架抬回家去；饕客斯蒂芬便在其中，他缠绵病榻许久，但到末了——前后足足两个月——竟痊愈如初，康健一如往日。这般重伤的共有十人；至于还能凭自己的双腿把伤痛驮回家的，至少有二十六人；但当场殒命的，有十三人。众人经过一番斟酌，觉得最好就把他们安葬在战场上，离裂丘湾不远的地方；那处葬所上头堆着厚厚的封土，从那时起便一直唤作射手冈。

## XXXIV / 三十四

===Original===

Osberne Sorrows for the Loss of Elfhild

Now some while before men were boun to depart to their own homes, the sound of fresh battle was borne to them on the southwest; so, saving those who must needs go tend the hurt on their way home, they might not tear themselves away from that field of deed; and in special Osberne, who had been busy enough in kenning the dead and wounded of his folk while need was, came back to the verge of the Flood, where so oft he had stood in love and joy, and stood there a long while, scarce moving, with a shaft in his fingers and his bended bow in his fist, his brows knit, his eyes staring out over the western field. It was two hours after noon when the Westdalers turned to stir up the battle again. And then was an hour ere the clamour of the fight came down thither, and two hours yet it endured and was in all men’s ears; and then it died away, and the East men began to wander off from the watching-place, wending this way and that, and the autumn day fell to wane, and soon there were none left save Osberne and a half dozen of the men of Wethermel. And one or another of them plucked him by the sleeve and bade him come home with them, since the day was done, and the battle would not quicken again, and the Westdalers had overmuch on their hands to bear them any tidings till the morrow was a new day. At first he heeded them nought, but in the end he turned on them with an angry eye, yet spake mildly, and bade them get them home and eat and sleep. “But leave me here,” quoth he, “that I may watch a while lest aught of new befalleth; and I will come to Wethermel when my heart will suffer me.” So they departed and left him; and there he stood, till himseemed he had been there a long, long time. Night grew black about him, and silence fell on the cloven plain of the Dale, save that below him the speech of the eddies seemed to grow greater as other voices failed. Then arose the wind, and went through the long grass and talked in the crannies of the rock-wall of the Flood as the waters spake below; and none came anear, nor might he hearken any foot of man, only far-off voices from the steads of a barking dog or crowing cock or lowing cow.

At last, when the night was beginning to change amidst the depth of the darkness, himseemed he heard somewhat drawing anigh and coming up the bent on the western side, and he wotted not but it might be the unshod feed of men, and he lightly asked himself if the ghosts of the dead made any sound with their feet as they trod the puddled earth where a many had trodden before them; and so wild was his heart grown now, that he thought it no great marvel if those that they had laid to earth there should stand up and come before him in the night watches. Then he nocked an arrow on his bowstring and handled his weapon, but could not make up his mind to shoot lest the bow-draft should pierce the quiet and rouse up inextinguishable shrieks and moans; and even therewith, over those paddling feet, he seemed to hear a voice beginning to cry, and he thought within himself: Now, now it is on the way, and presently the air shall be full of it; and will it kindle fire in the air?

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩为失埃尔弗希尔德而哀恸

且说众人束装欲归各自家宅之前不久，西南方向又传来新起战事的声息；于是除却那些必须顺路照护伤者还家的人，谁也割舍不下这片鏖战之地；尤其是奥斯伯恩——方才事急之时，他曾忙着查验部众中的死者与伤者，如今便回到洪流的岸边，那往日他多少次怀着爱与欢喜伫立的地方——久久站着，几乎一动不动：指间捻着一支箭，拳中攥着弯弓，眉头紧锁，双眼直瞪着西面的旷野。午后两个钟头，西岸谷民重整旗鼓，再启战端。此后又过一个钟头，厮杀的喧嚣才传到这边，又延续了两个钟头，声声在众人耳中；随后渐渐止息，东岸人开始从瞭望处走散，三三两两各奔一方，秋日西沉，不多时除了奥斯伯恩和五六个韦瑟梅尔人，再无一人留下。他们中这个那个的便来扯他的衣袖，劝他随大伙儿回家，说天已晚了，战事不会再起，西岸谷民手头的事太多，不到明日新的一天，断然捎不得什么消息来。起先他全不理会，末了才瞪了他们一眼，说出话来却还温和，吩咐他们各自回家吃饭睡觉去。“只是把我留在此地吧，”他说，“让我再守望一阵，防有什么新的变故发生；等我的心受得住了，我自会回韦瑟梅尔来。”于是他们动身离去，撇下了他；他便立在那里，直觉得自己已在那儿站了很久很久。夜色在他四围黑透，寂静落在谷中这被劈开的平原上，唯有脚下的旋涡絮语，仿佛旁的声音一歇，它反倒愈发响亮起来。接着起了风，穿过长草沙沙作响，又在洪流石壁的罅隙间低语，恰似河水在下面说话；无人走近来，他也听不见半点人的脚步声，只有远处庄舍那边传来的犬吠、鸡鸣与牛哞。

终于，当黑夜正自深沉至极处开始转换之际，他恍惚听得有什么东西正在临近，从西面攀上草坡而来；他摸不清那会不会是人赤着脚的脚步，还淡淡自问：死者的鬼魂脚踏那片烂泥地时，可会出声么——那泥地上先前已有许多人在前头踏过；此刻他的心已狂乱至此，竟觉得倘若他们方才葬在那里的那些人站起身来，趁着夜更走到他面前，也算不得什么了不得的奇事。于是他把一支箭搭上弓弦，把稳手中的兵器，却横不下心来放箭，唯恐这一箭的弓风刺破寂静，激起那浇不灭的哀号与呻吟；恰在此时，隔着那啪嗒作响的脚步，他仿佛听见一个声音开始叫喊起来，心中暗想：来了，来了，它已在路上，转眼间空气便要被它充满；它会在空中燃起火来么？

===Original===

But at that point of time the voice sounded louder and was in two or three places, and even amidst its wildness the familiar sound smote to his heart, for it was but the bleating of sheep, and now all the bent over against him was alive with it. And of a sudden he was come to himself and wotted what it was, that it was Elfhild’s sheep, and that they had been loosed or thrust out from their folds and had wandered up there in the dark where so oft she had led them before. And now the mere bitterness of grief took the place of his wildness, and he let his bow and arrow drop to earth, and cast himself down on to the trodden ground and buried his face in his hands and moaned, and speedily the images of his life to come and the sorrow he must face passed through his soul, for he knew that she was gone, and either slain or carried away to where he should never hear of her or see her again.

At last, that his grief and wanhope might not rend his heart and slay him then and there, and lest all the deeds whereto he was fated should be spoiled and undone, self-pity fell upon him with the sweet remembrance of his love, and loosed the well of his tears, and he wept and wept, and might not be satiated of his mourning a long while. But when the night was yet dark and no sign of dawn in the sky, and, might he have seen it, the southwest was driving the rack low adown along the earth, he rose up slowly and gat his bow and arrows into his hands, and weakly and stiffly, like a man who hath been long sick, he fell to going along the riverside toward Wethermel, and his feet knew the way though his eyes might see it not. And as he went, with the wind whistling about his ears and the picture of Wethermel before his eyes, he found that life was come again to him, and he was beginning to think about what he should be doing to win some way back to the love that had been rent from him. Ever and anon, forsooth, as he was amidst such thoughts, the tears brake out from his eyes again, but still now he could refrain them better and better after each outburst, and he had no more wildness as erst, as if he were out of the world and drifting he knew not whither or why; but now he knew which was himself, and which was grief and pain.

It was but just the grey of the morning when he crept into the hall at Wethermel, and found his bed and cast himself thereon, and, all undone by weariness, fell asleep at once.

===Chinese===

可正在那一刻，那声音响得更亮了，而且在两三处地方一齐响起；纵然其声狂乱，这熟悉的声响却直击他的心，原来那不过是羊儿的咩叫，此刻他对面整面草坡都因它而活了过来。蓦地他清醒过来，明白了是怎么回事：那是埃尔弗希尔德的羊，它们被放出圈来、或是被人赶出了圈，在黑暗里游荡上了这面坡——往日她多少次在前头引着它们来此。于是纯粹的悲苦取代了他的狂乱；他任凭弓箭脱手落地，扑倒在踏烂的泥地上，把脸埋进双手里呻吟起来；日后生涯的种种图景和他必将面对的哀伤，倏忽间接连掠过他的灵魂，因为他知道她已经去了：不是被杀，便是被掳到了一个他再听不见她音讯、再见不到她容颜的地方。

末了，为免这悲恸与绝望撕裂他的心、当场便结果了他，也为免他命里注定的种种功业就此败坏落空，自怜之情携着对爱人的甜美回忆降落在他身上，打开了他泪水的泉眼；他哭了又哭，许久许久，哀思不得餍足。但当夜色仍黑、天上尚无破晓之象时——他若看得见的话，西南风正驱着乱云贴地疾行——他缓缓起身，将弓箭拿在手中，软弱而僵硬，像一个久病之人，沿着河边朝韦瑟梅尔走去；他的眼睛虽看不见路，脚却认得。一路行去，风在耳边呼啸，韦瑟梅尔的影像浮在眼前，他发觉生命又回到了自己身上，开始思量该做些什么，好寻出一条路来，回到那份被生生夺走的爱那里去。诚然，每当他沉浸在这类思绪之中，眼泪还会不时从他眼中夺眶而出；但如今每经一番倾泻，他便愈能克制得住；他不再像先前那样狂乱，仿佛身在世外，不知飘向何处、又为何而飘；如今他分得清哪一个是自己，哪一个是悲恸与痛苦了。

他爬进韦瑟梅尔的大厅时，天色才蒙蒙亮；他寻着自己的床，一头栽倒上去，疲惫得浑身散了架一般，霎时便睡着了。

===Original===

He awoke with the house astir about him, and arose and sat down to eat with the others, and was no harsher of speech than his wont, albeit he looked stark and stern; and to some it seemed as if he had aged ten years since yestermorn, and they deemed that the death of the folk lay heavy on him, as was like to be, and they said as few words to him as might be, for his grief seemed aweful to them. But when they had eaten he bade three of his men come with him down the water to seek tidings of the Westdalers. So they went together, and a little below the Bight of the Cloven Knoll, out of earshot of the Dwarf-folk, they met with others from the lower steads come upon the same errand; and the Westdalers were just come to the waterside with Wulfstan for their spokesman, who forsooth had gotten some scratches from the war-beast, so that his head and his arm were bandaged. Now he spake: “Hail to you, stout-hearts of the East! Ye may deem that we prevailed in the second battle yesterday, or ye would scarce have seen us here this morn. Now the battle was foughten all about the garth and the house of Longryggs, which the strong-thieves had fallen on to waste, but the womenfolk of the stead had saved their lives by flight, and the carles thereof were in our company fighting valiantly. So whatever is lost was lost in open battle, wherein two score and six of our best men have changed their lives; but as for the strong-thieves, besides them who fell in your shot-stour, we have buried over seven-score; and the rest are fled away, many of them grievously hurt. Wherefore, friends, we have won a great victory: God and his hallows keep us for any more such!” And it seemed as if the goodman were weeping-ripe, whereof none marvelled. But Osberne spake, and the sound of his own voice seemed strange unto him: “Tell me, goodman, have ye lost nought by the murder of men whenas the strong-thieves fell on some stead?”

“Nay,” said Wulfstan, “the thieves have wasted no other stead save Longryggs, whereas, as I have said, the folks escaped the murder, and this little house which is hard hereby of Hartshaw Knolls. There forsooth the two women be missing, but no slain body of carle or quean have we found, nought of slaughter save the slaughter of kine and sheep. And I must tell you that this morning our folk sought all about heedfully, yea, and looked into every thicket and nook of the wood.”

“Belike,” quoth Osberne, “they will have carried off the two women?” Said Wulfstan: “I fear it may well be so.”

===Chinese===

他醒来时，屋中人已在四下忙碌；他起身同众人坐下用饭，言语并不比平日严厉，只是神色冷峻森严；在有些人看来，他仿佛自昨日清晨起老了十岁。众人以为族人之死沉沉压在他心头，这原也在情理之中，便都尽量少同他说话，因为他的悲恸在他们眼里近乎可畏。饭罢，他吩咐三名手下随他沿河而下，去探听西岸谷民的消息。于是一同行去，在裂丘湾下游不远、矮人们听不见的地方，他们遇见了下边各庄舍为同一事由而来的人；西岸谷民也刚到水边，以伍尔夫斯坦为代言人——他确实被那头战兽抓伤了几处，头上和臂上都缠着绷带。他便说道：“东岸的健儿们，向你们致敬！你们尽可想见我们昨日第二阵是打赢了，不然今早你们也难在这儿见到我们。这一仗是在朗里格斯的庄围和房舍左近打的，剧盗们原是扑向那庄子要行洗劫，庄中妇人都已逃命脱身，庄里的汉子们则在咱们队中奋勇厮杀。所以凡有所失，都是在明阵仗的野战里失去的，咱们四十六名最好的儿郎就此换了性命；至于剧盗，除了倒在你们箭斗里的，咱们又埋了一百四十多个；其余的都逃散了，其中许多伤得极重。所以，朋友们，咱们打了一场大胜仗：愿上帝和他的诸圣保佑咱们，再别叫咱们打这样的胜仗！”这位庄主瞧着已到了眼泪要夺眶而出的地步，无人觉得奇怪。奥斯伯恩开口了，自己的嗓音在自己听来都觉陌生：“告诉我，庄主，剧盗们扑向哪座庄舍杀人行凶的时候，你们可曾折损了什么？”

伍尔夫斯坦道：“不曾。剧盗们除了朗里格斯——那庄的人如我所说已逃过了凶刃——和哈特肖丘近旁这座小屋，没祸害别的庄子。那处确确实实少了两个女人，可我们没寻见一具汉子或女子的尸首，除了宰杀的牛羊，再无别样杀戮的痕迹。我还须告诉你，今早我们的人四下里仔细搜寻过，当真连林子里每一处灌木丛和角落都探看过了。”

奥斯伯恩道：“那两个女人，多半是被掳走了？”伍尔夫斯坦道：“我怕多半正是如此。”

===Original===

Said Osberne: “Well, this loss of two women, whom maybe ye shall find again, is but little: but grievous is the man-fall of the battles. Yet not soon meseem shall reivers fall upon West Dale now they have learned the valour of the folk thereof. Heried be the Lord God that the folk yet liveth and shall live!”

He spake measuredly and in a loud voice, so that all heard, and they cheered his speech with deep and strong voice; but they who stood nighest unto Osberne say that his face was stern and very pale as he spake; and it seemed to them that had Boardcleaver been naked on the West side in that stour yet more of the strong-thieves had fallen.

Now they parted, and Osberne and his Wethermelers went home, and the other Eastdalers also, each to his place. But as to the Westdalers, they fell to, and drew away the slain thieves from the field of deed, for that they feared the begrudging of the Dwarfs, and they laid them in earth hard by where they had stood to have that converse with them of the East; and they raised a great howe over them, and it is called Thieves’ Howe unto this day. And the tale of the said thieves who were slain by the Eastdalers in the shot-stour is three score and ten and seven.

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩道：“也罢。这两个女人纵然失了，兴许还能寻回，算不得什么大损失；惨的是两场血战里男儿的死伤。然而依我看来，剧盗们既已领教了西谷百姓的勇武，一时再不敢来犯西谷了。愿主上帝受赞美，这方生民今日犹存，来日亦必长存！”

他说得字斟句酌、声音洪亮，人人听得清楚，众人都以深沉而雄壮的声音为他的话喝彩；但站得离奥斯伯恩最近的人说，他说这话时面容冷峻、苍白之极；在他们看来，那一阵鏖战里，断板剑若曾在西岸出鞘，倒下的剧盗只怕还要更多。

于是两下分手，奥斯伯恩和他的韦瑟梅尔人回了家，其余东岸谷民也各归各处。至于西岸谷民，他们动手把剧盗的尸首从鏖战之地拖开——因为他们惧怕矮人一族心怀嫉恨——把他们埋在当初与东岸人交谈时所立之处的近旁，在上面起了一座大冢，至今还唤作贼人冢。至于东岸谷民在那场箭斗中杀死的剧盗，其数乃是七十七人。

## XXXV / 三十五

===Original===

Osberne Seeks Counsel of Steelhead

Wear the days hereafter into summer, and Osberne is at Wethermel, and doth what work cometh to hand no worser than heretofore; yet folk marvel that his sorrow over the man-fall of the Cloven battle seemeth to wear off him but little, though he is mild and kind in speech to all men. Much he sat talking with Stephen the Eater, who in these days was growing whole of his hurts, and it is thought he learned some hidden lore from him, for many deemed that Stephen was wise therein. Every third day he went all alone to the Bight of the Cloven Knoll, and sat there long through the day; but never had he any tidings of Elfhild, nor forsooth did he look for any such. He learned from over the water that there was no newcomer at Hartshaw Knolls, and that the house and garth lay waste, and so was like to abide.

Now when it lacked but three nights of Midsummer, Osberne, after he had spoken long with Stephen, set some victual in his scrip, and went afoot in the evening-tide up the bent and over it among the mountain-necks, and so into that same little dale where he had first met Steelhead. There he sat him down on the grass by the brookside and ate his meat, and then, when it darkened so much as it would that June night, he laid him down and slept in all trust of safekeeping. He awoke at the end of dawn and washed him in the brook, and then clad him and sat down to abide sunrise. Then even as the sun arose it smote a beam of light from some bright thing overtopping the crown of the hillside before him, and Osberne knew that there was come his friend Steelhead, in such guise as he had first beheld him there: which was in sooth the very thing which he desired.

So Osberne stood up to greet him and Steelhead came to him and put his arms about him and kissed and embraced him, and Osberne wept for pity and hope of his life. Then said Steelhead: “I know why thou art come to me; a while agone I laid my hands upon thee that I might make thy body stark for all adventure, and now thou wouldst have me do the like for the soul of thee. Herein will I do what I may, but first we will eat of the increase of Wethermel, that thou mayest see how much I love thee and the land that bred thee.”

So Osberne bestirred him, and kindled the cooking-fire and made ready the meat, and they ate together in all content and friendliness. But when they were full Steelhead spake: “Now whether wouldst thou tell me all thy tale, or whether wouldst thou be silent thereof, knowing that I know it without words spoken?”

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩向钢首问计

此后时日渐渐磨入夏季，奥斯伯恩守在韦瑟梅尔，手头有什么活计便做什么，做得不比往日差；只是众人暗暗称奇：裂丘一战折损了那许多人，他心头的哀痛却似久久不肯褪去，尽管他对人人言辞都温厚和善。他常陪着饕客斯蒂芬闲坐长谈——斯蒂芬这些日子伤势渐愈——据人揣想，他从斯蒂芬那里学得了些隐秘的学问，因为不少人都认定斯蒂芬于此道颇有智慧。每隔三日，他便独自一人去裂丘湾，在那里一坐便是大半日；却始终得不到埃尔弗希尔德的半点音信，说实在的，他也本不指望会有。他从对岸打听得来：哈特肖丘并无新来的人，那座宅院连同场圃一片荒废，看来还要这般荒下去。

且说离仲夏只差三夜，奥斯伯恩与斯蒂芬长谈了一番之后，往挎囊里装了些吃食，趁傍晚徒步登上草坡，翻过坡顶，穿行于山颈之间，一路走进他初次遇见钢首的那座小谷。他在溪畔草地上坐下用饭，待到那六月之夜黑到它所能黑的程度，便躺下安睡，一心信托自己必得护佑。破晓时分他醒来，在溪中洗浴了，随即穿衣束带，坐待日出。恰在旭日升起之际，日光打在一件明晃晃的物事上，从前方山坡顶高处投下一道光芒，奥斯伯恩便知他的朋友钢首到了，来的正是他当初在此见到的那副形貌：这实在正合他的心愿。

于是奥斯伯恩起身相迎，钢首走到他跟前，伸出双臂将他搂住，又吻他又拥抱他；奥斯伯恩落下泪来，一半出于怜惜，一半出于生平的指望。钢首便道：“我知道你为何来找我。前些时候我把双手按在你身上，为的是教你筋骨强健，足以担当一切艰险；如今你要我也照样医你的心灵。这事我必尽力而为，只是先咱们要吃一顿韦瑟梅尔的出产，好教你看出我多么爱你，也爱这养育你的土地。”

于是奥斯伯恩忙活起来，生起炊火，整治肉食，两人一同进餐，尽欢尽睦。待到吃饱，钢首开口道：“如今你是想把你的事原原本本讲给我听呢，还是自知我不待言语早已了然，就此缄默不提？”

===Original===

Quoth Osberne: “I would tell it.”

“There is yet time,” said Steelhead, smiling kindly on him, “so make no tarrying.”

Then Osberne began straightway, and spared not words overmuch, but herein he used the most when he told of Elfhild, what she was like in those latter days, and how his heart enfolded her, and how sweet was her converse with him; and when he was done Steelhead said: “What is in thy mind concerning dwelling in the Dale amidst thine own folk?” Said Osberne: “My mind it is to live and die here, and do all that is due to the folk of my fathers.” Said Steelhead: “Then thou must be healed of this trouble; that is, thou must forget thy love and thy longing, or at the least thou must think more of other matters than of this. For I will not have it that thou my fosterling shouldst be a killjoy among men of the kindred; wherefore ill-luck will come of it.”

Said Osberne, knitting his brows: “I will not be healed in this way. For do I not know that she also is wrapped in sorrow and tormented by longing. Shall I leave her, therefore, as the dastard leaves a wounded friend before the oncoming foeman?”

Steelhead smiled on him. Quoth he: “Thou wilt not be healed? So be it; then mayst thou not abide in the Dale amongst the kindred, but carry thy trouble to the lands of the aliens, where there is none to remember the joyous face of thee before the trouble was.”

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩道：“我愿意讲。”

“时候还来得及，”钢首朝他慈祥地微笑道，“那便不要再耽搁。”

于是奥斯伯恩当即讲了起来，辞句并不冗蔓，唯独讲到埃尔弗希尔德时用情最深——讲她后来那些日子是何等模样，讲他的心如何把她珍藏在怀，讲她与他对谈何等甜美。他讲完了，钢首便道：“住在谷地、置身亲族之间，你心里作何打算？”奥斯伯恩道：“我的心愿是生于斯、死于斯，把对先祖宗族应尽的本分一一尽到。”钢首道：“那么你这桩苦情必须得医；也就是说，你必须忘掉你的爱与渴念，或者至少，你要多惦念旁的事，少惦念这一桩。因为我断不容我抚育的孩子在族人当中做个败人清兴的人，那样只会招来厄运。”

奥斯伯恩皱眉道：“我不肯这样被医好。难道我不知道她也被愁苦包裹，受着渴念的煎熬么？难道我便因此丢下她，像那懦夫在强敌压境之际丢下受伤的伙伴一样么？”

钢首对他莞尔一笑，说道：“你不肯被医好？也罢；那你便不得留在谷地亲族中间，只得把你的苦楚带到异邦他乡去，那里没有人记得你受苦之前那张欢喜的脸。”

===Original===

“This may I do,” said Osberne, “and even so it shall be since it is thy will. But hast nought else to say to enhearten me in my travel?”

“This I have thereto,” quoth Steelhead, “that though the world be wide there are many ways about it, and meseemeth that there is somewhere a way whereon thy feet and Elfhild’s may draw toward one another.” Said Osberne: “May all good hap go with thee for thy word. Dost thou not see how my face is already gladdened thereby?”

Said Steelhead: “This is hope, my son, that flareth up swiftly and fadeth soon; but now this I shall give to thee, as I deem I may, that never shalt thou lack hope so long as thou hast deeds to do. Call to mind what thou thyself saidst unto Elfhild, that the only way to bridge the Sundering Flood is for one of you, or both, to wander wide in the world. But now tell me, what hast thou in thy mind to do in these days that pass?” Said Osberne: “I have been thinking of it, that when the Midsummer Feast is over I shall say farewell to my folk and to ride to Eastcheaping to find Sir Medard; for meseems he is the man whom I know out in the world who will put me in the way of deeds.” Said Steelhead: “And wilt thou go alone, or hast thou a mind to take any with thee? Suppose it were Stephen the Eater, who is a man of lore, and as I do thee to wit moreover, a friend of our own?” “Dost thou command me to have him with me, lord?” said Osberne. “Nay,” said Steelhead, “I but ask thee of thy mind in the matter.”

Said Osberne: “Then I shall tell thee that my mind is to go all birdalone. I would take no part of Wethermel with me, lest I soften towards the Dale, and turn back some fair day of summer and fall to nursing my sorrow therein. Moreover I know of Stephen that he is both a wise man and a champion, and I deem it were well to leave such an one to uphold the good days of Wethermel; so that whether I do that which I would, and come back in joy and honour; or do it not, and die away from my place, not without honour it may be, I shall yet know of the thriving of my kindred and the pleasures of Wethermel, which shall yet be glorious on the earth, even as it were a very living creature and mine own true friend. Many a time shall I think of it, in good hap and in ill hap, in grief and in joy.”

“Hail to thy word, son and stout-heart!” said Steelhead, “for herein thou thinkest of it as my very heart would that thou shouldst. Now I see that I have indeed sown the seed of hope in thee, and I call it the lack of fear.”

===Chinese===

“这个我做得到，”奥斯伯恩说，“既然是你的意旨，事情便这样定了。但除了这些，你可还有别的话，好教我旅途上提起胆气？”

“这我倒是有一句，”钢首道，“世界虽广，环世界而行的路却有好多条；依我看来，天下总有一条路，教你的脚和埃尔弗希尔德的脚得以渐渐走近。”奥斯伯恩道：“愿你这句话得百般好运相随。你难道看不出，我这张脸已经因此欢喜起来了么？”

钢首道：“我儿，这便是希望了——它燃起得快，消褪得也快；不过如今有一样东西我却可以给你，我自觉给得起：只要你还有事功可为，你便永远不会缺少希望。你记不记得你自己对埃尔弗希尔德说过的话——要在分离洪流上架起桥来，唯一的法子便是你们两人中的一个，或者两个一起，去世界上远远地闯荡一番。但现在你告诉我，眼下这些流逝的日子里，你打算做些什么？”奥斯伯恩道：“我正想着这事：等仲夏节的宴会一过，我便向家人辞行，骑马去东集投梅达德爵士；因为依我看来，我在世上认识的人里头，惟有他能引我上建立事功的路。”钢首道：“那你打算独自去呢，还是有意带个什么人同往？譬如带上饕客斯蒂芬如何？他是个有学问的人，而且——这我也让你知道——还是咱们自家的朋友。”“爵爷，你是吩咐我带他同去么？”奥斯伯恩说。“不是，”钢首道，“我不过问问你在这事上的心意。”

奥斯伯恩道：“那么我告诉你：我的心意是孤身只影上路。我不愿从韦瑟梅尔带走一物一事，只怕自己心肠一软，恋起谷地来，将来某个晴美的夏日打马回头，跌回那片土地上，只顾抚育自己的哀伤。再者，我深知斯蒂芬既有学问，又是一员健将，我以为留下这样一个人撑持韦瑟梅尔的好日子，乃是上策。这样，无论我如愿以偿，欢欢喜喜、载誉而归；还是功业未成，死在远离家园之地——纵然如此，或许也不失体面——我总还能得到音信，知道我宗族的兴旺、韦瑟梅尔的安乐。这庄园日后必在世间焕发光彩，宛如一个活生生的灵物、我真正的挚友。好运厄运、悲伤欢喜之中，我都要常常记起它。”

“向你这句话致敬，我的儿，好一条硬心汉！”钢首说，“因为在这事上，你想得正合我心底所愿你如此。如今我算看明白了，我确实已在你心里播下了希望的种子——我管它叫做无所畏惧。”

===Original===

And now he brought the talk on other matters, and was as kind and friendly as might be, and Osberne deemed it was a great thing for him that he had so won the love of this noble wight and great-heart. So in all pleasure the day sped, and when it was hard on sunset Steelhead spake: “Now must I get me back to my house and home of old time, and thou shalt go home to Wethermel the dear; and now I see of thee that thou shalt hold a cheerful countenance there, and depart when needs must in honour and well-liking of all men.”

So they stood up, but ere Osberne turned his face to the west he said: “And when shall I see thee again, lord?”

“Who knows?” said Steelhead; “maybe when thou lookest least for me: on the lonely marsh maybe, or in the thick of the forest; or in the midst of the fierce battle, or on thy very deathbed; or it may not be at all in thine earthly life.”

“And that house whereto thou art now going, shall I ever see thee there?” said Osberne.

“Surely I deem that thou shalt; and yet most surely not till thine earthly days are over. But now farewell, and my heart goes with thee.” Therewith he turned and was gone, and Osberne went his ways to Wethermel without looking after him. And now it seemed to him as if he had been fain not to have gone back to that well-beloved stead, but to have gone on east at all adventure; and he looked toward the day when he must depart at last as a sad and sore time, when hope would be dimmed by mere sorrow and trouble.

===Chinese===

随后他把话头引到别的事情上去，和蔼亲密，无可挑剔；奥斯伯恩自忖，自己竟能赢得这位高贵人物、这条伟烈心肠如此的眷爱，实是一桩莫大的幸事。这一天便在满襟欢愉中飞驰而过；眼看快到日落，钢首开口道：“如今我得回我古时的家园居所去了，你也该回你亲爱的韦瑟梅尔去。我如今看你，你必能在那里持一张欢颜，待到不得不走时，也必体体面面、为众人所爱重地辞别而去。”

于是两人站起身来；奥斯伯恩还未掉脸向西，先说道：“爵爷，我何时才能再见你呢？”

“谁知道呢？”钢首道，“多半在你最想不到我的时候：也许在寂寂的泽地，也许在密林深处；也许在鏖战的正中间，也许就在你的临终榻前；也或许，在你尘世的这一生里，压根儿就见不着。”

“还有你如今要回去的那座居所，我往后能到那里见到你么？”奥斯伯恩问。

“我深信你必有见到的一天；然而同样确然的是，那必得等你尘世的岁月终了之后。但如今别过了，我的心随你同去。”说罢他转身便去了；奥斯伯恩自回韦瑟梅尔，并不回头望他。这时他心中只觉，仿佛自己本不情愿回到那深爱的地方，倒宁愿径直向东、放胆闯荡去；他望向那终须动身的末日，只觉那将是一段愁惨酸辛的时辰，到那时，希望会被纯然的哀伤与烦难所遮暗。

## XXXVI / 三十六

===Original===

The Staves Which Osberne Taught to the Dalesmen

Now all folk at Wethermel when they looked upon Osberne’s face deemed that he was bettering of the drearihood which had weighed on him ever since the battle with the strong-thieves, and of that bettering they were right glad, for they were wont to have much joy of his fellowship. Came on therewith the Midsummer Feast of the Cloven Mote, which, as aforesaid, was the greatest of all the feasts of the Dalesmen, and Osberne was there with a countenance of good cheer no worser than the best. Now at this feast not only did they do in the heedfullest and solemnest wise all that belonged to Midsummer, as the Trundling of the Fiery Wheel, and the Kindling of the Bale, and the Leaping through the Fire; but also before noon, and ere these plays were begun, was high mass sung in the goodliest fashion in each of the two churches of Allhallows for the good rest of them who had fallen manfully in battle with the thieves. And last of all, when the summer night was as dark as it would be before the dawn, and the folk of the two sides were all ranged each in a line on their own shore of the river, they sang these staves from side to side across the Sundering Flood, the Westdalers beginning, and then the Eastdalers taking it up:

> ’Tis Summer and night,
> Little dusk and long light,
> Little loss and much gain
> When the day must needs wane,
> Little bitter, much sweet
> From the weed to the wheat;
> Little moan, mickle praise
> Of the Midsummer days,
> When the love of the sleeping sun lieth along
> And broodeth the acres abiding the song.

> Were the spring to come o’er
> And again as before,
> What then would ye crave
> From the summer to have?
> Sweeter grass would ye pray,
> And more lea-lading hay?
> For more wheat would ye cry,
> Thicker swathe of the rye?
> Stouter sons would ye ask for, and daughters more dear?
> Well-willers more trusty than them ye have here?

> O the wheat is yet green
> But full fair beseen,
> And the rye groweth tall
> By the turfen wall.
> Thick and sweet was the hay
> On the lealand that lay;
> Dear daughters had we,
> Sons goodly to see,
> And of all the well-willers ere trusted for true
> The least have ye failed us to deal and to do.

> What then is this,
> That the summer’s bliss
> Somewhat ye fail
> In your treasure’s tale?
> What then have ye lost,
> And what call ye the cost
> Of the months of life
> Since winter’s strife?
> For unseldom the summer sun curseth the Dale
> With the tears thrust aback and the unuttered wail.

> Forsooth o’er-well
> The tale may we tell:
> ’Tis the spear and the sword
> And the House of the Sward.
> The bright and the best
> Have gone to their rest,
> And our eyes are blind
> Their eyes to find.
> In mead and house wend we because they were stayed,
> And we stand up because in the earth they were laid.

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩教给谷民的战歌

如今，韦瑟梅尔全庄的人望着奥斯伯恩的脸，都看出他与剧盗之战以来一直压在他心头的那份阴郁正在消解；见他好转，众人皆大欢喜，因为平日与他相伴，他们向来得趣甚多。接着便到了裂丘大会的仲夏节；如前所述，这是谷民一切节庆中最盛大的一节，奥斯伯恩也到场了，脸上的一团欢容，不输给世上任何人。这一日，众人不但把仲夏应有的一应仪节办得最郑重、最肃穆，如转滚火轮、燃点燔堆、跃火而过之类；而且在午前、在这些游艺开场之前，还先在两岸两座诸圣堂里，以最隆重的仪式献唱大弥撒，超度那些与剧盗鏖战、奋勇捐躯者的亡灵，祈其安息。末了，待到夏夜黑到破晓前它所能黑的程度，两岸百姓各在本岸河滨排成一行，隔着分离洪流一递一声唱起下面这几节战歌：西岸谷民起头，东岸谷民接上。

> 正是夏天，又是夜晚，
> 暝色甚短，昼光漫漫，
> 亏损甚微，进益甚广，
> 纵然白日不得不降；
> 苦味甚少，甘味甚浓，
> 从莠草一直到麦禾之中；
> 悲吟甚少，礼赞甚多，
> 礼赞仲夏的时节——
> 当沉睡的太阳把爱意横卧，
> 伏孵着亩亩田畴，静候着歌。

> 倘若春天重回人间，
> 事事又复如前，
> 你们还愿向夏天
> 讨求得何物一件？
> 可要祈求青草更甜，
> 草场干草载得更满？
> 可要呼求小麦更多，
> 黑麦的刈行更密更阔？
> 可要儿子更雄健、女儿更亲爱？
> 可要善意之人比此地这些更可信赖？

> 啊，麦子虽犹青苍，
> 望去却已鲜亮，
> 黑麦长得颀长，
> 傍着草皮垒的墙；
> 干草又肥又香，
> 铺在低伏的草场上；
> 女儿们娇爱，
> 儿子们光彩，
> 而历来信为诚实的善意之人，
> 论行事作为，你们最不曾亏负我们。

> 这是何缘故，
> 仲夏的至福，
> 在你们的珍宝簿里，
> 竟记了亏空几许？
> 你们究竟有何失丧，
> 又唤什么作抵偿，
> 偿这自冬日苦斗一场
> 以来的生命时长？
> 因为夏日的太阳极少咒诅这谷地，
> 教人倒吞泪水、压住说不出的悲啼。

> 当真，这段情由
> 我们能细数从头：
> 是长枪，是利剑，
> 并众剑归宿的宅院。
> 顶光华、顶美好的人，
> 已归去安息其身，
> 而我们的眼已盲，
> 寻不见他们的目光；
> 我们在草场屋舍间行走，因他们已停驻；
> 我们挺身站立，因他们已卧入黄土。

===Original===

> Would ye call them aback
> Then, to look on your lack?

> Nay, we would that their tale
> From our hearts ne’er should fail.

> This then maketh you sad,
> That such dear death they had?

> This night are we sad
> For the joy that we had,
> And their memory’s beginning
> Great grief would be winning.
> But while weareth away,
> And e’en woe waxeth gay.
> In fair words is it told,
> Weighed e’en as fine gold;
> Sweet as wind of the south
> Grows the speech in the mouth.
> And from father to son speeds the tale of the true,
> Of the brave that forbore that the brethren might do.

When this was sung then each man went home to his house. But it is said that these staves were made by Osberne, and that he taught them to the Western men as well as to the Eastern.

===Chinese===

> 难道你们要把他们唤回，
> 回来眼看着你们的缺亏？

> 不，我们但愿他们的事迹，
> 在我们心中永不消逝。

> 那么教你们伤悲，
> 是他们死得那般可贵？

> 今夜我们悲伤，
> 是为了曾有的欢畅，
> 而对他们的追念方才开头，
> 偌大的哀伤便要到手；
> 但时光磨损而去，
> 连悲哀也渐渐生趣；
> 这故事讲得言辞美，
> 秤起来直如金贵；
> 言语长在口中，
> 甜美如南来之风；
> 而忠贞者的故事，自父亲传到儿郎，
> 那班勇士自敛锋芒，好教弟兄们把功业开创。

这歌唱罢，各人便自归家。但据人说，这几节战歌乃奥斯伯恩所作，而且他不但教给了东岸的人，也教给了西岸的人。

## XXXVII / 三十七

===Original===

Osberne Takes Leave of Wethermel

The next day at even, when all the folk were gathered before the porch of the hall at Wethermel, making the most of that fair time, Osberne craved silence a while, and when men were hushed he spake: “Kinsmen and friends, I make it known to you that I must needs depart from you tomorrow morning, though wheresoever I may be I shall ever hold in my heart the hope of coming back to Wethermel; for it will be well enough known to most of you that I love the Dale with great love, and this stead in especial. But now I may not abide here longer, to such a pass are things come with me.

“The story of the wherefore of this were long to tell if I had the heart to tell it, which I have not. But this much may I say, that I go to seek a life which will lead me back to Wethermel, it may be in joy, it may be in sorrow, but in either case with such a heart in me as I may live the rest of my days in the Dale, doing all that is due to the kindred and the folk. Now it will be of no avail for any to strive to put me from this mind, or to hinder me in my purpose, for go I must and will. But this even, as we sit amidst the summer, and our hearts are softened by beholding the peace and abundance of the Dale, and thinking of all days that have been, and our fathers that have lived and died here, I will ask you all and each one of you to say straightway if in any wise I have wronged or hurt you; and if I have, then will I make atonement to my power: so that since I may not bear away with me Wethermel and its folk, I may at least bear away the love of it.”

When they heard these words of his they were mostly exceeding downcast, for in sooth to every one of them his fellowship seemed both a joy and a safeguard; and of the women, some were moved to tears, let alone his grandam and his foster-mother. Albeit he had told his mind beforehand to Stephen the Eater, who had dight him all things ready for departure.

Now there was neither carle nor quean amongst them all who had a word to say against him, or might call to mind aught but kindness at his hands; and one after another they all said so much. But when they were done, and there was silence again, Osberne spake: “Thou, grandsire, art the master of Wethermel, but of late years hast thou suffered me to share in thy mastership; nay, thou hast laid many charges on me which I have taken, and done with them according to my might. Now therefore meseemeth that thou wouldst scarce have it otherwise but that somewhat of my redes and my will and my might should be left after me when I am gone; but if I err in this my thought, I pray thee say as much, and I will leave the matter where it stands, and thou to be sole and only master of Wethermel whiles I am away.”

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩辞别韦瑟梅尔

次日傍晚，众人正聚在韦瑟梅尔大厅前的门廊下，尽情消受那晴美的时光，奥斯伯恩请大家静默片刻；待众人静了下来，他便开言道：“列位亲族、列位朋友，我今告知你们：明日清晨，我势必离你们而去；然而无论身在何方，我心中总要长怀着重回韦瑟梅尔的指望；因为你们大半都深知，我是何等深爱这谷地，又尤其深爱这片田庄。只是如今事势所迫，我再不能在此逗留了。”

“其中缘由，说来话长，纵然我有心细讲，此刻也没有这份心肠。但有一句话我可以说：我去寻求的一条生路，终会引我回到韦瑟梅尔来——或许欢欢喜喜，或许凄凄惶惶——但无论哪样，我胸中怀的都是这样一颗心：往后余生，仍在谷地中过活，对亲族与百姓尽我应尽的一切本分。如今任凭谁来劝我回心转意，或阻挠我这番图谋，都是徒劳，因为我非走不可，也决意要走。只是今晚，我们坐在盛夏之中，眼见谷地的太平丰足，想起已逝的年年岁岁，想起在这里生息死葬的列祖列宗，心肠都软了；我便求你们众人、你们每一位，径直说出来：我可曾在什么事上亏负过你们、伤害过你们？若有，我必力之所及，一一补偿。这样一来，韦瑟梅尔和这里的众人我虽不能随身带走，至少可以把对它的爱随身带走。”

众人听了他这番话，大半都黯然神伤；因为说实在的，在他们每个人眼里，与他相伴既是一桩乐事，也是一份倚仗。女眷中有几个落下泪来，更不必说他的祖母和乳母了。只是他早已把心事先告知了饕客斯蒂芬，斯蒂芬已替他把启程的一应用物打点齐备。

众人当中，无论汉子还是妇人，没有一个对他有半句非难，也没有一个想得起他手上的恩遇之外还有什么；他们便一个接一个，都把这层意思说尽了。待众人说完，重又归于静默，奥斯伯恩方才开言：“祖父，您是韦瑟梅尔之主，只是近年来您容我分担了这份主权；不但如此，您还把许多职分搁在我身上，我都接下来，也量着我的力量办妥了。因此依我看来，您断乎不肯事情另作安排，总要我身后还留下几分我的计议、我的心志、我的气力；倘若我这念头想错了，我求您照直说，我便把此事撂下不再过问，只请您在我离家的日子里，独自做韦瑟梅尔独一无二的庄主。”

===Original===

Spake Nicholas thereat, and said that freely would he grant it that Osberne’s redes and well-doing should still be felt at Wethermel, and that for his own part the governance of an house so great and lordly as Wethermel had now become was overmuch of a burden to him, and that gladly would he take to any man whom Osberne would put in his place; and in good sooth he deemed he wotted who it would be.

Then turned Osberne to Stephen and said: “Thou, Stephen, art more in the heart of my redes than any man else, and thou art both a wise man as I deem, and a proven champion: so if I leave thee here in my skin, wilt thou do the best for me, and be debonnaire with Master Nicholas here and with my grandam, and kind to all the folk?” Said Stephen: “I will do my best thereto, and will pray this of the folk, that they will not hate me because I am not thou.” At that word all they gave him a welcome cheer, whereas their hearts burned within them for love of Osberne and for praise of his words and for sorrow of losing him and hope of his return; so that at that point of time themseemed they might promise anything.

But Osberne said: “Stephen, my friend and fellow, reach out thine hand that I give thee hansel before all of these of what mastership there is in me.” Even so did Stephen, and they clasped hands thereon.

After this Osberne looked about him and said: “Lo friends, how the dusk has been creeping in on us amidst all this talk. So now do ye women dight the board and light the candles within the hall, that we may eat and drink together this last time for a long while.”

Even so it was done, and all folk sat to meat, and thereafter was the drink brought in, and they drank all a cup to Osberne, and he to them; and then was the cup filled for Wethermel, and then again for the Dale; and the last cup was for Osberne’s luck.

Then came a word into his mouth, and he stood up and sang:

===Chinese===

尼古拉斯闻言开言，说奥斯伯恩的计议与善政仍当见于韦瑟梅尔，这话他欣然允诺；又说，据他一己而论，如今韦瑟梅尔已成这般宏大煊赫的门庭，执掌起来于他实是太重的担子，凡奥斯伯恩要置之于他地位的，他无不乐于接纳；而且说实在的，他自以为早知道那人是谁。

奥斯伯恩便转过身来，对斯蒂芬道：“斯蒂芬，在我计议的心底里，数你比别人都占得多；况且依我看，你既是个明智之人，又是身经战阵的好汉：那么我若把你留在此地做我的替身，你可肯为我尽力操持，对这边的尼古拉斯老爷和我的祖母谦和恭敬，对众人和善亲切？”斯蒂芬道：“这事我必尽力而为；只向众人求告一件：莫因为我不是他，便怨恨我。”这话一出，众人都向他报以热诚的喝彩；因为他们心内如焚：既爱奥斯伯恩，又称许他的言语，既痛惜将失了他，又指望他归来；当此一刻，众人只觉无论应承什么都使得。

奥斯伯恩又说：“斯蒂芬，我的朋友、我的同伴，伸出你的手来，让我当着众人，把我胸中当家做主的本事，先交付一件凭信给你。”斯蒂芬果然照办，两人便把手紧紧握在一处。

这过后，奥斯伯恩环顾四周，道：“看哪，朋友们，这番言语之间，暮色已悄悄爬到我们身上来了。那么如今请你们女眷摆设席面，到大厅里点起烛来，好让我们再同吃同饮一回——往后长日子里，这是末一遭了。”

果然照办了。众人入席用饭，随后酒浆捧上，众人举杯敬奥斯伯恩，他也举杯回敬；接着斟满一杯，为韦瑟梅尔而饮；再斟一杯，为谷地而饮；末后一杯，祝奥斯伯恩的鸿运。

这时有一阕歌辞来到他唇边，他便站起来唱道：

===Original===

> From the Wethermel reek
> I set me to seek
> The world-ways unkenned
> And the first of the end.
> For when out there I be
> Each way unto me
> Shall seem nought save it lead
> Back to Wethermel’s need,
> And many a twilight twixt dawning and day
> Shall the feet of the waker dream wending the way.

> When the war-gale speeds
> Point-bitter reeds,
> And the edges flash
> O’er the war-board’s clash,
> Through the battle’s rent
> Shall I see the bent,
> And the gable’s peace
> Midst the Dale’s increase,
> And the victory-whooping shall seem to me oft
> As the Dale shepherd’s cry where the reek wends aloft.

===Chinese===

> 别了韦瑟梅尔的炊烟，
> 我动身去探看
> 未曾经识的世路，
> 和结局的开初；
> 因为当我漂泊在外，
> 每一条路迎面而来，
> 于我皆虚，除非它引路，
> 直回韦瑟梅尔的所需处；
> 而黎明与白昼之间的多少熹微，
> 醒着的人双脚做梦，也把归途一步步走回。

> 当战风疾吹
> 苦尖的芦苇，
> 当锋刃闪光
> 掠过战盾的铿锵，
> 透过战阵的裂破，
> 我将望见草坡，
> 望见山墙下的安宁
> 恰在谷地的丰盈，
> 而得胜的欢呼，我将屡屡听得真切，
> 像谷地牧人的呼喝，正当炊烟升起的时节。

===Original===

> When to right and left
> The ranks are cleft,
> And the edges wan
> Mate master and man,
> It shall be as the fall
> Of a hindering wall
> Twixt my blade and me
> And the garth on the lea;
> So shall day unto day tell the hope of the year,
> And season on season shall draw the Dale near.

This they deemed kindly sung and well; and now so high rose their hearts, that it was to them as if they saw the day of his returning and the gladness of fellowship renewed.

===Chinese===

> 当战阵裂作左右，
> 分向两头，
> 当惨白的锋刃
> 叫主人与仆从同归于尽，
> 那便恰似一堵
> 拦路高墙的倾覆，
> 横在我和我的剑，
> 与草场上家园之间；
> 于是日子对日子，诉说着岁岁的盼念，
> 季节对季节，把谷地拉到近前。

众人都道这歌儿唱得亲切而又工妙；此刻他们的心潮高涨到这般地步，仿佛已亲眼望见他归来的日子，望见了情谊重续的那场欢欣。

## XXXVIII / 三十八

===Original===

Osberne Parts from Stephen the Eater

The next morning Osberne went his way riding on a good horse, and not without treasure in his scrip. He was girt to Boardcleaver and had the wonder-bow and shafts with him; but the byrny, Hardcastle’s Loom, he left behind, and had but a white basnet on his head, for he deemed that his friend Sir Medard would purvey him of armour. All the household were without in the garth to see him off, but none went further with him save Stephen the Eater, who rode with him as far as the bent, and closely and lovingly they spake together on the way. But when they were come to the crown of the bent Osberne drew rein and said: “Now, my friend, shalt thou turn about and let me go my ways.” And they turned about both and looked down on Wethermel, and Stephen cast his arm abroad and spake: “Best-beloved of all men, how long deemest thou it shall be ere thou seest this again?”

“I wot not,” said Osberne; “I am in the hands of Weird, to wend as she will have me: but I will tell thee that I have hoped and prayed that it might be in no longer space than five years; then shall I be of twenty years and three, and she but a few weeks younger, and manhood and womanhood and due service of the kindred shall lie before us both if I find her alive; but if I find her dead, or have sure witness that so she is, that moment shall I turn my face and come back to you, to live and die with you as I may. There is the third thing left, to wit, that I may wander about the world and find her not till I am exceeding old; but even then shall I come back with her, or the memory of her. Now I will not say Remember me, for therein I trust thee, but I will bid thee live hale and merry, that when I see thee again thy face may be as little changed as may be.”

Therewith they parted, and Osberne looked not back again.

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩与饕客斯蒂芬话别

次日清晨，奥斯伯恩骑着一匹好马上了路，挎囊里也并非没有金银财货。他腰间佩着断板剑，随身带着那张奇弓和箭矢；只是那件唤作“哈德卡斯尔的织机”的锁子甲，他留了下来，头上也只戴一顶白色战盔，因为他料想他的朋友梅达德爵士自会替他置办甲胄。全家上下都到场圃上为他送行，但除了饕客斯蒂芬，再没有人往前多送；斯蒂芬陪他骑马一直送到草坡，一路上两人亲亲热热、知心知意地说了不少话。待两人来到坡顶，奥斯伯恩勒住缰绳说道：“如今，我的朋友，你该转身回去了，由我自去走我的路。”于是两人一齐拨转马来，俯望韦瑟梅尔；斯蒂芬把臂膀向四外一展，开口道：“万万人中我最亲爱的人啊，你估量着要过多久，才能再见这地方一眼？”

“我不知道，”奥斯伯恩说，“我在命运女神的手中，她要引我往哪里去，我便往哪里去。但我告诉你：我一直盼着祷着，但愿这期限至多不过五年；到那时我二十有三，她也不过小我几个星期；只要我寻见她活着，男子的盛年、女子的盛年，连同对宗族应尽的本分，便都摆在我们两人面前。倘若我寻见她死了，或得了确凿的见证知道她已死，那一刻我便掉转身来，回到你们身边，尽我所能与你们同生共死。剩下还有第三种情形，那就是我或许要在世上四处飘流，直到老迈已甚才寻见她；但即便到那时，我也要带着她——或是她的音容——回来。如今我不说‘望你记挂我’，因为这层我信得过你；我只嘱你活得康健欢畅，好教我再见你时，你这张脸尽可能少变一些。”

说罢两人便分了手，奥斯伯恩再不曾回头望过一眼。

## XXXIX / 三十九

===Original===

Osberne Gets Him a New Master
On the second day thereafter he rode through the gate of Eastcheaping, and so up the street to the Castle; and many of the townsmen knew him, and cried out good welcome unto him, but he stayed not for any, but came his ways to the Castle, and lighted down in the forecourt and asked for Sir Medard. Here also was he well known, and men were joyful of his coming, and asked him many things of his doings and his welfare; but he answered as shortly as he might and still asked for Sir Medard; and they said that he might see him straightway, for that he was sitting in the solar, and albeit he had a guest with him, they doubted not but that the good knight would be fain of seeing his fellow-in-arms.
So they led him in, and Sir Medard arose at once and greeted Osberne with great joy, and embraced and kissed him. Then he turned to the other man who was in the solar with him, and said: “Lo thou, Sir Godrick, here is a champion whom thou wilt be glad to talk to, meseemeth, when we have drunk a cup.” Therewith he called for wine and spices, for it was the time of the morning bever. Sir Godrick hailed Osberne, who looked on him and saw that he was a tall man, long-armed and very strong-looking, a man swart of visage, long-nosed and long-chinned, with light grey eyes; but though he was somewhat sober of aspect, there was nought evil-looking in his face. He looked downright and hard at Osberne, and said: “If Sir Medard speaketh not by way of jest, thou hast begun early, young man, and I wish thee joy thereof.” Osberne reddened and held his peace, but Medard said: “There be of our foemen no few, who ever if they jested at the lad have done with it now forever.” Osberne reddened yet more if it might be, but the long man took him by the hand and spake kindly to him, and said: “Be not troubled at a light word of mine; at the first thou didst seem so young and fair that it was not easy to think of thee grim amongst the edges; but many a man lies hid within himself.”

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩得一新主
此后第二天，他便驰入东集城门，一路沿街直上城堡；城中许多人都认得他，高声向他道好相迎，他却不停下来见任何人，径自来到城堡，在前院下了马，求见梅达德爵士。这里的人也都熟识他，众人见他到来甚是欢喜，问了他许多行止起居之事；他只尽量简短作答，仍一味求见梅达德爵士；众人说他立时便可见到，因为爵士正在顶楼内室中坐着，虽有一位客人同在，这位好骑士却必定乐意见他这位旧日同袍，对此众人毫不怀疑。
于是众人引他进去，梅达德爵士立时起身，满怀喜意迎接奥斯伯恩，与他拥抱亲吻。随后他转向同在室内的另一人，说道：“你看，戈德里克爵士，这里有一位豪杰，依我想来，待我们饮过一杯，你必乐于与他叙谈。”说罢便命人取酒和香饵来，因为正是晨间小饮的时辰。戈德里克爵士向奥斯伯恩致意；奥斯伯恩打量他，见是个高个子，臂长，看去十分雄壮，面色黝黑，长鼻长颏，一双淡灰色的眼睛；虽则神情略见沉肃，脸上却无半点凶恶之相。他直截而严厉地盯着奥斯伯恩，说道：“倘若梅达德爵士不是说笑，那你可算发迹得早了，年轻人，我祝你由此得乐。”奥斯伯恩涨红了脸，默然不语；梅达德却说：“我们的敌人当中，凡曾拿这小子打趣的，如今永远笑不成了。”奥斯伯恩的脸若是还能更红，此刻便更红了；那高个子汉子却握住他的手，好言说道：“莫为我一句轻慢话见怪；起初你显得这般年少俊美，要人想象你狰狞于兵刃丛中，实在不易；但一个人内里藏着什么，外人本难知晓。”

===Original===

So now they were served of the wine, and Sir Medard spake to Osberne: “Well, Dalesman, thou art come amongst us again, grown in manliness, as was like to be. Now if thou be come but to see us and make us merry with thy fellowship, it is well; but if thou have an errand, and would ask something of us, it is better yet; since forsooth we deem that we owe thee somewhat.” Said Osberne: “Well then, true it is that I have an errand and crave something, and that is soon told; for I would have thee put me in the way of deeds to do, since I have left the Dale and am seeking adventures.”
“That will be the least of things to do for thee, my friend,” said Medard; “and in good time comest thou hither; for though the good town is in all peace and lacks not men, yet here is Sir Godrick of Longshaw, who is here with me partly for the gathering of men. But good must they be who ride with him, and all without fear, whereas I shall tell thee that he is the hardiest knight and most fearless rider of these days. Now do ye two talk it over together.”
Osberne looked Sir Godrick in the face, and ever the more he beheld him the better he liked the looks of his eyes and his visage. So now spake the Knight: “How sayest thou, young man? After all I have heard of thee I may well ask thee to be of mine. Only I must tell thee that the work may be both hard and rough; and though there may be somewhat to be won, yet on the other hand the pay may be little more than leave to do the work.” Said Osberne: “So far as that goes, I am well willing to take my chance of it; but there is one thing which might stand in the way of service with thee.” “What is that?” said Sir Godrick. Said Osberne: “It is in my mind that from henceforth for a while my days should pass in some land that is far hence, that is, from mine own place, and rather to the south than the north.”

===Chinese===

于是酒端了上来，梅达德爵士对奥斯伯恩说道：“好啊，谷民，你又回到我们中间来了，添了丈夫气概，这本是意料中事。你若只是来看看我们，让我们与你相聚同乐，那很好；但你若身负使命，要向我们求些什么，那就更好了；因为说实话，我们自觉还欠你几分情分呢。”奥斯伯恩道：“那么不错，我确有来意，也有所求，这说来也快：我既已离了谷地，正寻历险之途，愿你能指点我一条立功之路。”
“为你办成这桩事，是顶顶容易的，我的朋友，”梅达德说，“而且你来得正是时候；这座好城如今太平无事，也不缺人手，只是这里有朗肖的戈德里克爵士，他到我这里来，一半正是为了招集人马。只是随他骑行的人必须是好汉，而且人人无所畏惧，因为我要告诉你：他是当今最剽悍的骑士、最无所畏惧的骑手。现在你们两个自己商议吧。”
奥斯伯恩端详戈德里克爵士的脸，越看越喜欢他的眼神和相貌。于是骑士开口道：“你怎么说，年轻人？凭我听到的关于你的一切，我尽可邀你入我麾下。只是我必须告诉你，这差事又苦又粗；虽说或有可赢获的东西，但另一面，报酬多半不过是从命去干这差活罢了。”奥斯伯恩道：“就这一层说，我很愿意碰碰运气；只是有一桩事，或许会妨碍我投于你麾下效力。”“什么事？”戈德里克爵士问。奥斯伯恩道：“我心想，从今往后一段时间，我的日子当在一处离这里很远的地方度过——就是说，离我本土很远——而且宁南勿北。”

===Original===

“Where dwelleth thy kindred then?” said Godrick. “In the Dale, which is cleft by the Sundering Flood,” said Osberne, “up under the great mountains; and I am of the East Dale, else scarce had I been here.”
“Well,” said the Knight, “my chiefest house, which hight Longshaw, lieth a long way south of this; but I shall tell thee that it is no great way from the Sundering Flood; but betwixt it and the Flood is a great waste and forest. As to the Flood, it is there, where it runs through this forest which is called the Masterless Wood, a mighty great river, whereon are barges and cutters and seagoing dromonds even, so that it sunders nought, but joins rather. Now besides my house of Longshaw, which is as it were the knop and ouch of my manors, I have other houses and strongholds, some of which be in the very forest itself, and none of them more than a little way thence. For, sooth to say, the said forest is a shield and a refuge to me, and I had been overcome long ago save for its warding. I must tell thee further, that the southernmost skirts of the said forest come down within a score of miles of the great city by the sea which men call the City of the Sundering Flood; and that the city-folk love the forest little, save they might master it and make it their own, wherein they have failed hitherto, praise be to Allhallows! For then were I their very outlaw; whereas now there be others of the knighthood who dwell anigh me who deem that I have the right of it in warding my lands and theirs from these king-ruled chapmen. More by token that the day may come when the folk of their own town, as the gilds of the Lesser Crafts and the husbandmen and simple mariners, may rise against them, deeming them, as the truth is, hard masters and tyrants; wherefore, despite all their mastership, when I will and have occasion thereto, I may ride their streets in safety, for they wot that if they laid a hand on me or mine, it would be Bills and bows! bills and bows! up one street and down another. Wherefore they meddle not with me themselves, but set two or three of the barons who hold of them on the east side of the Wood Masterless to harry me from time to time. Lo thou, lad, now thou knowest not only whereabouts thou mayest go to serve me, but also some deal of the quarrel wherein thou shalt draw sword, if it come to that. How sayest thou?”
“Wait a while, Sir Knight,” said Osberne, “and tell me first: if the King of the city overcome thee, will he take from thee that which is thine own of right, or that which thou hast taken from some other?”

===Chinese===

“那么你的亲族住在哪里？”戈德里克说。“在分离洪流劈开的那处谷地，”奥斯伯恩说，“就在大山脚下；我是东谷人，不然只怕也到不了这里。”
“好，”骑士说，“我的首宅名叫朗肖，在此地以南很远处；但我告诉你，它离分离洪流并不远；只是它与洪流之间，隔着一片大荒原和一座森林。至于那洪流，在它流经这片名叫无主林的森林的地方，已是一条浩浩大川，河面上行驶着驳船、快艇，甚至海来的巨舰，因此它分隔不了什么，反倒把各地联结起来。除了朗肖这座宅子——它好比我各处田庄的冠顶宝扣——之外，我还有别的宅院和堡寨，有几处就在那森林里面，没有一处离林子很远。因为说实话，那片森林于我是盾牌，是庇护所；若非它遮护，我早被人打垮了。我还得告诉你：那森林最南的边沿，一直下到离海滨大城——人称洪流之城——不足二十英里之处；城里人不爱这森林，除非能将它制服、据为己有，而他们至今未能得逞，赞美诸圣堂！因为那样一来，我便成了他们不折不扣的亡命徒；而如今，左近还住着别的一些骑士，他们认为在这件事上是我有理——我守卫我自己的和他们的土地，使其不受这些受国王辖制的商贾侵夺。更有一层：说不定有一天，他们城里的百姓——比如小行会的工匠、庄稼人和寻常水手——会起来反抗他们，因为实情如此，他们确是严酷的主子和暴君；因此，尽管他们权势熏天，只要我愿意、又有事需往，我尽可安然骑行于他们的街市之间，因为他们知道，若有人对我或我的人下手，那便会是钩戟与长弓！钩戟与长弓！从一条街杀到另一条街。所以他们自己不来招惹我，却安插了两三个依附于他们的男爵，在无主林东面，时不时来骚扰我。你看，小子，如今你不但知道了该往何处去为我效力，还多少知道了若真到那地步，你拔剑相向的是一场怎样的争执。你怎么说？”
“且慢，骑士先生，”奥斯伯恩说，“先请告诉我：倘若那城的国王胜了你，他会夺去你那依法自有的东西，还是你从别人那里夺来的东西？”

===Original===

“He will take nothing more than my life,” said Sir Godrick; “but ye may add thereto some small matter of the remnants of houses and land which erst my fathers owned, well-beloved of all folk. Forsooth here and there I hold some tower or strength which I have taken from my foemen, who dared me thereto.”
“Good is that,” said Osberne; “now would I ask of another thing: when thou hast been so pushed to it that thou must needs burn men in their house, has it been the wont of thee and thine to let the old men and women and children come out safe, or to burn them in with the rest?”
The Knight looked grimly on him, and said: “Friend of the Dale, if thou comest to be my man, and thou dost such evil deeds as to burn in them that may make no defence, then if thou escapest hanging at my hands thou mayst call me thy dastard thenceforward.”

===Chinese===

“他至多取我的性命，”戈德里克爵士说，“不过你们还可以再添上少许房产田地的残余，那是先父们昔日拥有、为众人所爱戴的家业。说实话，我在各处还占着几座塔楼或堡垒，那是我从敌人手里夺来的——是他们先向我挑衅的。”
“这倒不错，”奥斯伯恩说，“现在我要问另一件事：当你被逼到不得不烧人于屋中时，你和你的部下素来的惯例，是让老人、妇人和孩子安全出来，还是把他们同其余的人一道烧死在里面？”
骑士面色森然地看着他，说道：“谷地的朋友，你若成了我的人，却做出这等恶行，把无力自卫的人烧死在屋里，那你若能逃得过我亲手绞死你，往后只管叫我懦夫。”

===Original===

Quoth Osberne: “One more thing I would ask yet: if these gilds of craft aforesaid should rise up against their King and the tyrants of the Porte, and they sent to thee for help, wouldst thou give them so much help as not to be against them, but let them fight it out and the mightiest to prevail? Or how much more wouldst thou give?”
Stood up Sir Godrick therewith and was very wroth. Said he: “If these good fellows of the Lesser Crafts rise against their lords and send to me, then if they have gotten to them so much as the littlest of the city gates, or if it be but a dromond on the river, then will I go to them with all mine and leave house and lands behind, that we may battle it out side by side to live or die together. Or if they may not do so much as that, yet if all or any of them may win out a-gates and turn their heads toward Longshaw, then will I ride to meet them with everything that may bear spear or axe, and I will have them home with me and arm them and clothe them and feed them and house them, and my lands shall be their lands, and bite and drop shall we share together, so long as it holds out: and a noble host shall we gather, and harry the King and his dastards till we prevail at last, and we will have a new rule of the City and a new Porte, and I will be the captain thereof if they will have it so: or else to die in the pain. Now I say this is the least that I shall do. And if any man be so bold as to tell me to my face that I will do less, I say that he lies in his throat; and that shall I prove on him, body to body.”
Now Sir Medard fell a-laughing, and he said: “There, there! here is no champion so hardy as to gainsay thee; for I know thee well, old friend, that thou art preux above all men. And as for the Dalesman, look on him and see how his eyes are glittering and his cheeks flushing. Trust me, thou shalt have a man after thine own heart, young though he be.”

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩道：“我还要问一件：倘若上述这些工匠行会起来反抗他们的国王和市府的暴君们，遣人向你求援，你可肯给他们那么多的援助——只是不与他们为敌，任他们打到底、让强者得胜？还是你肯给得更多？”
戈德里克爵士闻言站起身来，勃然大怒。他说：“倘若小行会这些好汉起来反抗他们的主子，遣人来找我，那么只要他们手里拿到了哪怕最小的一座城门，或是河上的一条巨舰，我便带着我全部人马投他们去，宅院田地一概抛下，好教我们并肩血战到底，同生共死。倘若他们连这也不成，但只要他们全体或任何一人能杀出城门，掉头望朗肖而来，那我便率领一切拿得动长枪板斧的人马迎上前去，接他们到我家，给他们甲胄，给他们衣穿，给他们饭吃，给他们屋住；我的土地便是他们的土地，一饮一食，我们同享，只要还维持得住：我们将聚起一支堂堂大军，袭扰那国王和他的懦夫们，直到最终得胜；我们要给那城立个新章法，设个新市府，他们若愿意，我便做它的统领：不然便战死在这场硬仗里。我说这是我起码要做的。若有人胆敢当面对我说，我连这也不做，我说他就是当面扯谎；这一点，我与他一对一肉身相搏，当面证明。”
梅达德爵士这时放声大笑，说道：“好了，好了！这里没有哪个豪杰胆敢反驳你；老朋友，我深知你，你的勇武冠绝当世。至于这位谷民，你瞧他，看他的眼睛怎样发亮，脸颊怎样泛红。相信我，你会得到一个称心合意的人，尽管他还年轻。”

===Original===

Sir Godrick sat down and passed his hand over his brow, and he smiled a little, and said: “Well, man of the East Dale, hast thou perchance yet another question to ask? for meseems for a man who would take wages of me thou hast already asked a few.”
Quoth Osberne: “Lord, be not wroth, but one more question have I to ask. And as to my wages, let that be; for to ask these questions and to have them thus answered, is better than wages to me. But now this is verily my last question. That Masterless Wood which thou hast said is a shield and refuge to thee, is it not also a refuge for rufflers and runagates and strong-thieves? and what dost thou do in dealing with such ill-doers?”
Now Sir Godrick spake quietly and said: “My lad, true it is that there is a sort of folk who haunt the said wood, who live by taking from others. But thou shalt wot that they do but little harm to husbandmen and other poor folk, because such have little to be robbed of. And forsooth many of those from whom they rob are worthy to lose that which they have gotten from poor folk by fraud and covin, and may as duly be called thieves as those that waylay them. Nevertheless we suffer not the said runagates to live and rob wholly in peace; and if we take them, they have the choice of a high gallows or somewhat hard service under my captains. Nay, if it be proven of them that they have been murderous and cruel, they may not forego the dance in the air, even as I said afore. Now then, deemest thou me so evil a lord? Or dost thou deem thee meet for nought save the host of heaven and to be a sergeant of the blessed Michael himself? May he help and save us!”

===Chinese===

戈德里克爵士坐下来，抬手抹了抹额头，微微一笑，说道：“好吧，东谷汉子，你可还有别的问题要问么？因为依我看，对一个要领我薪饷的人，你问得已经不算少了。”
奥斯伯恩道：“主公，请勿动怒，我还有一问。至于我的薪饷，那随它去；因为能这样发问、又得到这样的回答，于我已胜过薪饷。不过现在这真是我的最后一问：你说的那片无主林，既是你的盾牌和庇护所，岂不也是恶客、流窜匪徒和巨盗之流的藏身之地？对这等作恶之徒，你又是怎样处置的？”
这时戈德里克爵士语声平和地说：“我的孩子，不错，确有一路人在那片林子出没，靠劫夺他人为生。但你要知道，他们对庄稼人和别的穷苦人为害甚少，因为这样的人本没多少可抢的。而且说实话，他们所劫的许多人，正是活该失去自己靠欺诈串通从穷人手里搜刮来的财物；称这些人为贼，与称那些拦路行劫的人为贼，同样名正言顺。不过我们也不容这等流窜匪徒全然安然行劫；一旦被我们拿住，他们便由自己挑：或是上高高的绞架，或是在我众队长手下服一段苦役。不，倘若查明他们曾杀人害命、行事残忍，那便休想躲过空中之舞，正像我先前所说。那么如今，你当我竟是这样一位恶主君么？还是你自以为只配给天军当差、做有福的米迦勒本人麾下的军士？愿他扶助拯救我们！”

===Original===

Said Osberne: “That may come to pass, lord, one day, but meanwhile I pray thee receive me as thy man, and thou shalt find that I am not so ill at obeying a commandment as ye may deem.”
And therewith he knelt before the Knight and put his hands between his hands, and swore by Allhallows to be true to him.
Sir Godrick was well pleased, and said to Sir Medard: “Hath he done aught hitherto for which I might dub him a knight?” “Many deeds,” said Sir Medard, “hath he done whereby he might be made a knight; but he will not have it because his kindred are not and were not of the knighthood, albeit men of honour.”
“Well,” said Sir Godrick, “in these matters let each man go his own way, so let it be as it will; yet some name shall I give him that he may be known by it. And lo thou, he is clad all in red, and ruddy of countenance is he, and his sprouting beard shall be red when it hath grown greater, though his hair is yellow and shiny as glass. Wherefore now I shall call him the Red Lad; and by that name meseemeth he shall be known far and wide.”
Then they laughed all three, and the two knights drank, both of them, to the Red Lad, and Osberne thanked them and pledged them in turn. And well content was he with the way that things had gone.

===Chinese===

奥斯伯恩道：“主公，那说不定有一天会成真；但眼下我求你收我为你的人，你将会发现，我服从号令，并不像你们想的那么差。”
说着他便在骑士面前跪下，把自己的双手放进他的双手之间，指着诸圣堂起誓，对他永守忠心。
戈德里克爵士大为欢喜，对梅达德爵士说：“他至今可曾立下什么功劳，够我册他为骑士？”“他立下的功劳多了，”梅达德爵士说，“凭哪一件都够封他骑士；只是他不肯受封，因为他的亲族不是、也从来不是骑士门第，虽则都是有身份的正派人。”
“好吧，”戈德里克爵士说，“这类事各人自便，那就随他去；不过我得给他起个名号，好教人凭它认得他。你看，他通身上下穿红，面色红润，初生的胡子待长得大了也必是红的，虽然他的头发黄得如玻璃一般发亮。所以从今我要唤他红衣少年；我想，凭这个名字，他会远近皆知。”
于是三人都大笑起来，两位骑士都举杯为红衣少年祝饮，奥斯伯恩谢过他们，也依次回敬。事情这般了结，他心下十分满意。

## XL / 四十
===Original===
Osberne Rides with Sir Godrick
Osberne tarried at Eastcheaping for half a month while Sir Godrick was doing his business, which was, in short, gathering good men for his fellowship; at the end of that time he had gotten him one score and five besides Osberne, of whom a half score were well known to Osberne from the war of Deepdale: and he was fain of them.
At last they departed, and Sir Medard took a kind leave of Osberne. And Sir Godrick rode oftenest beside the Red Lad and talked much with him. They had a let-pass through the lands of the Baron of Deepdale, but he would not suffer Sir Godrick to take any men from his country. So they came to Deepham, which was the Baron’s chief town, in a very fair and fertile dale, well watered. And there was nought for it but that the Baron would see the Red Lad, for Sir Godrick must needs speak of him to the lord; and it must be said that there was now no enmity between the Baron and Eastcheaping. So the Baron feasted them well amongst his folk in his great hall; and when he saw Osberne he knew him, and had been told as aforesaid that the Red Lad had been at the carrying him away from the midst of his warriors; but the Baron hailed him merrily, and cried out to Sir Godrick: “Sir Knight, if thou wouldst have any man-stealing done thou art in the luck of it, for this youngling is a past-master in the craft.” And before the feast was over, he sent for Osberne to talk to him, and asked many things concerning the war as Osberne saw it from his side; and he showed that he owed him no grudge for the stealing, for he gave Osberne gifts, a fair gown of crimson cloth of gold, and a ruby ring. So all went well: nevertheless Osberne was nought loth to leave Deepham, and thought it not ill that his life lay not overnigh to the lord thereof.
Now when they had left the lands of Deepdale they turned away toward the south, and rode two days through a fair country and peaceful, of much tillage, besprinkled with goodly thorps, where they had entertainment for their money and none seemed to fear them; and there they saw no men-at-arms, and but few carles that bore any weapons save whittle or boar-spear. At the end of that land they came to a good town walled and warded; and there none hindered them, for the Knight had acquaintance with the captain of the Porte, who had gathered him a half dozen of stout carles, and there they rested three days. Thence they rode one day amidst the same fair country, and they entered a forest through which was a way which led them a little west of south. The said forest gave out in three days, and then they came into a wide valley watered by a fair river running due west. The said valley was more for pasture than tillage, so that it was not thickly housed, albeit when they had crossed the river they came on to a big stead of many houses (and it was evening) much peopled, and the folk, who had seen their riding, were standing with weapons outside the houses.
===Chinese===
奥斯伯恩随戈德里克爵士同行
奥斯伯恩在东集盘桓了半个月，由戈德里克爵士料理他的事务；简而言之，那事务便是为他的队伍罗致好汉子。到那时节，除奥斯伯恩之外，他已招得了二十五人，其中十人是奥斯伯恩自深谷之战起便熟识的：他见了这些人，心中很是欢喜。
末了他们启程，梅达德爵士与奥斯伯恩殷勤话别。一路上，戈德里克爵士多半策马行在红衣少年身侧，同他谈叙甚多。他们持有取道深谷男爵领地的通行文书，但男爵却不许戈德里克爵士从他国中带走一兵一卒。于是他们到了深庄——那是男爵的都邑，坐落在一处十分丰饶肥沃、水泉充足的谷地里。男爵定要见红衣少年一面，这事无可推脱，因为戈德里克爵士对领主说起他，原是免不了的；也得说一句，此时男爵与东集之间已无敌意。于是男爵在大厅中设下盛宴，命自家族众相陪，款待得甚是丰盛；他一见奥斯伯恩便认了出来，并且如前文所述，早已有人告诉过他，红衣少年当日也在把他从武士丛中掳走的人里面；但男爵欢欢喜喜地向他招呼，还朝戈德里克爵士高声叫道：“骑士先生，你若有什么掳人的勾当要做，那可是正交好运，这少年郎便是此道中的老手。”盛宴未散，他便召奥斯伯恩前去叙谈，问了许多战事上的事，都是奥斯伯恩从自己那一边亲见亲历的；他也表明对那场掳掠并无芥蒂，因为他赠了奥斯伯恩礼物：一件绯红织金的华贵长袍，一枚红宝石戒指。于是诸事顺遂：只是奥斯伯恩离开深庄并无半分不愿，并且觉得自己的性命离这位领主不太近，也算不得坏事。
且说他们出了深谷地界，便折向南方，在一片美好太平的乡野中走了两天；那里耕作繁盛，好村庄星罗棋布，他们只要付钱便有款待，也无人显得惧怕他们；在那里他们没见到一个兵卒，携兵器的汉子也寥寥无几，所携无非小刀或猎野猪的长矛。走到那片地界的尽头，他们来到一座墙堞完好、有人戍守的市镇；那里无人阻拦他们，因为骑士与市府的队长素有交往，那队长已聚起六名精壮汉子；他们便在那里歇了三天。此后他们在同样的美好乡野间又走了一天，进入一座森林，林中有一条道路，引他们向南稍偏西而行。那森林三日方尽，随后他们进入一条宽阔的谷地，一道秀美的河流正当面向西流去，滋润其间。这谷地宜牧多于宜耕，故而人烟不稠；只是待他们渡过河去（时已黄昏），便来到一座房舍众多的大庄，人口稠密；庄里的人已望见他们驰来，都手持兵器站在房舍外面。
===Original===
But when Sir Godrick had ridden forward alone and given out his name and errand, to wit, that he was riding to Longshaw with some good fellows who were fain to be of his folk, they all cried out a fair welcome to him and his; for they knew of his deeds and his fame, and were well-willers to him, and were fain of seeing him this first time. Then stood forth an old long-hoary man, but tall and stark, and gave himself out for the master of the stead, which hight Riverlease, and he named him David and said: “Sir Knight, I am father of ten of these men and the grandsire of one score and five, and other good fellows I have with me to the tale of ten score and ten, and all these thou wilt make merry by thy presence here tonight.”
So he brought the Knight and his into the hall, and fair greeting he gave them; and to Osberne, though the land were other and the houses far bigger, for this David was as it were a king of the meadows, it was almost as if he were back at Wethermel, so yeomanly and free seemed all about him. And the folk were a fair folk, the women goodly and the men free and bold. So all men were merry and thought but little of the morrow. But ere the feast was over the old David spake to the good Knight and said: “Sir Godrick, meseems thou shalt have many a foeman on thy back these coming seasons, wherefore if any of my grandsons or the swains here have a longing to ride with thee and become thy men, I will spare them to the number of a half score. How say ye lads,” cried he down the hall, “be there any here who desire to see how the Lord of Longshaw arrayeth his battles, and would bring back some fair stories to the maidens’ ears?”
Now it was soon seen that no few there were that would be fain to ride with the Knight, who soon had his choice of ten tall men, stout, and deft in weapons, and the end of the feast was merrier than the beginning.
===Chinese===
但待戈德里克爵士单骑上前，通名报事——说他正偕几名好汉投朗肖去，这些人皆愿做他的部众——众人便齐声向他和他一行人喊出热忱的欢迎；因为他们素来闻知他的事迹与声名，本就对他怀有好感，这回头一回见他，都觉欢喜。当下站出一位须发皓白的老人，身量却高大硬朗，自称是这庄的庄主，庄名河草场，他又自道名叫大卫，说：“骑士先生，这些人里有十个是我的儿子，二十五个是我的孙儿；此外随我在庄的好汉子，总数凑足二百一十人；今夜你驾临此处，他们全都要因你而畅怀尽欢了。”
于是他引骑士一行进了大厅，向他们殷殷致意；在奥斯伯恩看来，此地虽是别乡，房舍也远为宏大——因为这大卫俨然是草甸之王——却几乎像回到了韦瑟梅尔，他身边处处透着庄户人家的质朴自在。而且这些人品貌端正，女子秀美，男子洒脱勇毅。于是人人尽欢，把明日都抛在了脑后。但宴席未毕，老大卫便向好骑士说道：“戈德里克爵士，依我看来，往后这些时节，你背上少不了要有许多仇敌；因此，我的孙儿或是此地的年轻人中，若有渴望随你出行、做你部下的，我可以放他们十个给你。”说着他隔着大厅朝下喊：“小伙子们，你们怎么说？这里可有想亲眼看看朗肖之主如何布阵列兵、再带几段好故事回来讲给姑娘们听的？”
不多时便见分晓：乐意随骑士出行的人着实不少，骑士很快就拣选了十个高大汉子，个个壮健，武艺娴熟；于是这场宴席的收梢比起开头更加欢畅。
===Original===
Next morning they were away early, and the old man led them out over his meadows, which were exceeding rich of neat and sheep; and at parting he said: “Fair Knight of Longshaw, I have gone as far as I may this day, and must turn again; but this I say to thee, If ever the world goes amiss with thee, as it yet may for all thy valiancy, or forsooth because of it, come hither to me, or if I be dead, to my sons and my grandsons, and abide here as merrily as thou mayst. And spare not to bring whomso of thine thou wilt, as maybe this goodly youngling here,” laying his hand on Osberne’s shoulder, “of whom some of thy men were telling tales to some of mine last night. And now I bid farewell to thee and thine.”
So Sir Godrick and his went their ways, and the new fellows led them by the shortest road, when they knew whither Sir Godrick had will to wend. And when they were out of that valley they came up on to the down-country, which ran along the edge of the plain like a wall; and thereby they went due south for three days, seeing but few folk and no houses, save here and there the cot of a shepherd, and that often builded on a wain. The three days ended, they come on a dale in the downs where a little river cleft them, running about southwest, and by the rede of their shepherd-fellows they turned and followed it out of the down-country, and were presently in a land of mingled tillage and pasture, well builded, but more with single homesteads than thorps, though these were not lacking: albeit the folk of them were not very free with their guesting, but yet for money, and as if half compelled, they yielded up such good as the riders would have of them. The next day, riding the samelike country, they saw on a bent a fair town with white walls, and many goodly gables and slim spires rising above them. But when they drew nigh thereto, an hour before sunset, they found that the said walls were of other uses than to be looked at, to wit to keep them out of their night’s lodging; for the gates were shut, and there were spears and basnets glittering over the battlements. So Sir Godrick rode forward toward the gate, taking Osberne and a trumpet with him, and there bade blow a point of peace and crave speech of the captain of the guard.
Then stood up a tall man on the gate, armed at all points in white armour, and by him were two or three men-at-arms and one with a crossbow ready bent. Cried out the tall man: “Go ye, trumpet and all, and let us see the last of you! For we know you, outlaws of Longshaw. The better luck for you if we come not to your house speedily. Go ye, make ready for us!” Sir Godrick burst out a-laughing and turned his horses head; but even therewith Osberne, who was exceeding keen-sighted, saw the crossbowman raise his engine; but the Red Lad had his dwarf-wrought bow bended in his hand, so that ere the crossbow stock came to the man’s shoulder he fell clattering down with a shaft through his throat, and Osberne rode back speedily after his lord with a half dozen shafts and quarrels whistling about him, but none touched him, and great was the cry and yell that came from the town gate.
Now when Osberne was with his captain again, that one spake to him and said: “Red Lad, Red Lad, a sharp shaft is somewhat of a fierce answer to a rough word. Next time let them shoot ere ye shoot.”
===Chinese===
次日清晨他们早早登程，老人引他们取道穿过自家的草甸，那草甸上牛羊极其繁盛；临别时他说道：“朗肖的好骑士，今日我只能送到这里，须得回去了；但我有话嘱咐你：倘若有一日世道对不住你——便有你这般英勇，这也许仍难免，甚或恰恰因为英勇——便上我这里来；我若死了，便去找我的儿子和孙儿，在这里尽量快快活活地住下。你要带什么人来，也尽管都带来，比如身旁这位俊美的少年郎，”说着他把手搭在奥斯伯恩肩上，“昨夜你的人还对我的人讲了他不少故事呢。如今我向你和你的一行道别了。”
于是戈德里克爵士一行上了路；新伙伴们得知爵士有意去往何处，便引他们抄最近的路。出了那条谷地，他们登上丘原；丘原沿着平原的边缘延展，宛如一道墙。他们傍着它向正南走了三天，一路少有人烟，也不见房舍，只偶尔有一座牧羊人的小屋，而且往往建在大车上。三天走完，他们遇到丘原间被一条小河剖开的一道涧谷，河水约莫向西南流去；依照牧羊人同伴的指点，他们折转方向，沿河出了丘原，不久便到一片耕地牧场相杂的地方，屋舍齐整，只是独门独户的田庄多于村落，尽管村庄也并非没有：只是那里的庄户待客并不怎么慷慨，然而看在钱财份上，又像是半受强逼似的，骑士一行想要的货食，他们也就拿了出来。次日，行经同样的乡野，他们在一道草坡上望见一座秀美的市镇，白色城墙，墙上耸起许多好看的山墙和纤细的尖塔。可待他们日落前一个钟头赶到近前，才发现那城墙的用处并不止于供人观瞻，正是要把他们挡在夜宿之外：城门紧闭，雉堞上闪动着长矛与战盔的寒光。于是戈德里克爵士策马向城门行去，只带了奥斯伯恩和一名号手，命吹一通和平的号音，求那卫队长答话。
城门上当即站起一个高大的汉子，通身白甲，披挂齐全，身边有两三名兵卒，还有一个弩手，弩已张满。那高大汉子喝道：“滚你们的，连号手一齐滚，让我们再别看见你们！我们认得你们，朗肖的亡命之徒。我们若不早日登你们的家门，算你们走运。滚吧，给我们准备着！”戈德里克爵士放声大笑，拨转了马头；可就在此时，目力极锐的奥斯伯恩看见那弩手举起了弩机；红衣少年手中那张矮人所制的奇弓早已弯在手里，因此不等弩托挨上那人肩头，那人便咽喉中箭，哐啷啷栽下城去；奥斯伯恩疾忙随着主人驰回，六支长箭与弩矢在他身边嗖嗖掠过，却一支也没沾着他，城门上顿时爆出一片呐喊咆哮。
待奥斯伯恩回到队长身边，队长开口对他说道：“红衣少年啊红衣少年，一句粗话换一支快箭，这回答未免太狠了些。下回让他们先放箭，你们再放箭。”
===Original===
“Nay, lord,” said Osberne, “had I waited this time thou might’st have come by a knock from yonder carle’s quarrel.” And he told him what he had seen. Then said Sir Godrick: “Then I am wrong and thou right, and I thank thee for the shaft. I might have known that thou wouldst be wise.”
So they fetched a compass about that surly town, and rode a two hours ere they took harbour in a little wood, and held good watch and ward all that night. But none meddled with them.
The day after, by the rede of the shepherd-folk, they turned up into the hills again, for they had no wish to raise the country against them; and to say sooth, Sir Godrick was somewhat pensive that he found enmity so far off his own land. So they rode the hills for five days, falling in with few folk, and going slowly because of the rough ways. Thereafter they needed victual, and had been fain of better lodging might they get it; and whereas they saw a fair plain well builded and tilled, with good roads through the same, and knew that this was the nighest way to the Wood Masterless, they turned down thither at all adventure, and found no evil haps there, but that the folk were well enough pleased to make their market of the riders, and had neither fear of them nor harboured enmity against them. Thus then they rode for two days, and at the end of the second day entered a good cheaping-town, unfenced save by timber pales. There they abode a whole day, yet warily, since, though there were not waged men-at-arms in the stead, there went about many stout carles, who all bore long whittles, and looked as if their bills and bows had not been far to seek. But no strife betid.
===Chinese===
“不然，主公，”奥斯伯恩说，“这一回我若等了，你怕就要挨上那汉子的一记弩箭了。”他便把所见告诉了他。于是戈德里克爵士说：“那么是我错了，你对了；这一箭我多谢你。我本该知道你自有见识。”
于是他们绕着那座凶横的市镇兜了个大圈，又走了约莫两个钟头，才在一片小树林里栖身过夜，彻夜严加守望。却没有人来搅扰他们。
第三日，依照牧羊人的指点，他们又折上山去，因为他们不愿惹得乡民群起与他们为敌；说句实话，戈德里克爵士发现在离自己领地这般遥远的地方也有敌意，心里不免有些怏怏。于是他们在山中走了五天，一路少遇人烟，又因道路崎岖，行进缓慢。此后他们缺了粮秣，若能寻着好些的宿处，也是求之不得；他们望见一片丰美的平原，屋舍耕作齐整，中间有好路可通，又知道这便是去无主林最近的路，便不管三七二十一折了下去；在那里并未遭逢祸事，只是乡民倒颇乐意同骑士一行做买卖，既不怕他们，对他们也并无宿怨。他们就这样走了两天，第二天尽头进了一座好好的市集镇，除一圈木栅外别无城防。他们在那里盘桓了一整天，却处处提着小心，因为镇上虽无受雇的兵卒，却有许多精壮汉子往来行走，个个带着长长的小刀，瞧那样子，长戟和弓都近在手边。但终究没有起衅。

## XLI / 四十一

===Original===

They Joust with the Knight of the Fish

Thence they rode through the fields and the thorps two days, and on the third day in the morning they saw a fair white castle on a hill, and on the plain underneath a little plump of men-at-arms under a banner. So the Knight arrayed his folk and went forward warily, although that folk seemed to be not above a score; for he knew not what might be behind them; and they were hard on the baily of the said castle. But when they were come within half a bowshot, and Osberne could see the banner that it bore two silver Fish addorsed on a blue ground, a herald pricked forth from the castle-folk, and when he drew nigh to Sir Godrick and his he said: “If I knew which were the captain of the riders I would give him the greeting of my lord, Sir Raynold Fisher of the Castle of the Fish.” “Here then is the captain,” said Sir Godrick; “what would Sir Raynold with him?”

“This,” said the herald, “that whensoever my lord seeth the riding of any weaponed men over a half score by tale, they must tarry and joust with him, two of theirs against two of his, and must run with sharp spears of war till one side is overthrown or sorely hurt. This is the custom of the Castle of the Fish, and hath been these hundred years. Wherefore now declare thy name, Sir Knight.”

“This is an evil custom,” said Sir Godrick, “and sorts but little with mine errand, for I have overmuch bitter earnest on hand to play at battle. But since thy lord besetteth the way I must needs defend myself against him, as I would against any other ruffler or strong-thief. Go tell him that the Knight of the Weary-Strife will come presently with a good man of his and deliver him of his jousts.” And Sir Godrick was very wroth.

===Chinese===

他们与鱼骑士比枪

于是他们从那里策马前行，在田野村郭之间走了两日；第三日清晨，望见山上一座秀美的白色城堡，山下平原上有一小队带甲兵士，打着旗号。骑士便整列部众，谨慎前行，虽说那队人看去不过二十之数；因为他不知他们背后还有什么人马；何况他们已逼近那座城堡的外郭。待行到半箭之地内，奥斯伯恩看清了旗号——蓝底之上，两条银鱼相背而列——便有一名传令官从堡中人马里策骑而出，到得戈德里克爵士一行近前，说道：“我若知道哪一位是这队骑士的队长，便要将我主上、鱼堡的雷诺德·费舍尔爵士的问候，转致于他。”“这便是队长，”戈德里克爵士道；“雷诺德爵士找他何事？”

“是这样的，”传令官道，“凡我主上望见带兵刃之人结队逾十名的，便须停下与他比枪：他们出两人，他方出两人，各持锋利战枪对驰，直到一方落马或重伤方休。这是鱼堡的规矩，行之已有一百年。所以如今，骑士先生，请通名报姓。”

“这规矩可恶，”戈德里克爵士道，“与我的差事全不相合；我手头正有太多苦楚的正事，没工夫戏耍打仗。只是你家长上既然拦路相逼，我便不得不自卫御他，正如对付别的什么恶客或剧盗一般。去告诉他：倦斗骑士即刻便带一名好手前来，把他这些比枪的纠缠一并了结。”戈德里克爵士盛怒之极。

===Original===

So when the herald was gone Sir Godrick turned to Osberne and said: “How sayest thou, Red Lad, is this any of thy business?” “All of my business, lord,” said Osberne, “albeit I am none so wroth as thou art.” Said the Knight, looking on him kindly: “Thou art not bound to run, Red Lad; the sharp spear is an unhappy beast, and these men are doubtless of the deftest.” Said Osberne: “It all comes in the day’s work, lord; I pray thee turn me not back.”

“Well, do we on our basnets and make we speedy end of it,” quoth Sir Godrick; “a wise man must ever wait upon a fool’s pleasure.”

So the two of them went forth, and found the others ready over against them, the Knight of the Fish against Sir Godrick, and a very tall, stark man-at-arms against Osberne. Short is the story of this course; for Sir Godrick and the Fish brake their spears, but in such wise that the Castle-knight lost his stirrups, and it went but a little but that he fell to field. As for Osberne, he played so warily that he set his spear-point in the default of the long man’s defence just where arm joins shoulder, and the spear went through and through him, and he fell to the earth most grievously hurt. Therewith Osberne, who must needs let his spear fall, took a short ax from his saddlebow (for he would not draw Boardcleaver) and abode what was to do.
